Classification of del Pezzo surfaces of rank one III. Height 3 Thanks: This project was funded by the National Science Centre, Poland, grant number 2021/41/B/ST1/02062. For the purpose of Open Access, the authors have applied a CC-BY public copyright license to any Author Accepted Manuscript version arising from this submission.
Karol Palka, Tomasz Pełka

Abstract
. This article is a part of a series aimed at classifying normal del Pezzo surfaces of Picard rank one over an algebraically closed field of arbitrary characteristic, up to an isomorphism. The key invariant guiding our classification is the height , defined as the minimal number h {h} such that the minimal resolution of singularities admits a ℙ 1 \mathbb{P}^{1} -fibration whose fiber meets the exceptional divisor h {h} times. It is expected that every singular del Pezzo surface of rank one is of height h ⩽ 4 {h}\leqslant 4 , with minor exceptions in characteristics 2 2 and 3 3 . Having settled the case h ⩽ 2 {h}\leqslant 2 in [ PP24a ] , we now give a classification in case the height equals 3 3 .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
14J10, 14D06; 14J45, 14R05

1.
Introduction

A normal surface
\overline{X}
is
del Pezzo
if its anti-canonical divisor
-K_{\overline{X}}
is ample. This article continues a series started in
[
PP24a
]
, whose goal is to give a self-contained classification of del Pezzo surfaces of Picard rank one, over an algebraically closed field
{k}
of arbitrary characteristic. Such surfaces, especially the log terminal ones, are important building blocks in the birational classification of varieties. They have a rich, interesting geometry on their own: for instance, if
{k}=\mathbb{C}
then their smooth loci are covered by images of
\mathbb{A}^{1}

[
KM99
]
and have finite fundamental groups
[
GZ94
]
. Despite many partial results, log terminal del Pezzo surfaces of rank one are still not classified, for instance, no list of their singularity types is available yet.

Our classification is guided by a new geometric invariant of
\overline{X}
, its
height
. It is introduced in
[
PP24b
, Definition 1.1]
as follows. For a smooth projective surface
X
and a reduced simple normal crossings divisor
D
on
X
we define the
height
of
(X,D)
as

(1)

0pt(X,D)\coloneqq\inf\{{h}:\mbox{ there is a }\mathbb{P}^{1}\mbox{-fibration of }X\mbox{ with fiber }F\mbox{ satisfying }F\cdot D={h}\}.

For a normal surface
\overline{X}
we put
0pt(\overline{X})\coloneqq 0pt(X,D)
, where
X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
is the minimal resolution of singularities and
D
is its reduced exceptional divisor. Its key expected property is that it is bounded. More precisely, it is expected
that any singular del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}
has height at most
4
, with some exceptions if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2,3
. In
[
PP24a
]
we classified del Pezzo surfaces of height at most
2
. We also introduced and described a class of del Pezzo surfaces of rank one which have
descendants with elliptic boundary
, that is, such that
X
admits a morphism onto a normal surface
\overline{Y}
mapping
D
onto an irreducible curve of arithmetic genus
1
contained in the smooth locus of
\overline{Y}
, see Definition
2.10
.

In this article we settle the case when
0pt(\overline{X})=3
and
\overline{X}
has no descendant with elliptic boundary. By
[
PP24a
, Proposition B]
this condition implies that
\overline{X}
is log terminal, with some exceptions if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
.
Our classification is summarized in Theorem
A
.
Within the proof we establish the following results:

•

In Proposition
B
we construct a birational morphism
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow Z
, where
Z
, a minimal model of
X
, is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
or
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and
\psi_{*}D
is a simple configuration of curves of low degree (drawn in the second-to-last column of Table
1
).

•

In Proposition
C
we factor
\psi
as
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{n-1}}
, where each
\varphi_{j}
is a blowup, each
X_{j}
is a minimal resolution of a del Pezzo surface of rank one, and
X_{n}
, a vertically primitive model of
X
with a fixed
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height
3
with respect to
D
, is particularly simple (shown in the last column of Table
1
).

We note that in case
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3
, Lacini
[
Lac24
]
obtained an independent rough classification of all log terminal del Pezzo surfaces. Extending the method
[
KM99
]
he arranged such surfaces in 24 non-disjoint series. Nonetheless, the question of uniqueness of surfaces of a given singularity type has not been addressed, and an explicit list of such types has not been given. We compare this result with our Theorem
A
in Section
7
.

Numerous particular classes of del Pezzo surfaces are well described in the literature, for instance, those of index
\leqslant 3
are classified in
[
AN06
,
FY17
]
. Other partial classification results such as
[
Zha89
,
Koj14
,
Bel24
]
are surveyed in
[
PP24a
, §7]
.

1A.
Main result

We now state the main result of this article. Since non–log terminal del Pezzo surfaces of rank one are described in
[
PP24a
, Proposition B]
, see Remark below, we restrict our attention to the log terminal ones.

A
singularity type
of such a surface is the weighted graph of the exceptional divisor of its minimal resolution. We denote it using conventions summarized in Section
2G
; in particular,
[a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]
stands for a rational chain whose subsequent components have self-intersection numbers
-a_{1},\dots,-a_{n}
. We also use standard notation
\mathrm{A}_{k},\mathrm{D}_{k},\mathrm{E}_{k}
for the canonical singularity types. Given a singularity type
\mathcal{S}
, we denote by
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 3}(\mathcal{S})
the set of isomorphism classes of normal del Pezzo surfaces of rank one, height at most
3
and singularity type
\mathcal{S}
.

Theorem A

(Del Pezzo surfaces of height
3
)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a log terminal del Pezzo surface of rank
1
and height
3
, with no descendant with elliptic boundary. Let
\mathcal{S}
be the singularity type of
\overline{X}
. Then the following hold.

(a)

The type
\mathcal{S}
is listed in Lemmas
4.12
,
5.15
,
5.16
,
6.6
or
6.7
, see Tables
4
–
7
.

(b)

We either have
\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 3}(\mathcal{S})=1
,
or one of the following exceptional cases holds.

(i)

\mathcal{S}=[2,2,3,(2)_{5}]+[3,2]
. Then
\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 3}(\mathcal{S})=2
and
\overline{X}
is as in
Example
4.9
,

(ii)

\operatorname{char}{k}=2
,
\mathcal{S}
is
listed in Table
6
, and
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 3}(\mathcal{S})
has moduli dimension
1
.

For the definition of
moduli dimension
see
[
PP24a
, Definition 1.10]
, we recall it in Section
2E
.

Remark 1.1

(cf.
[
PP24a
, Remark 1.14]
)
.

In a forthcoming article we will show that for each singularity type
\mathcal{S}
as in Theorem
A
, the set
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 3}(\mathcal{S})
consists of isomorphism classes of
all
del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and singularity type
\mathcal{S}
, i.e.
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 3}(\mathcal{S})=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
.

1B.
Choosing witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations and their primitive models

In the lemmas cited in Theorem
A
we describe the singularity types
\mathcal{S}
together with the structure of a specific
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
of the minimal log resolution
(X,D)
of
\overline{X}
which realizes the infimum in formula (
1
), i.e. whose fiber
F
satisfies
F\cdot D=3
. We call such
p
a
witnessing

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration.

Blowing down
(-1)
-curves in degenerate fibers of
p
and in their images we obtain a morphism onto some Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{m}
; in case
m=1
we contract the negative section, too. A description of this
minimalization
morphism
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow Z
provides a recipe to reconstruct
(X,D)
, hence the original surface
\overline{X}\in\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 3}(\mathcal{S})
.

As a first step towards proving Theorem
A
, we show how to choose a witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
and the minimalization
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow Z
in such a way that
Z
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
or
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
\psi_{*}D
is one of the simple configurations shown in Table
1
below. This is the content of Proposition
B
below.

It is convenient to choose
p
in such a way that
D
has a maximal number of horizontal components among all witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations: we call this number the
width
of
\overline{X}
and denote by
0pt(\overline{X})
, see
[
PP24a
, Definition 2.2]
. Clearly,
1\leqslant 0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 0pt(\overline{X})
. This way, we avoid as much as possible the situation when
D
contains a multi-section. If
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
, i.e. if
D
necessarily contains a multi-section
H
, the classification splits into cases depending on whether the finite morphism
p|_{H}\colon H\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
is separable or not, the latter case leading to many additional examples in characteristics
2
and
3
, cf. Table
1
.

Proposition B

(Choosing minimal models of
X
, see Table
1
)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a log terminal del Pezzo surface of rank one and height
3
, and let
(X,D)
be its minimal log resolution. Then there is a birational morphism
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow Z
such that one of the following holds.

(a)

0pt(\overline{X})=3
,
Z=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and
\psi_{*}D
is a sum of three vertical and three horizontal lines, see Figure
1
.

(b)

0pt(\overline{X})=2
,
Z=\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and
\psi_{*}D
is a sum of a conic
{c}
and lines
{l}_{1},\dots,{l}_{\nu}
meeting at a common point off
{c}
, such that one of the following holds:

(i)

\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
,
\nu=3
, the lines
{l}_{1},{l}_{2}
are tangent to
{c}
and
{l}_{3}
is not, see Figure
2
,

(ii)

\operatorname{char}{k}=2
,
\nu\in\{3,4\}
and all lines
{l}_{1},\dots,{l}_{\nu}
are tangent to
{c}
, see Figure
3
.

(c)

0pt(\overline{X})=1
,
Z=\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and
\psi_{*}D={q}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{3}
, where
{q}
is a cuspidal cubic and
{l}_{1},{l}_{2},{l}_{3}
are lines meeting at a common point away from
{q}
, such that
{l}_{1}
is tangent to the cusp of
{q}
, and
{l}_{2},{l}_{3}
are tangent to
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
with multiplicity
2
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
and with multiplicity
3
if
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
, see Figure
4
.

To state the next result, we recall the definition of an
elementary vertical swap
from
[
PP24a
, Definition 1.3]
.
Let
X
be a smooth projective surface and let
D
be a reduced snc divisor on
X
. Fix a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p
of
X
. Assume that there is a vertical
(-1)
-curve
L\not\subseteq D
such that
L\cdot D\leqslant 2
and
L
meets exactly one
(-2)
-curve
C\subseteq D
. Assume that
C
is vertical, too (this is a typical situation, cf. Lemma
2.5
). Let
\varphi\colon X\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
be the contraction of
L
. Then
\varphi(C)
is a new vertical
(-1)
-curve; let
D^{\prime}=\varphi_{*}(D-C)
. The operation
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is called an
elementary vertical swap
. A
vertical swap
is a composition of elementary vertical swaps with respect to the induced
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations. We say that
(X,D,p)
is
vertically primitive
if it admits no vertical swaps.

We say that
(X,D)

swaps vertically
to
(Y,D_{Y})
if there is a vertical swap
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(Y,D_{Y})
. If
(Y,D_{Y})
with the induced
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration is vertically primitive, we call it a
vertically primitive model
of
(X,D,p)
, and we often omit
p
in the notation if it is clear from the context. If both
(X,D)
and
(Y,D_{Y})
are minimal log resolutions of normal surfaces
\overline{X}
and
\overline{Y}
, we abuse the terminology by saying that
\overline{X}
vertically swaps to
\overline{Y}
, and call
\overline{Y}
vertically primitive if
(Y,D_{Y})
with the induced
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration is vertically primitive.

If
\overline{X}
is a log terminal del Pezzo surface of rank 1, and
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X_{1},D_{1})\rightsquigarrow\dots\rightsquigarrow(X_{n},D_{n})=(Y,D_{Y})
is a sequence of elementary vertical swaps then by Lemma
2.7
(b)
each
(X_{i},D_{i})
is the minimal log resolution of a log terminal del Pezzo surface of rank 1, too, so we get a
“cascade”
of del Pezzo surfaces
\overline{X}\rightsquigarrow\overline{X}_{1}\rightsquigarrow\dots\rightsquigarrow\overline{Y}
.

If
\overline{X}
is a log terminal del Pezzo surface of rank one and height at most
2
then
[
PP24a
, Theorem A(1)]
shows that
\overline{X}
vertically swaps to a canonical surface, with some exceptions in case
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. An analogous statement in case
0pt(\overline{X})=3
is our next intermediate result, Proposition
C
. It implies that
\overline{X}
vertically swaps to a surface
\overline{Y}
which is canonical or has a descendant with elliptic boundary, with some exceptions if
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
.

Proposition C

(Choosing vertically primitive models)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a log terminal del Pezzo surface of rank
1
and height
3
. Then for some witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of its minimal log resolution, the surface
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to one of the following vertically primitive surfaces
\overline{Y}
, see Table
1
. We write
\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})
for the singularity type of
\overline{Y}
.

(a)

The surface
\overline{Y}
is canonical, and one of the following holds:

(i)

0pt(\overline{X})=3
,
\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})
equals
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
or
2\mathrm{A}_{4}
and
\overline{Y}
is as in Example
3.1
,

(ii)

\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
,
0pt(\overline{X})=2
,
\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})
equals
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
or
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
, and
\overline{Y}
is as in Example
3.2
(b)
,
(c)
.

(b)

The surface
\overline{Y}
has a descendant of singularity type
\mathcal{D}
with elliptic boundary, and one of the following holds:

(i)

\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
,
0pt(\overline{X})=2
,
\mathcal{D}=\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{7}
,
\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})=\mathcal{D}+[3]
and
\overline{Y}
is as in Example
3.2
(a)
,

(ii)

\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3
,
0pt(\overline{X})=1
,
\mathcal{D}=\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
,
\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})=\mathcal{D}+[3]
and
\overline{Y}
is as in Example
3.4
(a)
,

(iii)

\operatorname{char}{k}=3
,
0pt(\overline{X})=1
,
\mathcal{D}=3\mathrm{A}_{2}
,
\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})=\mathcal{D}+\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\cdot[3]
and
\overline{Y}
is as in Example
3.4
(b)
,

(iv)

\operatorname{char}{k}=2
,
0pt(\overline{X})=2
, and either
\mathcal{D}=3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
and
\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})
equals
\mathcal{D}+[3,2]
or
\mathcal{D}+\mathrm{A}_{1}+[3]+[4]
; or
\mathcal{D}=2\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{3}
,
\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})=\mathcal{D}+2\mathrm{A}_{1}+[3]
. In either case,
\overline{Y}
is as in Example
3.3
.

(c)

\operatorname{char}{k}=3
,
0pt(\overline{X})=1
,
\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})=4\cdot[3]+3\mathrm{A}_{2}
and
\overline{Y}
is as in Example
3.4
(c)
.

Once Proposition
C
is proved, to get Theorem
A
we reconstruct the cascade over each
\overline{Y}
. Note that by Proposition
3.6
(e)
each surface
\overline{Y}
above is uniquely determined, up to an isomorphism, by its singularity type
\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})
, except for those constructed in Examples
3.3
(a)
and
3.3
(b)
, which form families of moduli dimension
1
. To reconstruct the cascade from
\overline{Y}
we blow up points on vertical
(-1)
-curves as long as the resulting anti-canonical divisor is ample. The latter condition turns out to be so restrictive that (in most cases) we need to stop before we can perform an outer blowup, hence each blown up point is uniquely determined by the singularity type of the surface we are reconstructing, see
[
PP24a
, Observation 2.10]
. This is the reason behind the uniqueness part of Theorem
A
. The same principle allows us to infer the following Proposition
D
, which describes the cohomology of the logarithmic tangent sheaf
\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D)
. For basic properties of
\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D)
, in particular for its relation with infinitesimal deformations of
(X,D)
, we refer to
[
FZ94
,
Kaw78
]
.

Proposition D

(Rigidity and unobstructedness, cf.
[
PP24a
, Proposition D]
)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a log terminal del Pezzo surface of rank one and height
3
which does not have a descendant with elliptic boundary. Let
(X,D)
be its minimal log resolution and let
h^{i}\coloneqq h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
. Then the following hold.

(a)

We have
h^{0}=0
.

(b)

We have
h^{1}=0
, unless
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
and
\overline{X}
is as in Theorem
A
(b)(b.ii)
, in which case
h^{1}=1
.

(c)

We have
h^{2}=0
,
unless
\operatorname{char}{k}\in\{2,3\}
and
\overline{X}
vertically swaps to a vertically primitive surface
\overline{Y}
from Examples
3.3
(a)
,
3.3
(c)
or Examples
3.3
(b)
,
3.4
(c)
, in which case
h^{2}
equals
1
or
2
, respectively.

We remark that in case
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
the ampleness of
-K_{\overline{X}}
follows simply from the log terminality, see
[
PP24a
, Lemma 2.6]
or Lemma
2.6
, so the structure of del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and height
\leqslant 2
is much less rigid. Indeed, even if
\operatorname{char}{k}=0
there exist positive-dimensional families of such surfaces which are pairwise non-isomorphic but have the same singularity type, see
[
PP24a
, Corollary 1.6(a)]
.

Remark 1.2

(Non–log terminal del Pezzo surfaces of rank
1
and height
3
)
.

Assume that
\overline{X}
is a non–log terminal del Pezzo surface of rank one and height
3
. Then by
[
PP24a
, Proposition B]
, we have
\operatorname{char}{k}\in\{2,3,5\}
. Assume furthermore that
\overline{X}
has no descendant with elliptic boundary. Then
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
and
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to the surface from Example
3.3
(c)
, hence satisfies the conclusion of Propositions
B
(b)(b.ii)
and
C
(b)(b.iv)
. Moreover, if
\overline{X}
is log canonical then its isomorphism class is uniquely determined by its singularity type,
listed in Table
8
.

1C.
Outline of the proof

In Section
3
we construct vertically primitive models of
\overline{X}
from Proposition
C
.
In Sections
4
,
5
and
6
we prove Theorem
A
in cases
0pt(\overline{X})=3,2,1
respectively. In each case the proof is organized as follows.

Step 1.

We choose a witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
in such a way that contracting vertical
(-1)
-curves on
X
and its images we get a minimalization morphism
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
or
X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
as in Proposition
B
.

Step 2.

We adjust the chosen witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p
so that the minimalization
\psi
factors through a minimal resolution of a vertically primitive surface
\overline{Y}
as in Proposition
C
.

Step 3.

We reconstruct
(X,D)
by reversing vertical swaps.
Here we need to control all possible
“cascades”
over
\overline{Y}
, ensuring that
(X,D)
is the minimal log resolution of a log terminal surface
\overline{X}

with ample
-K_{\overline{X}}
.

The proofs of Propositions
B
,
C
are summarized
at the end of Section
6A
; those of Theorem
A
and of Proposition
D
are summarized
at the end of Section
6D
.

Eventually, in Section
7
we compare our classification with the one given by Lacini
[
Lac24
]
in case
\operatorname{char}{k}\not\in\{2,3\}
, see Tables
3
and
4
.

3\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\cdot[3]
Ex.
3.4
(b)

4\cdot[3]+3\mathrm{A}_{2}

.
The structure of the chosen
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations.

2.
Preliminaries

We work over an algebraically closed field
{k}
of arbitrary characteristic. In this section, we briefly recall the notation used in the previous part
[
PP24a
]
, which mostly follows
[
Fuj82
]
and
[
Pal24
]
.

2A.
Divisors on surfaces

Curves and surfaces are understood to be irreducible and reduced. Given a birational morphism
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow Y
between normal surfaces we write
\operatorname{Exc}\psi
for its reduced exceptional divisor, and denote by
\operatorname{Bs}\psi^{-1}
the base locus of the inverse map
\psi^{-1}\colon Y\dashrightarrow X
. We often abuse the notation and write infinitely near points as elements of
\operatorname{Bs}\psi^{-1}
: for instance, if
\psi=\psi_{1}\circ\psi_{2}
, where
\psi_{1}
is a blowup at
y\in Y
and
\psi_{2}
is a blowup at
y^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Exc}\psi_{1}
then we write
y,y^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Bs}\psi^{-1}
.
We denote by
\rho(\psi)
the relative Picard rank of
\psi
, i.e.
\rho(\psi)=\rho(X)-\rho(Y)
is the number of irreducible components of
\operatorname{Exc}\psi
.

A
log surface

(X,D)
consists of a proper normal surface
X
and a
boundary
Weil divisor
D
with coefficients in the interval
[0,1]
, such that
K_{X}+D
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. In this article, we consider only log surfaces with reduced boundary.
A birational morphism of log surfaces
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is a birational morphism
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
such that
\psi_{*}D=\overline{D}
. It is a
log resolution of
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
if
X
is smooth,
D=\psi^{-1}_{*}\overline{D}+\operatorname{Exc}\psi
, and
D
is a simple normal crossing (snc) divisor.
A log resolution is
minimal
if it does not dominate birationally any other log resolution. A (minimal) log resolution of a normal surface
\overline{X}
is a (minimal) log resolution of
(\overline{X},0)
.

Let
D
be an effective divisor on a normal surface
X
. We say that
D
is
connected
if
\operatorname{Supp}D
is connected (in the Zariski topology). We write
D_{\mathrm{red}}
for
D
with reduced structure. By a
component
of
D
we mean an irreducible component of
D_{\mathrm{red}}
; we denote their number by
\#D
. A
subdivisor
of
D
is an effective divisor
T
such that
D-T
is effective, too. For two effective divisors
D_{1},D_{2}
we write
D_{1}\cap D_{2}
for the intersection of their supports. If they are reduced, we write
D_{1}\wedge D_{2}
for the sum of their common components.

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface. A
curve

C
on
X
is called an
n
-curve
if
C\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
C^{2}=n
. Let
D
be a reduced snc divisor on
X
, and let
C
be a component of
D
. The
branching number
of
C
in
D
is
\beta_{D}(C)\coloneqq C\cdot(D-C)
, i.e. the number of common points of
C
with the remaining part of
D
. We say that
C
is a
tip of
D
if
\beta_{D}(C)\leqslant 1
, and
C
is
branching in

D
if
\beta_{D}(C)\geqslant 3
.

We say that
D
is
negative definite
if so is its intersection matrix
[T_{i}\cdot T_{j}]_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant\#D}
, where
T_{1},\dots,T_{\#D}
are components of
D
. A
discriminant
of
D
is defined as
d(D)\coloneqq\det[-T_{i}\cdot T_{j}]_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant\#D}
if
D\neq 0
; and as
1
if
D=0
.

Lemma 2.1

(Recursive formula for discriminants, see
[
Fuj82
, Section 3]
)
.

Let
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
be reduced snc divisors on
X
with no common component. Assume that
D_{1}\cdot D_{2}=1
, and for
j\in\{1,2\}
let
C_{j}
be the unique component of
D_{j}
which meets
D_{3-j}
. Then

d(D_{1}+D_{2})=d(D_{1})d(D_{2})-d(D_{1}-C_{1})d(D_{2}-C_{2}).

Let
T
be an snc divisor on a smooth projective surface
X
. We say that
T
is
rational
all its components are. If
T
is connected and has no branching component then
T
is called a
chain
if it has a tip and
circular
if it does not. A connected snc divisor is a
tree
if it has no circular subdivisor. A
fork
is a tree with exactly one branching component, which additionally has branching number
3
.

Let
T
be a chain. It is
ordered
if it has a distinguished
first
tip, denoted by
\mathrm{tip}^{+}(T)
. We write
T^{(i)}
for the
i
-th component of
T
in the natural order, given by
T^{(1)}=\mathrm{tip}^{+}(T)
,
T^{(i)}\cdot T^{(i+1)}=1
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant\#T-1
. Then
\mathrm{tip}^{-}(T)\coloneqq T^{(\#T)}
is the
last
tip of
T
. We write
T^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}
for a chain
T
with the opposite order.

An ordered rational subchain
T
of a reduced divisor
D
is a
twig
of
D
if
\mathrm{tip}^{+}(T)
is a tip of
D
, and no component of
T
is branching in
D
. In this case, either
T=D
or
\mathrm{tip}^{-}(T)
meets
D-T
. We say that a twig of
D
is
maximal
if it is not properly contained in any other twig of
D
. A
(-2)
-chain
(
(-2)
-fork
,
(-2)
-twig
) is a chain (fork, twig) whose all components are
(-2)
-curves.

2B.
Types of chains and forks

A
type
of an ordered rational chain
T
is a sequence of integers
[a_{1},\dots,a_{\#T}]
, where
a_{i}=-(T^{(i)})^{2}
. We write
(a)_{m}
for an integer
a
repeated
m
times. We say that
T
is
admissible
if
a_{1},\dots,a_{\#T}\geqslant 2
.

For types
T_{1}=[a_{1},\dots,a_{k}]
,
T_{2}=[b_{1},\dots,b_{l}]
we write
[T_{1},T_{2}]=[T_{1},b_{1},\dots,b_{l}]=[a_{1},\dots,a_{k},b_{1},\dots,b_{l}]
etc. We also use the following conventions:

(2)

\begin{split}[a_{1},\dots,a_{k}]*[b_{1},\dots,b_{l}]&\coloneqq[a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1},a_{k}+b_{1}-1,b_{2},\dots,b_{l}],\\
[(2)_{-1}]*[b_{1},\dots,b_{l}]&\coloneqq[b_{1}+1,b_{2}\dots,b_{l}],\quad[(2)_{-1},b_{1},\dots,b_{l}]\coloneqq[b_{2},\dots,b_{l}].\end{split}

If
T
is an admissible chain or
T=[1]
, there is a unique type
T^{*}
such that a chain of type
[T,1,T^{*}]
can be blown down to a
0
-curve, see
[
Fuj82
, Proposition 4.7]
or
[
Rus02
]
. A chain
[T,1,T^{\prime}]
blows down to a smooth point if and only if
T^{\prime}=T^{*}*[(2)_{k}]
for some
k\geqslant-1
. Moreover, if a curve
C
meets a chain
R=[T,1,T^{*}]*[(2)_{k}]
once, normally, in
\mathrm{tip}^{+}(R)
, then blowing down
R
to a smooth point increases the self-intersection number of
C
by
k+2
. We refer to
[
Rus02
]
for more details on the combinatorics of such blowdowns.

Let
T
be a fork. Then
T=B+T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}
, where
\beta_{T}(B)=3
, and
T_{j}
are maximal twigs of
T
. Assume that
T
is rational, so
B=[b]
for some integer
b
. Then we say that
T
is of
type

\langle b;T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\rangle
. We say that
T
is
admissible
if
b\geqslant 2
, each twig
T_{j}
is admissible, and
\sum_{j=1}^{3}\frac{1}{d(T_{j})}>1
. Solving this inequality one gets the following known result, cf.
[
Miy01
, I.5.3.4]
.

Lemma 2.2

(Admissible forks
)
.

Let
F=\langle b;T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\rangle
be an admissible fork. Then the triple
\{d(T_{1}),d(T_{2}),d(T_{3})\}
is one of the following:
\{2,2,k\}
for some
k\geqslant 2
;
\{2,3,3\}
,
\{2,3,4\}
,
\{2,3,5\}
. In particular, the following hold.

(a)

At least one of the twigs of
F
is of type
[2]
.

(b)

For each
i
we have either
T_{i}\in\{[d],[(2)_{d-1}]\}
, where
d=d(T_{i})\in\{2,3,4,5\}
, or
T_{i}\in\{[2,3],[3,2]\}
,
d(T_{i})=5
.

(c)

If
\#T_{i}\geqslant 3
then either
F=\langle b;[2],[2],T_{i}\rangle
or
T_{i}=[(2)_{d-1}]
for some
d\in\{4,5\}
.

2C.
Log discrepancies

Let
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be a resolution of singularities of a normal surface, with exceptional divisor
D
. Let
T_{1},\dots,T_{k}
be the components of
D
. Since
D
is negative definite, the formula

\varphi^{*}K_{\overline{X}}=K_{X}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\operatorname{cf}(T_{i})T_{i}

uniquely defines rational numbers
\operatorname{cf}(T_{i})
, called the
coefficients
of
T_{i}
(with respect to
\overline{X}
). The
log discrepancy
of
T_{i}
is
\operatorname{ld}(T_{i})\coloneqq 1-\operatorname{cf}(T_{i})
. A normal surface is
log terminal
if for some (or, equivalently, any) resolution, every component of the exceptional divisor has positive log discrepancy
[
Kol13
, Definition 2.8]
. By
[
Kol13
, 3.40]
, a singularity is log terminal if and only if the exceptional divisor of its minimal resolution is an admissible chain or an admissible fork. If
D
is a disjoint sum of admissible chains and admissible forks, and
C
is a component of
D
, we write
\operatorname{ld}_{D}(C)
for the log discrepancy of
C
with respect to the log terminal surface obtained by contracting
D
. These numbers can be easily computed using Lemma
2.1
and formulas in Lemma
2.3
below. We will use them very frequently, often without explicit reference.

Lemma 2.3

(Formulas for log discrepancies, cf.
[
Miy01
, II.3.3]
or
[
Kol92
, 3.2]
)
.

(a)

Let
T
be an ordered admissible chain. Put

T^{(<j)}=\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}T^{(i)},\quad T^{(>j)}=\sum_{i=j+1}^{\#T}T^{(i)}.\qquad\mbox{Then}\quad\operatorname{ld}_{T}(T^{(j)})=\frac{d(T^{(>j)})+d(T^{(<j)})}{d(T)}.

(b)

Let
T=\langle b;T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\rangle
be an admissible fork with a branching component
B
. Put

\delta=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{1}{d(T_{i})},\quad e=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{d(T_{i}-\mathrm{tip}^{-}(T_{i}))}{d(T_{i})}.

Then
\delta>1
,
e<2\leqslant b
, and

\operatorname{ld}_{T}(B)=\frac{\delta-1}{b-e},\quad\operatorname{ld}_{T}(T_{i}^{(j)})=\frac{\operatorname{ld}_{T}(B)\cdot d(T_{i}^{(<j)})+d(T_{i}^{(>j)})}{d(T_{i})}.

The
weighted graph

\Gamma
of a reduced snc divisor
D
has a vertex
v_{C}
of weight
-C^{2}
for each component
C
of
D
, and an edge between
v_{C}
,
v_{C^{\prime}}
for each point of
C\cap C^{\prime}
. We say that a subgraph
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Gamma
is a
weighted subgraph
of
\Gamma
if the weight of each vertex of
\Gamma^{\prime}
does not exceed its weight as a vertex of
\Gamma
.

Lemma 2.4

(Log discrepancies do not decrease in weighted subgraphs
[
KM99
, L.1(2)]
, cf.
[
Pal24
, Lemma 7.6]
)
.

Let
D_{1},D_{2}
be rational chains or rational forks whose components have self-intersection numbers at most
-2
. Assume that
D_{2}
is admissible, and the weighted graph of
D_{1}
is a weighted subgraph of the weighted graph of
D_{2}
. Then
D_{1}
is admissible, too, and if
C_{1}
is a component of
D_{1}
corresponding to a component
C_{2}
of
D_{2}
, then

\operatorname{ld}_{D_{1}}(C_{1})\geqslant\operatorname{ld}_{D_{2}}(C_{2}).

A normal surface
\overline{X}
is
log terminal
if all its singularities are log terminal. The exceptional divisor of the minimal resolution of
\overline{X}
is a disjoint union of admissible chains and forks, say
T_{1},\dots,T_{k}
. The
singularity type
of
\overline{X}
is the collection of their types; we write it as
T_{1}+\dots+T_{k}
.

2D.
\mathbb{P}^{1}
–fibrations

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface. A
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
of
X
is a morphism
p\colon X\longrightarrow B
onto a curve
B
whose general fiber
F
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Let
D
be a reduced snc divisor on
X
. The number
F\cdot D
is called the
height
of
p
(with respect to
D
). The height of
(X,D)
, denoted by
0pt(X,D)
, is the infimum of heights with respect to
D
of all
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations of
X
, see formula (
1
). A
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
whose height with respect to
D
is equal to
0pt(X,D)
is called a
witnessing

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration.

Assume that
X
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration, and fix one such. For a reduced divisor
T
we write
T_{\mathrm{vert}}
for the sum of components of
T
which are
vertical
, i.e. are contained in fibers. We call
T_{\mathrm{hor}}\coloneqq T-T_{\mathrm{vert}}
the
horizontal part
of
T
. A horizontal curve
H
is called an
n
-section if
H\cdot F=n
for some (hence every) fiber
F
.

The
width
of
(X,D)
, denoted by
0pt(X,D)
, is the maximal number
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}
among all witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations, see
[
PP24a
, Definition 2.2]
. Clearly,
0pt(X,D)\leqslant 0pt(X,D)
, and the equality holds if and only if
X
admits a witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration such that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of
1
-sections. If
(X,D)
is the minimal log resolution of a normal surface
\overline{X}
then we write
0pt(\overline{X})\coloneqq 0pt(X,D)
,
0pt(\overline{X})\coloneqq 0pt(X,D)
.

A fiber
F
which is not isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
is called
degenerate
. Such fibers are constructed by blowing up over
0
-curves, so their structure is easy to understand, see
[
Fuj82
, §4]
. In particular, the
(-1)
-curves in
F_{\mathrm{red}}
are non-branching, and if
F_{\mathrm{red}}
contains a unique
(-1)
-curve
L
then
F
has exactly two components of multiplicity one, and they are tips of
F_{\mathrm{red}}
. Furthermore, if those tips lie in different connected components of
F_{\mathrm{red}}-L
then
F_{\mathrm{red}}
is a chain of type
[T,1,T^{*}]
.

We will frequently use the following consequence of
[
Fuj82
, 4.16]
proved in
[
PP24b
, Lemma 2.6]
.

Lemma 2.5

(Vertical
(-1)
-curves)
.

Let
(X,D)
be the minimal log resolution of a del Pezzo surface of rank one. Fix a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
, let
F
be a degenerate fiber and let
\sigma(F)
be the number of
(-1)
-curves in
F
.

(a)

A component of
F
is a
(-1)
-curve if and only if it is not contained in
D
.

(b)

We have
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}-1=\sum_{F}(\sigma(F)-1)
, where the sum runs over all degenerate fibers.

In the proof of Theorem
A
(Step
3
)
we will reconstruct cascades of minimal log resolutions of del Pezzo surfaces of rank 1 by blowing up within degenerate fibers, or in other words, reversing vertical swaps, see Section
1B
. The following elementary results tell us when to stop. More precisely, once we reach a log surface for which inequality (
3
) fails, then Lemma
2.7
(b)
implies that blowing up further cannot yield a del Pezzo surface, so the whole cascade has been reconstructed.

Lemma 2.6

(Criterion for ampleness of
-K_{\overline{X}}
,
[
PP24a
, Lemma 2.6]
)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a normal surface of rank 1 such that
K_{\overline{X}}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Let
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be its resolution,
D\coloneqq\operatorname{Exc}\pi
. Assume that
X
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration, let
F
be its fiber and let
H_{1},\dots,H_{h}
be all horizontal components of
D
. Then
\overline{X}
is del Pezzo if and only if

(3)

\sum_{j=1}^{h}\operatorname{ld}(H_{j})\,H_{j}\cdot F>D\cdot F-2.

Lemma 2.7

(Log discrepancies do not decrease after swaps,
[
PP24a
, Lemma 2.9]
)
.

Let
(X,D)
be the minimal log resolution of a log terminal del Pezzo surface of rank one. Let
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be a vertical swap.

(a)

Let
T^{\prime}
be a component of
D^{\prime}
and let
T
be its proper transform on
X
. Then
\operatorname{ld}_{D^{\prime}}(T^{\prime})\geqslant\operatorname{ld}_{D}(T)
.

(b)

The log surface
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is the minimal log resolution of a log terminal del Pezzo surface of rank one, too.

Part
(a)
follows from Lemma
2.4
, and
(b)
follows from
(a)
and Lemma
2.6
, see
[
PP24a
, Lemma 2.9]
.

∎

2E.
Representing families and moduli dimension

We now recall the definition of
moduli dimension
used in Theorem
A
(b)(b.ii)
, see
[
PP24a
, Definitions 1.9, 1.10]
for details. Let
\mathcal{C}
be a set consisting of some isomorphism classes of normal surfaces. We say that a smooth family
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
, where
\mathcal{D}
is a divisor on
\mathcal{X}
,
represents

\mathcal{C}
if the following hold.

(i)

The restriction of
f
to each component of
\mathcal{D}
is a smooth surjective morphism with irreducible fibers.

(ii)

The set of isomorphism classes of fibers
(X_{b},D_{b})\coloneqq(f^{-1}(b),\mathcal{D}|_{f^{-1}(b)})
equals the set of isomorphism classes of minimal log resolutions of surfaces in
\mathcal{C}
.

We say that the set
\mathcal{C}

has moduli dimension
d
if it is represented by a family
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
with
\dim B=d
, which is
almost universal
, that is, satisfies the following additional properties.

(iii)

The morphism
f
is equivariant with respect to the action of some finite group
G
, such that two fibers
(X_{b},D_{b})
and
(X_{b^{\prime}},D_{b^{\prime}})
are isomorphic if and only if
b
and
b^{\prime}
lie in the same
G
-orbit.

(iv)

The formal germ of
f
at each
b\in B
is a semiuniversal deformation of
(X_{b},D_{b})
.

In particular,
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{b}}(-\log D_{b}))=d
.

The image in
\operatorname{Aut}(B)
of the finite group
G
from
(iii)
is called the
symmetry group
of
f
.

Remark 2.8
.

We will see in Lemma
5.16
(1)
that each set
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 3}(\mathcal{S})
in Theorem
A
(b)(b.ii)
is represented by an almost universal families over
\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
, with symmetry group listed in Table
6
.

Remark 2.9
.

Theorem
A
and
[
PP24a
, Proposition C]
imply that for every log terminal singularity type
\mathcal{S}
other than
[2,2,3,(2)_{5}]+[3,2]
the set
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 3}(\mathcal{S})
is represented by a family.

For
\mathcal{S}=[2,2,3,(2)_{5}]+[3,2]
this is not the case. To see this write
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 3}(\mathcal{S})=\{\overline{X}_{1},\overline{X}_{2}\}
. By Proposition
D
(b)
the minimal log resolution
(X_{i},D_{i})
of
\overline{X}_{i}
for each
i=1,2
has
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{i}}(-\log D_{i}))=0
, so its infinitesimal deformations are trivial. We note that if
{k}=\mathbb{C}
then the smooth loci of
\overline{X}_{1}
and
\overline{X}_{2}
are not even homeomorphic in the Euclidean topology, as
H_{1}(\overline{X}_{1}^{\mathrm{reg}};\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}/3
and
H_{1}(\overline{X}_{2}^{\mathrm{reg}};\mathbb{Z})=0
by Proposition
4.10
(d)
.

2F.
Descendants with elliptic boundary

We now recall the definition of descendants with elliptic boundary from
[
PP24a
, Definition 1.11]
, and introduce we the notion of an
elliptic tie
, which will be used to distinguish del Pezzo surfaces with such descendants. Those surfaces are excluded in the assumptions of Theorem
A
, because their common structure makes them easier to understand together, regardless of their height. They have been classified in
[
PP24a
, Theorem E]
.

Definition 2.10

(Descendant with elliptic boundary, see
[
PP24a
, Definition 1.11]
)
.

Let
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
be a log surface. We say that the boundary
\overline{T}
is
elliptic
if it is an irreducible curve of arithmetic genus one contained in the smooth locus of
\overline{Y}
. We say that
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
is a
descendant
of a normal projective surface
\overline{X}
if the minimal log resolution
(X,D)
of
\overline{X}
admits a birational morphism
\varphi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{Y}
such that
\varphi_{*}D=\overline{T}
.

Definition 2.11

(An elliptic tie)
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface, and let
D
be a reduced snc divisor on
X
. Let
L
be a
(-1)
-curve on
X
which is not contained in
D
, and let
E
be the connected component of
D+L
containing
L
. We say that
L
is an
elliptic tie
on
(X,D)
if there is a birational morphism
\sigma\colon X\longrightarrow Y
onto a smooth surface
Y
such that
\operatorname{Exc}\sigma\subseteq E
and
\sigma_{*}E
is an irreducible curve of arithmetic genus 1.

Lemma 2.12
.

A normal projective surface of rank one has a descendant with elliptic boundary if and only if its minimal log resolution has an elliptic tie.

Let
(X,D)
be the minimal log resolution of a normal surface
\overline{X}
. Assume that
(X,D)
has an elliptic tie. Let
L
,
E
and
\sigma\colon X\longrightarrow Y
be as in Definition
2.11
. Since
\sigma
is an isomorphism in a neighborhood of
D+L-E
, there is a birational morphism
\alpha\colon Y\longrightarrow\overline{Y}
with
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha=\sigma_{*}(D+L-E)
, so
(\overline{Y},(\alpha\circ\sigma)_{*}E)
is a descendant of
\overline{X}
with elliptic boundary. The converse follows from
[
PP24a
, Lemma 1.12(b)]
, see Notation 6.1 loc. cit.

∎

We will see that del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and types listed in Theorem
A
have
no descendants with elliptic boundaries. For most types, this is a consequence of the following criterion.

Lemma 2.13

(Types with no elliptic-boundary descendants,
[
PP24a
, Theorem E(b)]
)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a del Pezzo surface of rank one and height at least
3
. Assume that the minimal resolution of all canonical singularities of
\overline{X}
has at most
5
exceptional components. Then
\overline{X}
has no descendant with elliptic boundary.

2G.
Summary of the notation

Let
(X,D)
be the minimal log resolution of a log terminal del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}
of rank 1. In the lemmas quoted in Theorem
A
we describe the singularity type of
\overline{X}
together with the structure of a certain witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
(chosen so that it leads to vertical swaps in Proposition
C
). More precisely, we describe the weighted graph of
D+\sum_{j}A_{j}
, where
A_{1},\dots A_{n}
are all vertical
(-1)
-curves, together with its decomposition into horizontal and vertical parts. We refer to this data as the
combinatorial type of
(X,D,p)
. To present it in an efficient way, we first write down the weighted graph of
D

using notation from Section
2B
, and then we decorate it as explained in
[
PP24a
, §2G]
. We now briefly recall this notation.

The preimage of each singular point of
\overline{X}
is an admissible chain or fork. We write down its type as follows.

•

[a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]
is a rational chain whose subsequent components have self-intersection numbers
-a_{1},\dots,-a_{n}
,

•

\langle b;T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\rangle
is a rational fork with a branching component
[b]
and twigs
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
,

•

(m)_{k}
stands for an integer
m
repeated
k
times,

•

we use the operator
*
and convention for
(2)_{-1}
introduced in formula (
2
).

We write the singularity type of
\overline{X}
as a formal sum of types of its singularities, and decorate it as follows.

•

We put in boldface those numbers which correspond to horizontal components of
D
.

•

If
D
contains a
1
- and a
2
-section, then we underline the (bold) number corresponding to the
2
-section.

•

We decorate by
|j|
those numbers which correspond to the components meeting the vertical
(-1)
-curve
A_{j}
.

•

We write
\prescript{|j|}{}{[}a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]\coloneqq[a_{1}^{|j|},a_{2},\dots,a_{n}]
and
[a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]^{|j|}\coloneqq[a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1},a_{n}^{|j|}]
.

In the figures, we use the following notation:

•

A solid line with label
“
n
”
denotes an
n
-curve in
D
. Label
n=-2
is skipped.

•

A dashed line denotes one of the vertical
(-1)
-curves
A_{j}
.

Sometimes, especially in Section
5
, we will enumerate the curves
A_{j}
starting from
0
, not
1
.

Notation 2.14
.

For a singularity type
\mathcal{S}
and for
w\in\{1,2,3\}
we denote by
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=w}(\mathcal{S})
the set of isomorphism classes of del Pezzo surfaces of rank one, height
3
and width
w
.

3.
Vertically primitive
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations of height
3

In this section we construct del Pezzo surfaces
\overline{Y}
which serve as vertically primitive models in Proposition
C
. More precisely, we construct log surfaces
(Y,D_{Y})
which are minimal log resolutions of del Pezzo surfaces
\overline{Y}
of singularity types listed in Proposition
C
, together with
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations
p_{Y}\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
of height
3
with respect to
D_{Y}
, such that
(Y,D_{Y},p_{Y})
is vertically primitive, see Section
1B
for a definition. The log surface
(Y,D_{Y})
, together with all degenerate fibers of
p_{Y}
, is shown in the last column of Table
1
. We put
{w}\coloneqq\#(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Proposition
C
asserts that the minimal log resolution of a log terminal del Pezzo surface of rank one, height
3
and width
{w}
, with some witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration, swaps vertically to some
(Y,D_{Y},p_{Y})
constructed below.

In each case, the construction proceeds as follows. We start with a log surface
(Z,B)
as in Proposition
B
and define a morphism
\varphi\colon(Y,D_{Y})\longrightarrow(Z,B)
where
D_{Y}
equals
\varphi^{*}B_{\mathrm{red}}
minus all
(-1)
-curves. The required
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height
3
on
(Y,D_{Y})
will be a pullback of the first projection in case
Z=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and of a pencil of lines through a point
p_{0}
in case
Z=\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

We write
\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})
for the singularity type of
\overline{Y}
. Some basic properties of
\overline{Y}
are listed in Proposition
3.6
.

{w}=3
, see Propositions

(a)
and

Remark 3.5

(Alternative construction of the surface from Example
3.4
(c)
)
.

The surface
\overline{Y}
from Example
3.4
(c)
has been first constructed by Bernasconi in
[
Ber21
]
as a counterexample to the Kawamata–Viehweg vanishing in characteristic
3
. In fact, Theorem 3.6 loc. cit. shows that
H^{1}(\overline{Y},\mathcal{O}_{\overline{Y}}(-\overline{A}))\neq 0
for the ample divisor
\overline{A}=\overline{A}_{1}+\overline{A}_{2}-\overline{A}_{3}
, where
\overline{A}_{j}
is the image of the
(-1)
-curve over
p_{j}
.

We now recall the construction from
[
Ber21
, §3.1]
and show that it indeed yields a surface isomorphic to
\overline{Y}
from Example
3.4
(c)
. This construction will be useful in the proof of Lemma
3.10
. Fix coordinates
[x:y]
and
[z:w]
on each factor of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and put
H=\{xz^{3}=yw^{3}\}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Since
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
, the curve
H
is tangent with multiplicity
3
to each fiber of the first projection
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Fix three such fibers, say
F_{1},F_{2},F_{3}
, let
(X,\widetilde{D})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},H+F_{1}+F_{2}+F_{3})
be the minimal log resolution, and let
X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the contraction of
D\coloneqq\widetilde{D}-A_{1}-A_{2}-A_{3}
, where
A_{j}
is the
(-1)
-curve in the preimage of
F_{j}
. Now
\overline{X}
is the surface from
[
Ber21
]
. To see that it is isomorphic to the one from Example
3.4
(c)
, let
\sigma\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a blowup at the point
F_{1}\cap H
, followed by the contraction of the proper transform of
F_{1}
and of the horizontal line passing through
F_{1}\cap H
. Then
\sigma_{*}(H+\sum_{i}F_{i})
is projectively equivalent to
{q}+\sum_{i}{l}_{i}
, so
\sigma
lifts to the required isomorphism.

We now summarize basic properties of surfaces
\overline{Y}
constructed above. Most importantly, we note that each
\overline{Y}
is unique up to an isomorphism, except in Examples
3.3
(a)
,
(b)
, where the choice of the fourth line yields a one-parameter family. This observation will be crucial for the proof of the uniqueness part of Theorem
A
. Proposition
3.6
(d)
below is a slightly stronger version of this statement, formulated using the language of
[
PP24a
, §2F]
which will be convenient later.

Furthermore, we note that even though the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations
p_{Y}
constructed above are vertically primitive, i.e. do not admit vertical swaps, the surfaces
\overline{Y}
itself may be non-primitive, i.e.
(Y,D_{Y})
may admit swaps which do not preserve the chosen
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations
p_{Y}
, see
[
PP24a
, Definition 1.3]
. Similarly, we may have
0pt(\overline{Y})=2
, so
p_{Y}
may be a non-witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration. Such cases are listed in Proposition
3.6
(b)
and
(c)
, respectively.

Proposition 3.6

(Properties of vertically primitive models from Proposition
C
)
.

Let
\overline{Y}
be a surface as in one of Examples
3.1
–
3.4
above. Then the following hold.

(a)

The surface
\overline{Y}
has a descendant with elliptic boundary, unless it is as in Example
3.4
(c)
.

(b)

The surface
\overline{Y}
is primitive, see
[
PP24a
, Definition 1.3(e)]
,
unless it is as in in Examples
3.2
(a)
or
3.3
(b)
,
(c)
.

(c)

We have
0pt(\overline{Y})=2
in Examples
3.1
,
3.2
(b)
,
(c)
and
3.3
(c)
. In all the other cases we have
0pt(\overline{Y})=3
.

(d)

Let
\widecheck{D}_{Y}
be the sum of
D_{Y}
and all vertical
(-1)
-curves except those meeting
D
in a node (i.e. except the one over
p_{1}
in Example
3.3
(a)
and
p_{3}
in Example
3.4
(c)
). Let
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
be the combinatorial type of
(Y,\widecheck{D}_{Y})
. Then
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})=1
, unless
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
is as in Example
3.3
(a)
,
(b)
. In the latter case
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
is represented by an
\operatorname{Aut}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
-faithful universal family over
\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
, parametrized by the choice of the fourth line
{l}_{4}
.

(e)

The isomorphism class of
\overline{Y}
is uniquely determined by the singularity type of
\overline{Y}
, unless
\overline{Y}
is as in Example
3.3
(a)
or
3.3
(b)
. In the latter cases, the set of isomorphism classes of surfaces
\overline{Y}
has moduli dimension
1
.

(f)

Put
h^{i}\coloneqq h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))
. Then
h^{0}=0
and the following hold.

(i)

h^{1}=1
in Examples
3.3
(a)
,
(b)
; and
h^{1}=0
otherwise.

(ii)

h^{2}=2
in Examples
3.3
(b)
and
3.4
(c)
;
h^{2}=1
in Examples
3.3
(a)
,
(c)
and
3.4
(b)
; and
h^{2}=0
otherwise.

In particular, if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3
then
h^{i}=0
for all
i\geqslant 0
.

(a)

The surface from Example
3.4
(c)
has no descendant with elliptic boundary because it has more than two non-canonical singularities, which is excluded in
[
PP24a
, Theorem E(c)]
. In the remaining cases we need to find an elliptic tie, see Definition
2.11
. We check directly that each of the following is an elliptic tie:

•

In Example
3.1
(a)
: the
(-1)
-curve over
p_{32}
, see Figure
1(a)
.

•

In Example
3.1
(b)
: the
(-1)
-curve over
p_{22}
, see Figure
1(b)
.

It remains to construct the cubic

used in the case
3.4
(a)
above. Fix four points

in a general position. For

and

\{q_{i}\}=\overline{{l}}_{i}\cap\overline{{l}}_{jk}

,

,

\overline{{l}}_{23}

,

on

such that

,

,

,

, we have
\overline{{q}}=\{(y-z)^{2}=x(y+z)\}
and
\overline{{c}}=\{y(y-x)=3z(x-z)\}
. The first conic is smooth since
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
, and the second one since
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3
. Let

be the standard quadratic transformation centered at

, i.e. we blow up those points and blow down the proper transforms of the lines joining them. Put

,

,

. Then
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{q}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{3})
is as in Example
3.4
(a)
, and

is the required cubic.

(b)

\overline{Y}
from Examples

and

,

. An elementary exercise, or an application of

. Witnessing

.

In the remaining cases when
\overline{Y}
has a descendant with elliptic boundary we have

by
[

, Lemma 6.3]
. By
(a)
it remains to prove that

in Example
3.4
(c)
. Suppose the contrary, so
0pt(\overline{Y})\leqslant 2
. Then some degenerate fiber
F
of a witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration contains a
(-3)
-curve. By Lemma
2.5

F_{\mathrm{red}}
is a sum of some components of

and at most two

or

. We have seen in

that
\overline{Y}
is primitive, so each
(-1)
-curve in

. Thus

, a contradiction.

(d)
Fix an element of
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
, i.e. a log surface
(Y,\widecheck{D}_{Y})
together with the fixed order of components of
\widecheck{D}_{Y}
, see
[

, §2F]
. Then a subdivisor of
\widecheck{D}_{Y}
supports a fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration as above. Thus we get a morphism
\varphi\colon(Y,\widecheck{D}_{Y})\longrightarrow(Z,B)
as above such that the center of each blowup in the decomposition of
\varphi
is uniquely determined by
(Z,B)
and the fixed order of components of
B
. If
\overline{Y}
is not as in Example
3.3
(a)
,
(b)
, then the latter is unique up to an isomorphism
so by the universal property of blowing up we get
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})=1
, as needed.

Consider
\overline{Y}
as in Example
3.3
(a)
,
(b)
. We have seen in
(a)
that
\overline{Y}
admits a descendant with elliptic boundary of type
3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
. Hence the required family is constructed in
[
PP24a
, Lemma 6.14 and Proposition 6.17(b)]
. To be precise, loc. cit. yields families representing the sets of isomorphism classes of log surfaces
(Y,D_{Y})
, but it is clear from their construction that their total spaces contain divisors corresponding to the
(-1)
-curves
\widecheck{D}_{Y}-D_{Y}
.

(e)
This follows from
(d)
and
[
PP24a
, Lema 2.20(c)]
. Alternatively, in all cases except
3.4
(c)
parts
(a)
,

show that
0pt(\overline{Y})\leqslant 2
or
\overline{Y}
has a descendant with elliptic boundary, so

follows from the classification
[
PP24a
]
.

(f)
If
0pt(\overline{Y})=2
or
0pt(\overline{Y})=3
and
\overline{Y}
has a descendant with elliptic boundary then the numbers
h^{i}
are computed in
[
PP24a
, Proposition D and Theorem E]
. Thus by
(a)
and
(c)
we can assume that
\overline{Y}
is as in Example
3.4
(c)
. Let
A=[1]
,
G=[2]
be the third and the second exceptional curve over
p_{3}
, and let
\sigma\colon(Y,D_{Y}-G)\longrightarrow(Y^{\prime},D_{Y^{\prime}})
be the contraction of
A
, so
(Y^{\prime},D_{Y^{\prime}})
is as in Example
3.4
(b)
, see Figure
4
. The blowup
\sigma
is inner, so
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log(D_{Y}-G)))=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{Y^{\prime}}(-\log D_{Y^{\prime}}))
, see
[
PP24a
, Lemma 2.11(a),(b)]
. Thus
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log(D_{Y}-G)))=0
for
i=0,1
and
h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log(D_{Y}-G)))=1
. We have an exact sequence
0\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y})\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log(D_{Y}-G))\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{G}\longrightarrow 0
, where
\mathcal{N}_{G}
is the normal bundle to
G
in
Y
, see Lemma 2.12(a) loc. cit. Since
h^{1}(\mathcal{N}_{G})=1
and
h^{i}(\mathcal{N}_{G})=0
for
i\neq 1
, we get
h^{i}=0
for
i=0,1
and
h^{2}=2
, as claimed.

∎

3D.
Automorphisms of primitive surfaces in case
{w}=1
,
\operatorname{char}{k}=3

The last result of this section describes the automorphism groups of surfaces from Example
3.4
(b)
,
(c)
. Some of these automorphisms will be used in the proof of Lemma
6.7
, i.e. in case
0pt(\overline{X})=1
,
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
of Theorem
A
.

Lemma 3.7

(
\operatorname{Aut}(\textnormal{\ref{ex:w=1}\ref{item:w=1_cha=3_GK}})\cong\mathrm{D}_{6}
)
.

Let
\overline{Y}
be the surface from Example
3.4
(b)
, and let
(Y,D_{Y})
be its minimal log resolution. Then
\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{Y})=\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
is isomorphic to the dihedral group of order
12
, and is generated by the following automorphisms, cf. Remark
3.8
:

(a)

(G_{1},G_{1}^{\prime},G_{2},G_{2}^{\prime},H,H^{\prime},T_{1},T_{2})\mapsto(H,H^{\prime},G_{1}^{\prime},G_{1},G_{2},G_{2}^{\prime},T_{2},T_{1})
,

(b)

(G_{1},G_{1}^{\prime},G_{2},G_{2}^{\prime},H,H^{\prime},T_{1},T_{2})\mapsto(G_{2}^{\prime},G_{2},G_{1}^{\prime},G_{1},H^{\prime},H,T_{1},T_{2})
,

where
H=\varphi^{-1}_{*}{q}
,
H+H^{\prime}=[2,2]
; and for
i\in\{1,2\}
we have
(\varphi^{*}{l}_{i})_{\mathrm{red}}=[3,1,2,2]=T_{i}+A_{i}+G_{i}+G_{i}^{\prime}
.

Let
L\subseteq Y
be the proper transform of the line joining
p_{1}
with
p_{3}
, and let
G
be the connected component of
D_{Y}
of type
[2]
. For every automorphism
\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
we have
\alpha(G)=G
and
\alpha(\{T_{1},T_{2}\})=\{T_{1},T_{2}\}
, so
\alpha(L)
is a
(-1)
-curve meeting
D_{Y}
normally in
G
,
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
. Since
K_{Y}
and the components of
D_{Y}
generate the group
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(Y)
, we get
\alpha(L)\equiv L
, hence
\alpha(L)\cdot L=L^{2}<0
and therefore
\alpha(L)=L
. Thus every automorphism of
(Y,D_{Y})
fixes
L
and
G
. It follows that
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
is a lift of the stabilizer subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(Z,D_{Z})
with respect to the point
\gamma(L)
, where
\gamma\colon(Y,D_{Y}-G)\longrightarrow(Z,D_{Z})
is the contraction of
L
.

The log surface
(Z,D_{Z})
is the minimal log resolution of the canonical del Pezzo surface
\overline{Z}
of rank one and type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
. An explicit construction of
\overline{Z}
is given for instance in
[
PP24b
, Example 7.1]
. The curves
G_{1}^{(1)}
,
G_{1}^{(2)}
,
G_{2}^{(2)}
,
G_{2}^{(1)}
,
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
,
Q
and
R
from loc. cit. can be identified with the images of
G_{1}
,
G_{1}^{\prime}
,
G_{2}
,
G_{2}^{\prime}
,
H
,
H^{\prime}
,
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, respectively (note the reverse order of
G_{2}^{(j)}
). Under this identification we have
\gamma(L)=Q\cap R
. The stabilizer of the point
\gamma(L)
in
\operatorname{Aut}(Z,D_{Z})
is generated by the automorphisms (i), (ii), (iv) from
[
PP24b
, Lemma 7.2(d)]
. They lift to
\sigma_{\textnormal{\ref{item:Aut_rotation}}}^{3}
,
\sigma_{\textnormal{\ref{item:Aut_rotation}}}^{-2}
,
\sigma_{\textnormal{\ref{item:Aut_symmetry}}}
, where
\sigma_{\textnormal{\ref{item:Aut_rotation}}}
,
\sigma_{\textnormal{\ref{item:Aut_symmetry}}}
are the automorphisms described in
(a)
,
(b)
, respectively.

∎

Remark 3.8

(Visualizing
\operatorname{Aut}(\textnormal{\ref{ex:w=1}\ref{item:w=1_cha=3_GK}})
, see Figure
5
)
.

One can view the isomorphism
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})\cong\mathrm{D}_{6}
from Lemma
3.7
as follows, see Figure
5
. Consider a regular hexagon with vertices labeled subsequently by
G_{1},H,G_{2},G_{1}^{\prime},H^{\prime},G_{2}^{\prime}
, and edges labeled alternatively by
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
. Now the automorphisms from Lemma
3.7
(a)
and
(b)
are, respectively, the
60^{\circ}
rotation and a symmetry through the axis perpendicular to
HH^{\prime}
.

.
Remark
3.8
: the automorphism group of the surface from Example
3.4
(b)
viewed as the symmetry group of a regular hexagon.

This identification leads to a convenient description of the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
on the set of
(-1)
-curves on
Y
. The
(-1)
-curve
L
is fixed by
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
. The
(-1)
-curve
A_{1}
meets
D_{Y}
on
T_{1}
,
G_{1}
and
H
. Acting by
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
, we see that for every edge of the hexagon, there is a
(-1)
-curve on
Y
meeting
D_{Y}
exactly three times, on the
(-3)
-curves corresponding to that edge, and the
(-2)
-curves corresponding to its vertices. Those
(-1)
-curves are:
A_{1},A_{2}
and, say,
A_{\spadesuit}
,
A_{\heartsuit}
,
A_{\diamondsuit}
and
A_{\clubsuit}
: in Figure
5
, we mark the common points of
A_{\star}
with
D_{Y}
by
\star\in\{\spadesuit,\heartsuit,\diamondsuit,\clubsuit\}
. Note that
A_{\heartsuit}=\varphi^{-1}(p_{0})
. Those
(-1)
-curves are pairwise disjoint since
A_{1}\cap A_{2}=\emptyset
.

Similarly, the orbit of
A_{3}
is
\{A_{3},V_{1},V_{2}\}
, where for
i\in\{1,2\}
the
(-1)
-curve
V_{i}
satisfies
V_{i}\cdot A_{i}=1
,
V_{i}\cdot D_{Y}=3
and passes through
G_{3-i}\cap G_{3-i}^{\prime}
and
A_{3}\cap G
. To see them in the hexagon, label the diagonals
HH^{\prime}
and
G_{i}G_{i}^{\prime}
by
A_{3}
and
V_{i}
, and label their common point by
G
. Now, each of those three
(-1)
-curves meets three
(-2)
-curves whose labels lie on the chosen diagonal; and two
(-1)
-curves corresponding to edges parallel to it.

Eventually, let
A_{\bullet}
and
A_{\circ}
be the proper transforms of, respectively, the line joining
p_{2}
with
p_{3}
and the conic passing through
p_{1}
and tangent to
{l}_{i}
at
p_{i}
,
i\in\{2,3\}
. Then
\{A_{\circ},A_{\bullet}\}
is another orbit of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
. To see it in the hexagon, we label the vertices
H,G_{1}^{\prime},G_{2}^{\prime}
by
“
\bullet
”
and the remaining ones by
“
\circ
”
, so the
(-1)
-curve
A_{\star}
meets all
(-2)
-curves labeled by
\star\in\{\bullet,\circ\}
.

The points where some two of the above twelve
(-1)
-curves meet are:
V_{1}\cap V_{2}\cap V_{3}
, and the orbit of
A_{3}\cap A_{\spadesuit}
.

These twelve
(-1)
-curves exhaust all
(-1)
-curves on
Y
. We will not use this fact, so we leave the proof as an exercise for the reader. One can see this either from a direct computation in
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(Y)
, or from the description of the
(-1)
-curves on the minimal resolution of del Pezzo surface of type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
given e.g. in
[
BBD84
, p. 595]
.

Remark 3.9

(
\operatorname{Aut}(\textnormal{\ref{ex:w=1}\ref{item:w=1_cha=3_GK}})\leqslant\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{3})
)
.

The automorphism group of the surface
\overline{Y}
from Example
3.4
(b)
can be described in yet another way, as follows. By
[
KN22
, Proposition 5.1(5)]
, cf.
[
PP24b
, Remark 7.3]
, the minimal log resolution
(Z,D_{Z})
of a del Pezzo surface of rank one and type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
is isomorphic to the blowup of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
in
p_{1},\dots,p_{8}
, where
p_{0},\dots,p_{8}
are all
\mathbb{F}_{3}
-rational points which do not lie on a fixed
\mathbb{F}_{3}
-rational line
{l}
. The divisor
D_{Z}
is the proper transform of all
\mathbb{F}_{3}
-rational lines not passing through
p_{0}
and different from
{l}
. Now
\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{Y})
can be identified with the stabilizer in
\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{3})
of
p_{0}
,
{l}
, and one
\mathbb{F}_{3}
-rational point on
{l}
.

Lemma 3.10

(
\operatorname{Aut}(\textnormal{\ref{ex:w=1}\ref{item:w=1_cha=3_not-GK}})\cong S_{4}
)
.

Let
\overline{Y}
be the surface from Example
3.4
(c)
, and let
(Y,D_{Y})
be its minimal log resolution. Then
\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{Y})=\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
is isomorphic to the symmetric group
S_{4}
, permuting the
(-3)
-curves in
D_{Y}
. It is generated by all permutations of the three degenerate fibers
\varphi^{*}{l}_{i}
,
i\in\{1,2,3\}
and the involution

(4)

(G_{1},G_{1}^{\prime},G_{2},G_{2}^{\prime},G_{3},G_{3}^{\prime},H,T_{3},T_{1},T_{2})\mapsto(G_{1}^{\prime},G_{1},G_{2}^{\prime},G_{2},G_{3},G_{3}^{\prime},T_{3},H,T_{2},T_{1}),

where
H=\varphi^{-1}_{*}{q}
and for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
we have
(\varphi^{*}{l}_{i})_{\mathrm{red}}=[3,1,2,2]=T_{i}+A_{i}+G_{i}+G_{i}^{\prime}
, cf. Remark
3.11
.

Consider the group homomorphism
\sigma\colon\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})\longrightarrow S_{4}
given by the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
on the set of
(-3)
-curves in
D_{Y}
. We will show that
\sigma
is surjective and injective.

The original construction of
\overline{Y}
from
[
Ber21
]
, recalled in Remark
3.5
, shows that
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
contains the symmetric group
S_{3}
permuting the degenerate fibers. To construct the involution (
4
), let
(Y,D_{Y}-G_{3})\longrightarrow(Z,D_{Z})
be the contraction of
A_{3}
. Then
(Z,D_{Z})
is the minimal log resolution of the surface from Example
3.4
(b)
, and the involution
\sigma_{\textnormal{\ref{item:Aut_rotation}}}^{3}
, where
\sigma_{\textnormal{\ref{item:Aut_rotation}}}
is as in Lemma
3.7
(a)
, lifts to (
4
). We conclude that
\sigma
is surjective.

To see that
\sigma
is injective fix
\alpha\in\ker\sigma
. Then
\alpha(H)=H
, so we have
\alpha(F)\cdot H=3
for a fiber
F
and
\alpha(F)\cdot V=0
for every component
V
of
D_{Y}-H
. Since
K_{Y}
and the components of
D_{Y}
generate
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(Y)
, it follows that
\alpha(F)\equiv F
, so
\alpha
maps fibers to fibers. Since
\alpha\in\ker\sigma
, it follows that
\alpha
fixes each component of
D_{Y}
, so it acts trivially on
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(Y)
, and therefore fixes each
(-1)
-curve on
Y
. We conclude that
\alpha
descends to an automorphism of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
fixing
\varphi_{*}D_{Y}={q}+\sum_{i}{l}_{i}
componentwise, so
\alpha=\mathrm{id}
, as claimed.

∎

Remark 3.11

(Visualizing
\operatorname{Aut}(\textnormal{\ref{ex:w=1}\ref{item:w=1_cha=3_not-GK}})
, see Figure
6
)
.

One can view the isomorphism
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})\cong S_{4}
from Lemma
3.10
as follows. Recall that
S_{4}
is the rotation group of a cube, permuting its four diagonals
[
Ser77
, Exercise 5.5]
. We label those diagonals by the
(-3)
-curves
H,T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
. Next, we label the faces of the cube by the
(-2)
-curves
G_{i}
,
G_{i}^{\prime}
,
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, so that
G_{i}
is opposite to
G_{i}^{\prime}
; the diagonal
T_{i}
passes through the common vertex of
G_{i}
and
G_{j}^{\prime}
for both
j\in\{1,2,3\}\setminus\{i\}
; and
H
passes through the common vertex of
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
and
G_{3}
. Now the rotation group
S_{4}
acts on those labels as in the same way as
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
acts on the components of
D_{Y}
. For example, the involution (
4
) is a
180^{\circ}
rotation about the axis joining the centers of faces
G_{3}
and
G_{3}^{\prime}
.

.
Remark
3.11
: the automorphism group of the surface from Example
3.4
(c)
viewed as the rotation group of a cube.

Using this identification, we can describe the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
on the set of
(-1)
-curves on
Y
, just like we did in Remark
3.11
for the surface from Example
3.4
(b)
. The orbit of
A_{1}
is
\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},A_{\heartsuit},A_{\diamondsuit},A_{\bigstar}\}
, where
A_{\bigstar}
is the proper transform of the line joining
p_{1}
and
p_{3}
, denoted by
L
in Remark
3.8
, and
A_{\heartsuit}
,
A_{\diamondsuit}
are proper transforms of
(-1)
-curves denoted in Remark
3.8
by the same letters. Each of these
(-1)
-curves meets exactly one
(-2)
-curve, which gives another labeling of the faces of the cube. more explicitly, we add labels
A_{i}
to the face
G_{i}
, and
\heartsuit,\diamondsuit,\bigstar
to faces
G_{1}^{\prime},G_{2}^{\prime},G_{3}^{\prime}
, respectively. In Figure
6
, we denote by
\heartsuit,\diamondsuit,\bigstar
the common points of
D_{Y}
with
A_{\heartsuit}
,
A_{\diamondsuit}
and
A_{\bigstar}
, respectively.

Let
V_{i}
be the proper transform of the curve
V_{i}
from Remark
3.8
,
i\in\{1,2\}
. Its orbit is
\{V_{1},V_{2},V_{3}\}
, where each
V_{i}
passes through
G_{j}\cap G_{j}^{\prime}
for both
j\in\{1,2,3\}\setminus\{i\}
. To see this orbit in the cube, we add a label
V_{i}
to both opposite faces
G_{i}
and
G_{i}^{\prime}
.

Eventually, let
A_{\bullet}
be the proper transform of the line joining
p_{2}
and
p_{3}
. Its orbit is
\{A_{\bullet},A_{\circ},A_{\spadesuit},A_{\clubsuit}\}
, where we denote the proper transforms of
(-1)
-curves from Remark
3.8
by the same letters. Each of those
(-1)
-curves meets exactly one
(-3)
-curve, and we add its label to the corresponding diagonal of the cube: to keep Figure
6
legible, we put those labels at the ends of each diagonal.

Now the action of
S_{4}
on this labeled cube is exactly the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
on the components of
D_{Y}
and the thirteen
(-1)
-curves described above. A direct computation in
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(Y)
shows that those are the only
(-1)
-curves on
Y
.

4.
Case
0pt(\overline{X})=3

We begin the proof of Theorem
A
.
In this section we study the case
0pt=3
, that is, we assume that

(5)

\overline{X}
is a log terminal del Pezzo surface of rank one,
0pt(\overline{X})=3
;
(X,D)
is the minimal log resolution of
\overline{X}
,
and
p\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration such that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of three
1
-sections.

Here
D=D_{\mathrm{hor}}+D_{\mathrm{vert}}
is the decomposition into a horizontal and vertical part, see Section
2D
.

As we have outlined in the introduction, we first prove the relevant case of Proposition
B
, that is, we choose
p
so that it factors as
p=\operatorname{pr}_{1}\circ\psi
for some
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},B)
, where
\operatorname{pr}_{1}\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the first projection, and
B
is a sum of three vertical and three horizontal lines. Next, we prove an analogous statement of Proposition
C
, that is, we choose
p
so that
\psi
can be factored as
\psi=\varphi\circ\varphi_{+}
, where
\varphi\colon(Y,D_{Y})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},B)
is as in Example
3.1
, and
\varphi_{+}\colon(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(Y,D_{Y})
is a vertical swap.

4A.
Proof of Proposition
B
: constructing the minimalizations
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},B)

The aim of this section is to prove Proposition
B
in case
0pt(\overline{X})=3
. We keep the assumption (
5
).
First, we summarize basic properties of
p
and we fix some notation for the remaining part of this section.

Lemma 4.1

(Degenerate fibers)
.

Assume
(X,D,p)
is as in (
5
). Let
F_{1},\dots,F_{\nu}
be all degenerate fibers of
p
. For
j=1,\dots,\nu
, let
\sigma(F_{j})
be the number of
(-1)
-curves in
(F_{j})_{\mathrm{red}}
. Let
H_{1},H_{2},H_{3}
be the components of
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
.

(a)

Put
\nu_{k}=\#\{j:\sigma(F_{j})=k\}
for
k\geqslant 1
. Then
(\nu_{2},\nu_{3})=(2,0)
or
(0,1)
, and
\nu_{k}=0
for
k\geqslant 4
.

(b)

Contract all vertical
(-1)
-curves on
X
and its images which are disjoint from the images of
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
, and denote the resulting morphism by
\varphi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\widehat{X},\widehat{D})
. For a degenerate fiber
F
of
p
put
\widehat{F}=\varphi_{*}F
and
V_{F}=\varphi^{-1}_{*}\widehat{F}
. Then one of the following holds.

(i)

\sigma(F)=1
,
\widehat{F}=[0]
,
V_{F}\subseteq D_{\mathrm{vert}}
,
V_{F}\cdot H_{j}=1
and
H_{i}\cdot H_{j}=0
for any
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
,
i\neq j
.

(ii)

\sigma(F)=1
,
\widehat{F}=[1,1]
,
V_{F}\subseteq D_{\mathrm{vert}}
.

(iii)

\sigma(F)=2
,
\widehat{F}=[1,(2)_{s},1]
for some
s\geqslant 0
.

(iv)

\sigma(F)=3
,
\widehat{F}=\langle 3;[1,(2)_{s_{1}}],[1,(2)_{s_{2}}],[1,(2)_{s_{3}}]\rangle
for some
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\geqslant 0
.

(a)
This follows from Lemma
2.5
(b)
.

(b)
. Assume
\widehat{F}=[0]
. Then
V_{F}
meets all components of
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
, so
V_{F}\subseteq D
and
\sigma(F)=1
by
[
PP24a
, Lemma 2.8(c)]
. Since
D
has no circular subdivisor, the components of
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
are pairwise disjoint, so
(b)(b.i)
holds.

Assume
\widehat{F}\neq[0]
, and let
\widehat{\sigma}
be the number of
(-1)
-curves in
\widehat{F}
. Every such
(-1)
-curve meets
\widehat{D}_{\mathrm{hor}}
, so its proper transform in
V_{F}
meets
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
, in particular has multiplicity one in
\widehat{F}
. It follows that
\widehat{\sigma}\geqslant 2
. If
\widehat{\sigma}>\sigma(F)
then two
(-1)
-curves in
\widehat{F}
meet at a base point of
\varphi^{-1}
, so
(b)(b.ii)
holds. In the other case, we have
\widehat{\sigma}\leqslant\sigma(F)\leqslant 3
by
(a)
, and since
\widehat{F}
blows down to a
0
-curve, we get
(b)(b.iii)
or
(b)(b.iv)
.

∎

The next lemma is a key step in this section.
Once it is proved, to infer the relevant part of Proposition
B
it is enough to show that one can choose
p
with only three degenerate fibers, which we do in Lemma
4.6
below.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
(X,D,p)
be as in (
5
). One can choose the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p
so that the components
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
,
H_{3}
of
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
are pairwise disjoint.

Moreover, there is a birational morphism
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},B)
such that
p=\operatorname{pr}_{1}\circ\psi
, where
\operatorname{pr}_{1}\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the first projection,
B_{\mathrm{hor}}
is a sum of three horizontal lines, and
\#B_{\mathrm{vert}}\geqslant 3
.

Contract all vertical
(-1)
-curves in
X
and its images which meet the horizontal part of the boundary at most once. Denote the resulting morphism by
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(Z,B)
. Then
Z
is smooth,
B
is snc and
B_{\mathrm{hor}}\cong D_{\mathrm{hor}}
.
Suppose that the induced
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
Z
has a degenerate fiber, say
F
. Since
F\cdot\psi_{*}D_{\mathrm{hor}}=3
,
F
has exactly one
(-1)
-curve, say
L
. Such
L
has multiplicity at least
2
in
F
, so
L\cdot\psi_{*}D_{\mathrm{hor}}\leqslant 1
, a contradiction. Thus
Z
is isomorphic to a Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{n}
for some
n\geqslant 0
. Put
\overline{H}_{i}=\psi(H_{i})
,
\overline{V}_{i}=\psi_{*}F_{i}
and
V_{i}=\psi^{-1}_{*}\overline{V}_{i}
. Then
V_{i}
is a component of the fiber
F_{i}
. Put
\eta=\#B_{\mathrm{vert}}
and order the degenerate fibers of
p
so that
B_{\mathrm{vert}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\eta}\overline{V}_{i}
.

If the components of
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
are pairwise disjoint, then
n=0
and the other projection of
\mathbb{F}_{0}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
pulls back to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height
\eta
, so
\eta\geqslant 0pt(\overline{X})=3
; as needed. Hence it remains to find
p
with disjoint
H_{j}
’s.

Claim 1
.

We can assume that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
is not a chain.

Assume that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
is a chain. Then
B_{\mathrm{hor}}\cong D_{\mathrm{hor}}
is a chain, too. Say that
\overline{H}_{j}=B_{\mathrm{hor}}^{(j)}
. Since each
\overline{H}_{j}
is a
1
-section,
\overline{H}_{i}-\overline{H}_{j}
is linearly equivalent to a multiple of a fiber. In particular
0=(\overline{H}_{1}-\overline{H}_{2})\cdot(\overline{H}_{1}-\overline{H}_{3})=\overline{H}_{1}^{2}-\overline{H}_{1}\cdot(\overline{H}_{2}+\overline{H}_{3})+\overline{H}_{2}\cdot\overline{H}_{3}=\overline{H}_{1}^{2}
, so
n=0
, i.e.
Z\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. It follows that
B_{\mathrm{hor}}=[0,-2,0]
.
Recall that the divisor
B=\psi_{*}D
is snc, so since
D
has no circular subdivisor, the fiber
\overline{V}_{j}
for each
j\in\{1,\dots,\eta\}
contains at least two base points of
\psi^{-1}
. Its preimage
F_{j}
has at least two
(-1)
-curves, so
\eta\leqslant\nu_{2}\leqslant 2
by Lemma
4.1
(a)
.

Let
\widetilde{p}
be the pullback to
X
of the other
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and let
\widetilde{D}_{\mathrm{hor}}
be the horizontal part of
D
with respect to
\widetilde{p}
. Then
\widetilde{D}_{\mathrm{hor}}=H_{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{\eta}V_{i}
. We have
3\geqslant\eta+1=\#\widetilde{D}_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 0pt(X,D)=3
, so
\widetilde{D}_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of three
1
-sections and
\eta=\nu_{2}
. It follows that
\eta=\nu
: indeed, by Lemma
4.1
(a)
we have
\nu_{k}=0
for
k\geqslant 3
, and by Lemma
4.1
(b)
every degenerate fiber
F
with
\sigma(F)=1
meets
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
in components of
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
, so its image is contained in
B_{\mathrm{vert}}
. In particular, every base point of
\psi^{-1}
lying on
B_{\mathrm{hor}}
lies on
B_{\mathrm{vert}}
, too. Since
H_{2}^{2}\leqslant-2<2=\overline{H}_{2}^{2}
, one of those base points lies in
\overline{H}_{2}\cap B_{\mathrm{vert}}
. Thus
\widetilde{D}_{\mathrm{hor}}
is not a chain, and replacing
p
with
\widetilde{p}
proves the claim.

∎

Since no subdivisor of
D
is circular, Claim
1
allows us to assume that
H_{1}\cdot H_{2}=1
and
H_{1}\cdot H_{3}=H_{2}\cdot H_{3}=0
. It follows that
Z\cong\mathbb{F}_{1}
and
\overline{H}_{3}=[1]
is the negative section.

Claim 2
.

We can assume that
\eta\geqslant 3
; in particular,
\nu\geqslant 3
.

Let
\tau\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the composition of
\psi
with the contraction of
\overline{H}_{3}
. Then
{l}_{i}\coloneqq\tau(H_{i})

i\in\{1,2\}
and
{l}_{j}^{\prime}\coloneqq\tau_{*}F_{j}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,\eta\}
are lines, and
{l}_{1}^{\prime}\dots,{l}_{\eta}^{\prime}
meet at a common point
\tau(H_{3})\not\in{l}_{1}\cup{l}_{2}
. Write
\{p_{ij}\}={l}_{i}\cap{l}_{j}^{\prime}
.

Since
D
has no circular subdivisor, for every
j\in\{1,\dots,\eta\}
there is an
i\in\{1,2\}
such that
p_{ij}\in\operatorname{Bs}\tau^{-1}
, so, say,
p_{11}\in\operatorname{Bs}\tau^{-1}
. If
\eta\geqslant 2
then since
H_{i}^{2}\leqslant-2<1=\ell_{i}^{2}
, we can assume that
p_{22}\in\operatorname{Bs}\tau^{-1}
, too. Moreover, interchanging
p_{11}
with
p_{22}
, if needed, we can assume that
\tau
factors through at least two blowups over
p_{11}
, so the proper transform of the first exceptional curve over
p_{11}
, call it
E
, is a component of
D
.

The pencil of lines through
p_{11}
pulls back to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
, such that the horizontal part of
D
consists of
1
-sections, namely:
E
,
\tau^{-1}_{*}{l}_{2}
, and
\tau^{-1}_{*}{l}_{2}^{\prime},\dots,\tau^{-1}_{*}{l}_{\eta}^{\prime}
. Thus
\eta+1\geqslant 0pt(\overline{X})=3
, so
\eta\geqslant 2
. If the equality holds then, since
p_{22}\in\operatorname{Bs}\tau^{-1}
, the above three
1
-sections are pairwise disjoint, as needed.

∎

For a degenerate fiber
F_{i}
, let
\widehat{F}_{i}\subseteq\widehat{X}
be as in Lemma
4.1
(b)
; and for a divisor
\widehat{G}
on
\widehat{X}
let
G
be its proper transform on
X
. Since
\nu\geqslant 3
by Claim
2
, Lemma
4.1
(a)
implies that
\nu_{1}\geqslant 1
, i.e.
p
has a degenerate fiber
F_{i}
with exactly one
(-1)
-curve. Since
H_{1}\cdot H_{2}=1
, this fiber is as in Lemma
4.1
(b)(b.ii)
, i.e.
\widehat{F}_{i}=[1,1]
, and both tips of
\widehat{F}_{i}
, namely
\widehat{V}_{i}
and, say,
\widehat{G}_{i}
meet
\widehat{D}_{\mathrm{hor}}
. We have
V_{i},G_{i}\subseteq D_{\mathrm{vert}}
. Since
(V_{i}+G_{i})\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}=F_{i}\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}=3
, we have
V_{i}\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}=2
and
G_{i}\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}=1
. Since
D
has no circular subdivisor,
V_{i}
meets
H_{3}
. It follows that all components of
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
lie in the same connected component of
D
, call it
D_{0}
.

The fact that no subdivisor of
D
is circular implies that there is only one fiber of the above type, i.e.
\nu_{1}=1
. By Lemma
6.1
(a)
we get
\nu_{2}=2
and
\nu_{k}=0
for
k\geqslant 3
, so
3=\nu=\eta
by Claim
2
.
We order the degenerate fibers so that
\sigma(F_{1})=\sigma(F_{2})=2
,
\sigma(F_{3})=1
. Write
\{1,2\}=\{j_{3},\widehat{j}_{3}\}
, where
V_{3}
meets
H_{j_{3}}
and
G_{3}
meets
H_{\widehat{j}_{3}}
.

Using this notation, we conclude that
D
contains a chain
[H_{3},V_{3},H_{j_{3}},H_{\widehat{j}_{3}},G_{3}]
. Since
D
has no circular subdivisor, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
the curve
V_{i}
meets at most one component of this chain. On the other hand, the image of this chain on
\mathbb{F}_{1}
meets
\overline{V}_{i}
in three points, so since
\sigma(F_{i})=2
, two of those points are base points of
\psi^{-1}
. As a consequence,
F_{i}
meets each section
H_{j}
in a different component, say
L_{i,j}
. Note that one of the curves
L_{i,j}
equals
V_{i}
, and the other two are contained in different connected components of
\operatorname{Exc}\psi
.

Claim 3
.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
we have
F_{i}=[1,(2)_{s_{i}},1]
for some
s_{i}\geqslant 1
. Moreover, after possibly interchanging
F_{1}
with
F_{2}
and
H_{1}
with
H_{2}
, we can pick an order on each chain
F_{1},F_{2}
so that the following hold (cf. Figure
7
).

(a)

H_{1}
meets
\mathrm{tip}^{+}(F_{1})
and
\mathrm{tip}^{+}(F_{2})
,

(b)

H_{2}
meets
\mathrm{tip}^{-}(F_{2})
and some tip of
F_{1}\wedge D_{\mathrm{vert}}
,

(c)

H_{3}
meets
\mathrm{tip}^{-}(F_{1})
and some tip of
F_{2}\wedge D_{\mathrm{vert}}
.

By Lemma
4.1
(b)
we have
\widehat{F}_{i}=[1,(2)_{s_{i}},1]
for some
s_{i}\geqslant 0
. By the definition of
\widehat{F}_{i}
, each tip of
\widehat{F}_{i}
equals
\widehat{L}_{i,j}
for some
j\in\{1,2,3\}
. On the other hand, since
\widehat{F}_{i}
has only two tips, for some
j_{i}\in\{1,2,3\}
the curve
\widehat{L}_{i,j_{i}}
is not a tip of
\widehat{F}_{i}
. Now, definition of
\psi
implies that the image of
\widehat{L}_{i,j_{i}}
equals
\overline{V}_{i}
, so
L_{i,j_{i}}=V_{i}
.

Suppose
j_{1}=j_{2}=3
. Then the contraction of
F_{i}-V_{i}
is an isomorphism in a neighborhood of
H_{3}
. The same is true for the contraction of
F_{3}-V_{3}
, since the latter is disjoint from
H_{3}
. Thus
H_{3}^{2}=\overline{H}_{3}^{2}=-1
; a contradiction.

Therefore, interchanging
F_{1}
with
F_{2}
and
H_{1}
with
H_{2}
, if needed, we can assume that
j_{2}=1
, i.e.
V_{1}
meets
H_{2}
. Since
\overline{X}
is log terminal,
D
is a sum of admissible chains and forks, so
\beta_{D}(H_{2})\leqslant 3
. Since
H_{2}
meets both
H_{1}
and
V_{3}+G_{3}
,
D_{0}
is a fork with branching component
H_{2}
, so
L_{2,2}\not\subseteq D
and
\beta_{D}(H_{j})\leqslant 2
for
j=1,3
.

Since
\beta_{D}(H_{1})\leqslant 2
, and
H_{1}
meets
H_{2}
and
V_{3}+G_{3}
, we have
L_{i,1}\not\subseteq D
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
. Similarly, since
\beta_{D}(H_{3})\leqslant 2
and
H_{3}
meets
V_{3}+G_{3}
, we have
L_{i_{0},3}\not\subseteq D
for some
i_{0}\in\{1,2\}
. It follows that the curves
\widehat{L}_{2,2}
,
\widehat{L}_{i_{0},3}
and
\widehat{L}_{i,1}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
are not contained in
\widehat{D}
, so they are tips of
\widehat{F}_{2}
,
\widehat{F}_{i_{0}}
and
\widehat{F}_{i}
, respectively. We order the chain
\widehat{F}_{i}
so that
\widehat{L}_{i,1}=\mathrm{tip}^{+}(\widehat{F}_{i})
. Now since
\widehat{L}_{i,j}\neq\widehat{L}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
for
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
, we get
\widehat{L}_{1,2}=\mathrm{tip}^{-}(\widehat{F}_{1})
,
i_{0}=1
and
\widehat{L}_{1,3}=\mathrm{tip}^{-}(\widehat{F}_{1})
. In particular, for
i\in\{1,2\}
both tips of
\widehat{F}_{i}
are not contained in
\widehat{D}
, hence
F_{i}=\widehat{F}_{i}
.

The remaining curves
L_{i,j}
are equal to
V_{i}
, so they lie in
D_{0}
. Since
D_{0}
is a fork with a branching component
H_{2}
, we get
\beta_{D}(V_{i})\leqslant 2
and thus
V_{i}
is a tip of
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
, as claimed.

∎

Claim

is a fork, whose branching component

and

. Moreover, for

and

.

We have

. Since

, we have
\overline{H}_{2}^{2}-H_{2}^{2}=(s_{2}+2-k_{2})+(2-j_{3})\leqslant s_{2}-k_{2}+3

.

[2]
. In particular,

. We have
