is the second exceptional curve over
p_{b}
. Let
\widetilde{p}
be the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X_{\eta}
induced by the pencil of conics tangent to
{c}
at
p_{1}
,
p_{3}
. As before, the horizontal part of
D_{\eta}+\sum_{j}A_{j}
consists of a
2
-section
L_{2}
and
1
-sections
A_{a}
and
T
.

Suppose that
A_{a}
contains a base point of
\eta_{+}^{-1}
, call it
q
. Let
\vartheta
be the composition of
\eta
with the blowup at
q
, so
\psi=\vartheta\circ\vartheta_{+}
for some vertical swap
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X_{\vartheta},D_{\vartheta})
, cf. Notation
5.7
. If
q\not\in D_{\eta}
then the proper transforms of both
H_{2}
and
A_{a}
are branching in the same connected component of
D_{\vartheta}
, which is impossible. Since
k_{a}=2
, we have
q\not\in H_{2}^{\eta}
, so
q\in G_{a}\cup L_{a}
. Note that
q\not\in G_{3}
because
D_{\vartheta}
has no circular subdivisor. The curve
\vartheta_{+}(H_{2})
is branching in
D_{\vartheta}
, and meets a maximal twig of
D_{\vartheta}
containing
\vartheta_{+}(H_{1})
. The latter twig is of type
[(2)_{5}]
if
a=1
, or of type
[3,2,2]
if
a=3
. The twig meeting
\vartheta_{+}(H_{2})
in the proper transform of
A_{a}
has length
2
. Hence the connected component of
D_{\vartheta}
containing it is a non-admissible fork, a contradiction.

Thus
A_{a}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
, so
(\eta_{+}^{-1})_{*}A_{a}\not\subseteq D
. As in Case
1
, it follows that
\widetilde{p}\circ\eta_{+}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height
3
with
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}=2
. Degenerate fibers of
\widetilde{p}
are supported on

\widetilde{F}_{1}\coloneqq A_{b}+H_{2}^{\eta}+G_{2}+A_{2}=[1^{\{1\}},2^{\{2\}},2,1^{\{2\}}]
;

\widetilde{F}_{2}\coloneqq G_{1}+L+G_{3}=[2,1^{\{2\}},2^{\{1\}}]
, where
L
is the proper transform of the line joining
p_{1}
with
p_{3}
; and

\widetilde{F}_{3}\coloneqq L_{3}+H_{1}^{\eta}+A_{0}+L_{1}=[2^{\{1\}},2^{\{2\}},1,3]
if
a=1
or
[2,2^{\{2\}},1,3^{\{1\}}]
if
a=3
. As before, decorations
{1}
,
{2}
indicate the components meeting the
1
-section
T
and the
2
-section
L_{2}
, respectively. Let
\widetilde{\eta}\colon X_{\eta}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the contraction of
(\widetilde{F}_{1}-H_{2}^{\eta})+(\widetilde{F}_{2}-G_{a})+(\widetilde{F}_{3}-L_{a})+T
. Replacing
p
and
\psi
with
\widetilde{p}\circ\eta_{+}
and
\widetilde{\eta}\circ\eta_{+}
leads back to Case
1
, which ends the proof.∎

5C.
Further restrictions and notation

We proceed with the proof of Theorem
A
under the assumption
0pt(\overline{X})=2
.
Cases
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
and
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
are settled in Lemmas
5.15
and
5.16
, respectively. We follow the same strategy as in Section
4D
. In particular, we use the following notation, analogous to
4.8
.

Notation 5.7

(Reconstructing the minimalization
\psi
, cf. Notation
4.8
)
.

We keep Notation
5.5
. By Lemma
5.6
we have a factorization
\psi=\varphi\circ\varphi_{+}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
, where
\varphi
is as in Example
3.2
or
3.3
. We study factorizations
\psi=\gamma\circ\gamma_{+}
, where
\gamma
is a composition of
\varphi
with some further blowups over
p_{0},\dots,p_{\nu}
; and
\gamma_{+}\colon(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X_{\gamma},D_{\gamma})
is a vertical swap. One such factorization is given by
\psi=\tau\circ\tau_{+}
, where
\tau\colon X_{\tau}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
is the blowup at
all
points listed there (including the underlined ones), see Figures
13
(case
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
) and
3
(case
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
).

For a given factorization
\psi=\gamma\circ\gamma_{+}
as above, we put
H_{j}^{\gamma}=\gamma_{+}(H_{j})
,
j\in\{1,2\}
, and denote by
U_{\gamma}
,
W_{\gamma}
the connected component of
D_{\gamma}
containing
H_{1}^{\gamma}
and
H_{2}^{\gamma}
, respectively. For
i\in\{1,\dots,\nu\}
we put
L_{i}^{\gamma}=\gamma^{-1}_{*}{l}_{i}
, and for
j\in\{0,\dots,\nu\}
we denote by
A_{j}^{\gamma}
the
(-1)
-curve in
\gamma^{-1}(p_{j})
: it is unique by Lemma
5.4
(d)
. For
i\in\{1,\dots,\nu\}
we denote by
G_{i}^{\gamma}
the first exceptional curve over
p_{i}
. We skip the superscript if
\gamma
is clear from the context.

We put
\widecheck{D}\coloneqq D+\sum_{j}A_{j}
, where the sum runs over all
j
such that
A_{j}
meets
D
normally. That is,
j\in\{0,\dots,\nu\}
in all cases except
5.6
(d)
with
p_{1}^{\prime}\not\in\operatorname{Bs}\psi^{-1}
, in which case
j\in\{0,2,3,4\}
because
A_{1}
meets
D
in a node, see Figure
3(a)
. We denote by
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
the combinatorial type of
(X,\widecheck{D})
, see
[
PP24a
, §2F]
for definitions and notation.

As in the case
0pt=3
, we recover the vertical swap
\tau_{+}
by reversing elementary swaps one by one as long as the inequality in Lemma
2.6
holds: this inequality now reads as (
16
). The following result, which is an analogue of Lemma
4.11
, shows that this process is essentially combinatorial, with minor exceptions if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
.

Lemma 5.8

(Reconstructing the minimalization
\psi
step by step, cf. Lemma
4.11
)
.

Let
\psi=\gamma\circ\gamma_{+}
be as in Notation
5.7
. Then the following hold.

(a)

The log surface
(X_{\gamma},D_{\gamma})
is the minimal log resolution of an lt del Pezzo surface of rank one. In particular,

(16)

\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\gamma})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\gamma})>1.

(b)

We have
\operatorname{Bs}\gamma_{+}^{-1}\subseteq\sum_{i=0}^{\nu}A_{i}^{\gamma}
.

(c)

Assume that
\gamma_{+}
has a base point
r\not\in D_{\gamma}
. Then the following hold.

(i)

We have
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
and
p_{1}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Bs}\psi^{-1}
, see Figures
3(b)
,
3(c)
.

(ii)

For any decomposition

\gamma_{+}\colon\hbox to187.05pt{\vbox to16.56pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 93.52466pt\lower-5.18054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

we have
\operatorname{Bs}\vartheta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq D_{\vartheta}
.

(iii)

Assume furthermore that
\overline{X}
has no descendant with elliptic boundary. Then
\nu=3
.

(d)

Put
d=\nu-3+\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon=1
if
\gamma_{+}
has a base point off
D_{\gamma}
(see
(c)
above), and
\varepsilon=0
otherwise. Then the following hold.

(i)

The set
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
has moduli dimension
d
, i.e. it is represented by a universal
\operatorname{Aut}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
-faithful family over a base
B
of dimension
d
(see Section
2E
for definitions). In particular, if
d=0
then
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})=1
, i.e.
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
uniquely determines the isomorphism class of
(X,\widecheck{D})
.
In case
d=1
we have
B\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
.

(ii)

Put
h^{i}=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
. Then
h^{0}=0
and
h^{1}=d
. Moreover,
h^{2}=0
if the corresponding surface
\overline{Y}
from Lemma
5.6
is as in Example
3.2
,
h^{2}=1
if it is as in
3.3
(a)
,
(c)
and
h^{2}=2
if it is as in
3.3
(b)
.

Remark 5.9

(Moduli dimension in Theorem
A
is at most
1
)
.

The moduli dimension
d
in Lemma
5.8
(d)
is at most
2
. It equals
2
if and only if
\nu=4
and
\varepsilon=1
, in which case Lemma
5.8
(c)(c.iii)
shows that
\overline{X}
has a descendant
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
with elliptic boundary. In these cases the
(-1)
-curve
A_{1}
is an elliptic tie, cf. Figure
3(b)
, so
\overline{T}
is cuspidal and
\overline{Y}
is of type
3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
or
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{6}
.
And indeed, case
(d,t)=1
of
[
PP24a
, Theorem E(e)]
shows that the class such surfaces
\overline{X}
, of a fixed singularity type, has moduli dimension
2
.

We conclude that in the setting of Theorem
A
, if
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})>1
then
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
has moduli dimension
1
. More precisely, it is represented by an almost universal family over
\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
.

(a)
As in the proof of Lemma
4.11
, the first statement follows from Lemma
2.7
(b)
, and the inequality (
16
) is a restatement of (
3
).

(b)
This follows from Lemma
5.4
(d)
.

(c)
Let
\eta
be a composition of
\gamma
with a blowup at
r
. In case
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
, looking at Figure
13
we see that
D_{\eta}
is not a sum of admissible chains and forks, contrary to
(a)
. Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. Then a similar analysis of Figure
3
shows that
p_{1}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Bs}\psi^{-1}
, i.e.
D_{\tau}
is as in
3.3
(b)
or
(c)
; and either
r\in A_{1}^{\gamma}
or
\nu=3
. Repeating this analysis after a blowup at
r
, we infer that each further blowup is centered on the boundary, i.e.
(c)(c.ii)
holds. Furthermore, in case
\nu=4
the morphism
\tau_{+}
is a composition of blowups over
r
and, possibly, one blowup over
A_{j}\cap H_{2}
for some
j\in\{2,3,4\}
, so the curve
A_{1}
is an elliptic tie, see Lemma
2.12
, so
(c)(c.iii)
holds. We leave the details, analogous to the proof of Lemma
4.11
(b)
, to the reader.

(d)

Write
\psi=\varphi\circ\varphi_{+}
, where
\varphi_{+}\colon(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(Y,D_{Y})
is a vertical swap onto a log surface as in Example
3.2
or
3.3
, see Lemma
5.6
. Let
\widecheck{D}_{\varphi}=\varphi_{+}(\widecheck{D})
and let
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}_{\varphi}
be the combinatorial type of
(X_{\varphi},\widecheck{D}_{\varphi})
. By Proposition
3.6
(d)

\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}_{\varphi})
is represented by a universal
\operatorname{Aut}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}_{\varphi})
-faithful family of dimension
\nu-3
, where
\operatorname{Aut}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}_{\varphi})
permutes degenerate fibers of the same type. The base of this family is a point if
\nu=3
and
\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
if
\nu=4
.

The only automorphisms of the weighted graph of
\widecheck{D}
are permutations of degenerate fibers, so the morphism
\varphi_{+}\colon(X,\widecheck{D})\longrightarrow(X_{\varphi},\widecheck{D}_{\varphi})
is
\operatorname{Aut}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
-equivariant. If
\varepsilon=0
then
\varphi_{+}
is inner, so
[
PP24a
, Lemma 2.18]
yields
(d)(d.i)
and the equality
h^{i}=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{\varphi}}(-\log D_{\varphi}))
, which together with Proposition
3.6
(f)
implies
(d)(d.ii)
. Assume
\varepsilon=1
, i.e. we are in the setting of part
(c)
. Then
\varphi_{+}
is a composition of inner blowups and a unique outer one, centered at a point of
A^{\circ}\coloneqq A_{1}^{\gamma}\setminus D_{\gamma}
, see Figures
3(b)
,
3(c)
. Every automorphism of the weighted graph of
\widecheck{D}_{\gamma}
fixes the three points
A_{1}^{\gamma}\cap D_{\gamma}
, so the group
\operatorname{Aut}(X_{\gamma},\widecheck{D}_{\gamma})
acts trivially on
A^{\circ}
. Now we infer
(d)
by applying
[
PP24a
, Lemma 2.19(1)]
to this outer blowup and Lemma 2.18 loc. cit. to the remaining inner ones. If
d=1
(so
\nu=3
) we get that
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
is represented by an almost universal family over
A^{\circ}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
.

∎

Recall that we restrict our attention to surfaces which do not have descendants with elliptic boundary, since the latter are classified in
[
PP24a
]
. This additional restriction has the following easy consequence. We prove it now in case
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
and postpone the other case until
the end of this section
.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
\overline{X}
be as in (
14
). Assume furthermore that
\overline{X}
has no descendant with elliptic boundary. Then the connected component
U
of
D
containing
H_{1}
is neither a
(-2)
-chain nor a
(-2)
-fork.

Suppose
U
consists of
(-2)
-curves. Then for every decomposition
\psi=\gamma\circ\gamma_{+}
as in Notation
5.7
,
U_{\gamma}
consists of
(-2)
-curves and
\operatorname{Bs}\gamma_{+}^{-1}\cap U_{\gamma}=\emptyset
. By Lemma
2.12
we need to find an elliptic tie.

Since
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
, Lemma
5.6
shows that
D_{\tau}
is as in Figure
13
. Since
U_{\tau}
consists of
(-2)
-curves,
D_{\tau}
is as in Figure
13(b)
,
13(d)
or
13(f)
. Consider the first two cases. Then
D_{\tau}
has two connected components,
U_{\tau}
and
W_{\tau}
. By Lemma
5.8
(c)(c.i)

\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}\subseteq D_{\tau}
, so in fact
\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}\subseteq W_{\tau}
, and by induction we get
\operatorname{Bs}\gamma_{+}^{-1}\subseteq W_{\gamma}
for any
\gamma
.

Assume
\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}\cap A_{0}^{\tau}=\emptyset
. Then every blowup in the decomposition of
\tau_{+}
is centered over
A_{1}
or away from
W+A_{1}-H_{2}
. Hence
W+A_{1}-H_{2}
blows down to
W^{\tau}+A_{1}^{\tau}-H_{2}^{\tau}
. The latter is a chain
[3,1,2]
or
[1,2,2]
, see Figure
13(b)
or
13(d)
, so blows down to a smooth point. Since
H_{2}^{\tau}
meets this chain in tips, the contraction of
W+A_{1}-H_{2}
maps
H_{2}
to a nodal curve of arithmetic genus one, so
A_{1}
is an elliptic tie.

Assume now that
A_{0}^{\tau}
contains a base point
q
of
\tau_{+}^{-1}
. Let
\gamma
be a composition of
\tau
with a blowup at
q
. In the first case (Figure
13(b)
)
U_{\gamma}
is not admissible, so the second case holds (Figure
13(d)
). The admissibility of
U
implies that
\operatorname{Bs}\gamma_{+}\cap W_{\gamma}\subseteq A_{1}^{\gamma}
. As before, we conclude that
W+A_{1}-L_{1}
blows down to
W^{\gamma}+A_{1}^{\gamma}-L_{1}^{\gamma}=[3,1,2]
, hence to a smooth point. This blowdown maps
L_{1}
to a nodal curve of arithmetic genus
1
. So
A_{1}
an elliptic tie.

Consider the last case (Figure
13(f)
). Since
U
is admissible and consists of
(-2)
-curves, we get
\psi=\tau
. We claim that there is a
(-1)
-curve
A\subseteq X
such that
A\cdot D=A\cdot G_{3}=2
: such
A
will be the required elliptic tie.

For
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
let
G_{j}^{\prime}
be the second exceptional curve over
p_{j}
, and let
L_{ij}
be the proper transform of the line joining
p_{i}
with
p_{j}
. Let
R
be the proper transform of the conic passing through
p_{0},p_{2},p_{3}
and tangent to
{c}
at
p_{1}
. Consider a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
induced by
|2G_{3}^{\prime}+2A_{3}+L_{3}+H_{2}|
. The horizontal part of
D
consists of
1
-sections
L_{1}
and
H_{1}
, and a
2
-section
G_{3}
. Degenerate fibers are supported on
G_{3}^{\prime}+A_{3}+L_{3}+H_{2}=\langle 2;[1],[2],[2]\rangle
, on
G_{1}+L_{13}+L_{2}=[2,1,2]
, on
L_{23}+G_{2}+R=[1,2,1]
and on
A_{0}+A
for some divisor
A
which has no common components with
D
. By Lemma
2.5
(b)
there are no more degenerate fibers, and
A_{0}+A=[1,1]
. Since
A_{0}
meets
L_{1}
and
H_{1}
we get
A\cdot D=A\cdot G_{3}=2
, as needed.

∎

We now focus on the case
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
,
\nu=3
. In Lemma
5.11
below, we use the assumption (
14
) and symmetries of
(X,D)
to impose some additional restrictions of the map
\varphi_{+}^{-1}
which we need to reconstruct.

Lemma 5.11

(Additional blowup in case
5.6
(e)
)
.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
and
\nu=3
, see Figure
3(c)
. Then one can choose the morphism
\psi
as in Notation
5.7
so that
\varphi_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
q_{j}\in A_{j}^{\varphi}
for
j=1,2
.

The proof of Lemma
5.11
is based on the following observation, which will be useful in Section
5E
, too.

Remark 5.12

(Reduction via symmetries of the Fano plane)
.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
and
\nu=3
.
Write
\psi=\alpha\circ\alpha_{+}
, where
\alpha\colon X_{\alpha}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
is a blowup at
p_{0}
and
p_{j},p_{j}^{\prime}
for
j\in\{1,2,3\}
, so
(X_{\varphi},D_{\varphi})\rightsquigarrow(X_{\alpha},D_{\alpha})
is a swap contracting
A_{0}
. Then
X_{\alpha}
is the minimal resolution of a del Pezzo surface of rank 1 and type
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
.
It can be obtained by a blowup
\beta\colon X_{\alpha}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
of all seven
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-rational points on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, see
[
KN22
, Corollary 5.11]
.

The exceptional divisor of
\beta
is a sum of seven disjoint
(-1)
-curves. They are: the
(-1)
-curves
A_{j}^{\alpha}
over
p_{j}
,
j\in\{0,1,2,3\}
, and the proper transform of the lines joining
p_{i}
with
p_{j}
,
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
.
In turn,
D_{\alpha}
is the proper transform of all
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-rational lines.
Put
q_{j}=\beta(A_{j}^{\alpha})\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Note that
q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}
lie on a line
\beta(H_{2})
, and
q_{0}\not\in\beta(H_{2})
.

Choose
i_{0},\overline{i}_{0}\in\{1,2,3\}
, and write
\{i,j,k\}=\{0,1,2,3\}\setminus\{i_{0}\}
,
\{\overline{i},\overline{j},\overline{k}\}=\{0,1,2,3\}\setminus\{\overline{i}_{0}\}
. Since the points
\{q_{i},q_{j},q_{k}\}
and
\{q_{\overline{i}},q_{\overline{j}},q_{\overline{k}}\}
are not collinear, there is a unique element of
\operatorname{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
mapping
(q_{i},q_{j},q_{k})
to
(q_{\overline{i}},q_{\overline{j}},q_{\overline{k}})
; its lift to
(X_{\alpha},D_{\alpha})
maps
(A_{i}^{\alpha},A_{j}^{\alpha},A_{k}^{\alpha})
to
(A_{\overline{i}}^{\alpha},A_{\overline{j}}^{\alpha},A_{\overline{k}}^{\alpha})
. If such an automorphism lifts to some intermediate log surface
(X_{\gamma},D_{\gamma})
introduced in Notation
5.7
, then it maps
(A_{i}^{\gamma},A_{j}^{\gamma},A_{k}^{\gamma})
to
(A_{\overline{i}}^{\gamma},A_{\overline{j}}^{\gamma},A_{\overline{k}}^{\gamma})
, and we denote it simply by
(A_{i},A_{j},A_{k})\mapsto(A_{\overline{i}},A_{\overline{j}},A_{\overline{k}})
.

Example 5.13

(See Figure
15
)
.

Assume that
\gamma
is a composition of

with a blowup at

. Then the log surface

. Looking at its action on the

, to

,

,

, respectively.

.
Example
5.13
: involution
(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2})\mapsto(A_{2},A_{1},A_{0})
from Remark
5.12
.

Example 5.14

(How we use Remark
5.12
)
.

Automorphisms from Remark
5.12
allow us to narrow down the possibilities for the morphism
\gamma_{+}
. To see how this works, let
\gamma
be as in Example
5.13
. Assume that, for some reason, we know that
\operatorname{Bs}\gamma_{+}^{-1}\cap A_{3}^{\gamma}=\emptyset
. Since
U_{\gamma}
is a
(-2)
-chain and, by Lemma
5.10
,
U
is not, we see that
\gamma_{+}^{-1}
has a base point on
A_{0}^{\gamma}
or
A_{2}^{\gamma}
. Thus applying
(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2})\mapsto(A_{2},A_{1},A_{0})
, we can assume that
A_{0}^{\gamma}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\gamma_{+}^{-1}\neq\emptyset
.

Note that in this reduction we have implicitly used the condition
A_{3}^{\gamma}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\gamma_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
. Indeed, our automorphism maps
A_{3}^{\gamma}
away from the base locus of the morphism
\gamma_{+}^{-1}
which we reconstruct. In fact, it maps
A_{3}^{\gamma}
to the
(-1)
-curve over the point
\beta(L_{1})\cap\beta(G_{2})\cap\beta(G_{3})
, i.e. to the proper transform of the line joining
p_{2}
with
p_{3}
.

If
A_{i}^{\varphi}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\varphi_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
for two indices
i\in\{1,2,3\}
then the pencil of conics tangent to
{c}
at both points
p_{i}
pulls back to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height
2
, which is impossible. Thus we can assume that
\varphi_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
r_{i}^{\varphi}\in A_{i}^{\varphi}
for
i=2,3
. Since
D
has no circular subdivisor, we have
r_{i}^{\varphi}\in(H_{2}^{\varphi}\cup L_{i}^{\varphi})
for some
i\in\{2,3\}
, and interchanging
{l}_{2}
with
{l}_{3}
, if needed, we can assume
r_{2}^{\varphi}\in(H_{2}^{\varphi}\cup L_{2}^{\varphi})
.

Let
\sigma\colon X_{\varphi}\longrightarrow X_{\alpha}
be the contraction of
A_{1}
, and let
\alpha_{+}=\sigma\circ\varphi_{+}
, so we have a factorization
\psi=\alpha\circ\alpha_{+}
as in Remark
5.12
. Put
r_{0}=\sigma(A_{1})\in A_{0}^{\alpha}
and
r_{i}=\sigma(r_{i}^{\varphi})\in A_{i}^{\alpha}
for
i=2,3
, so
r_{0},r_{2},r_{3}\in\operatorname{Bs}\alpha_{+}^{-1}
, and
r_{2}\in H_{2}^{\alpha}
or
r_{2}\in L_{2}^{\alpha}
. Define
\iota\in\operatorname{Aut}(X_{\alpha},D_{\alpha})
as
(A_{0},A_{2},A_{3})\mapsto(A_{2},A_{0},A_{1})
if
r_{2}\in H_{2}
or
(A_{0},A_{2},A_{3})\mapsto(A_{1},A_{0},A_{2})
if
r_{2}\in L_{2}
; and let
\widetilde{\alpha}_{+}=\iota^{-1}\circ\alpha_{+}
. Then
\iota(r_{2})=r_{0}
, so
\widetilde{\varphi}_{+}\coloneqq\sigma^{-1}\circ\widetilde{\alpha}_{+}
is regular, and
\iota(r_{i})\in A_{1},\iota(r_{j})\in A_{2}
for
\{i,j\}=\{0,2\}
, so
\widetilde{\varphi}_{+}^{-1}
has base points on
A_{1},A_{2}
. Thus replacing
\psi=\varphi\circ\varphi_{+}
by
\varphi\circ\widetilde{\varphi}_{+}
we get the claim.

∎

Suppose
U
consists of
(-2)
-curves. To get a contradiction, by Lemma
2.12
we need to find an elliptic tie. By Lemma
5.6
we have
\nu=4
or
\nu=3
and
D_{\varphi}
is as in Figure
3
.

If
\nu=4
then since
U
is admissible and consists of
(-2)
-curves, we see from Figure
3
that
\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
and
A_{1}
is an elliptic tie. Thus we can assume
\nu=3
, so
D_{\varphi}
is as in Figure
3(c)
. By Lemma
5.11
we can assume that
\varphi_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
q_{i}\in A_{i}^{\varphi}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. The admissibility of
U
and the fact that
U
consists of
(-2)
-curves imply that
q_{1},q_{2}\in D_{\varphi}-U_{\varphi}
, in particular
q_{2}\in H_{2}^{\varphi}\cup G_{2}^{\varphi}
.

Consider the case
q_{2}\in G_{2}^{\varphi}
. Since
U
consists of
(-2)
-curves we have
q_{1}\in L_{1}^{\varphi}
, and since
U
is admissible we conclude that
\varphi_{+}
is a blowup at
q_{1},q_{2}
and possibly at a point infinitely near to
q_{1}
on the proper transform of
L_{1}
. Denoting by
A
the proper transform of the line joining
p_{2}
with
p_{3}
we see that
A+L_{1}+G_{2}+G_{3}
is a connected component of
D+A
, of type
\langle 1;[2],[3],[k]\rangle
for some
k\in\{4,5\}
, so
A
is an elliptic tie, as needed.

Consider the case
q_{2}\in H_{2}^{\varphi}
. Let
\eta
be a composition of
\varphi
with a blowup at
q_{2}
. Since
U
consists of
(-2)
-curves, all base points of
\eta_{+}^{-1}
, including the infinitely near ones, lie on the proper transforms of
L_{1}^{\eta}+H_{2}^{\eta}
or on the preimage of
A_{1}^{\eta}
. Moreover, the admissibility of
U
implies that if
L_{1}^{\eta}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}\not\subseteq A_{1}^{\eta}
then
H_{2}^{\eta}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}^{\eta}
. Thus
L_{1}+H_{2}+G_{1}+\varphi_{+}^{-1}(A_{1})
is a connected component of
D+A_{1}
which blows down to a fork
\langle 1;[2],[3],[k]\rangle
, where the
(-k)
-curve is the proper transform of
H_{2}
or
L_{1}
. Hence
A_{1}
is an elliptic tie, as needed.

∎

5D.
The list of singularity types: case
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2

We can now state the classification results. Like in Lemma
4.12
, we describe the structure of the chosen witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration by writing the singularity type (i.e. the weighted graph of
D
) as a sum of admissible chains
[a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]
and forks
\langle b;T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\rangle
, and decorating it as explained in Section
2G
, that is: the bold numbers correspond to horizontal components, the underlined one to the
2
-section, and the ones decorated by
|j|
to the components meeting the vertical
(-1)
-curve
A_{j}
, see Notation
5.7
. The list of singularity types without decorations is given in Tables
4
–
7
. We begin with case
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
, summarized in the second part of Table
4
.

Lemma 5.15

(Classification, case
0pt=2
,
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
, see Table
4
)
.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
. Let
\mathcal{S}
be a singularity type of a log terminal surface. Let
\overline{X}
be a del Pezzo surface of rank
1
, height
3
, width
2
and singularity type
\mathcal{S}
. Assume
\overline{X}
has no descendant with elliptic boundary. Then
\mathcal{S}
is as below, and the following hold.

(
1
)

We have
\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})=1
, i.e.
\overline{X}
is unique up to an isomorphism.

(
2
)

The minimal log resolution
(X,D)
of
\overline{X}
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p
such that
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to one of the canonical surfaces
\overline{Y}
from Example
3.2
, and the combinatorial type of
(X,D,p)
is one of the following.

(a)

\overline{Y}
is of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{7}+[3]
, see Example
3.2
(a)
, case
5.6
(a)
holds, and
(X,D,p)
is

(1)

[2,2^{|3|},2,\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2^{|2|},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|1,3|},2^{|2|}]+[3^{|0|},2^{|1|},2]
,

(b)

\overline{Y}
is of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
, see Example
3.2
(b)
, case
5.6
(b)
holds, and
(X,D,p)
is

(2)

[2,2^{|2|},2,\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},3^{|3|}]+\langle\uline{\boldsymbol{2}}^{|2|},[2]^{|1|},[2,2]^{|3|},[3]^{|0,1|}\rangle
,

(c)

\overline{Y}
is of type
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
, see Example
3.2
(c)
, case
5.6
(c)
holds, and
(X,D,p)
is one of the following:

(3)

[2,3^{|2|},2,\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2,2^{|3|},2]+[2^{|2|},\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}^{|1,3|},2^{|0|},2^{|1|}]
,

(4)

[2^{|2|},3,\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2,2^{|3|},2]+\prescript{|1|}{}{[}2,2^{|0|},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|1,3|},3^{|2|},2]
,

(5)

[3^{|2|},\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2,2^{|3|},2]+\prescript{|1|}{}{[}(2)_{k-2},2^{|0|},\uline{\boldsymbol{k}}^{|1,3|},2^{|2|},2]
,
k\in\{3,4\}
,

(6)

[2^{|1|},2^{|0|},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|1,3|},2^{|2|},2,\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2,2^{|3|},2]+[3]^{|2|}
,

(7)

[2,k^{|3|},2,\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2^{|2|}]+\prescript{|3|}{}{[}(2)_{k-2},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|1,2|},2^{|0|},2]^{|1|}+[2]^{|2|}
,
k\geqslant 3
,

(8)

[2,k^{|3|},2,\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2^{|2|}]+\prescript{|3|}{}{[}(2)_{k-2},\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}^{|1,2|},2^{|0|},2,2]^{|1|}+[2]^{|2|}
,
k\in\{3,4\}
,

(9)

[2,3^{|3|},2,\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2^{|2|}]+\prescript{|3|}{}{[}2,\uline{\boldsymbol{5}}^{|1,2|},2^{|0|},2,2,2]^{|1|}+[2]^{|2|}
,

(10)

[2^{|3|},3,\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2^{|2|}]+[2,3^{|3|},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|1,2|},2^{|0|},2,2^{|1|}]+[2]^{|2|}
,

(11)

[3^{|3|},\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2^{|2|}]+[2,2^{|3|},\uline{\boldsymbol{k}}^{|1,2|},2^{|0|},(2)_{k-1}]^{|1|}+[2]^{|2|}
,
k\in\{3,4\}
,

(12)

[2^{|1|},2,2^{|0|},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|1,2|},2^{|3|},2,\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2^{|2|}]+[3]^{|3|}+[2]^{|2|}
,

(13)

[2^{|1|},2^{|0|},\uline{\boldsymbol{2}}^{|1,2|},k^{|3|},2,\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2^{|2|}]+[3,(2)_{k-2}]^{|3|}+[2]^{|2|}
,
k\geqslant 3
,

(14)

[2^{|2|},\boldsymbol{k}^{|0|},2,2^{|3|},2]+\langle 2;\prescript{|0|}{}{[}(2)_{k-2}],[2]^{|1|},[\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]^{|1,2,3|}\rangle+[2]^{|2|}
,
k\in\{3,4\}
,

(15)

\langle 2;[2],[3]^{|3|},[2^{|2|},\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2]\rangle+[2^{|3|},\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}^{|1,2|},2^{|0|},2^{|1|}]+[2]^{|2|}
,

(16)

\langle k;[2],\prescript{|3|}{}{T},[2^{|2|},\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2]\rangle+\prescript{|3|}{}{T}^{*}*[(2)_{k-2},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|1,2|},2^{|0|},2^{|1|}]+[2]^{|2|}
,
k\geqslant 3
,
d(T)\leqslant 3
.

As in the proof of Lemma
4.12
, we first prove that
(2)
implies
(1)
. Fix a singularity type
\mathcal{S}
as in the above list. By Lemma
2.13
no surface
\overline{X}\in\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})
has a descendant with elliptic boundary, so by
(2)
its minimal log resolution
(X,D)
has a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p
as above. Since
\mathcal{S}
appears exactly once on the above list, it uniquely determines the combinatorial type of
(X,D,p)
. The latter uniquely determines the isomorphism class of
\overline{X}
by Lemma
5.8
(d)(d.i)
, case
\nu=3
,
\varepsilon=0
. Thus
\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})\leqslant 1
. To see that the equality holds we note that a triple
(X,D,p)
of each combinatorial type listed above exists and satisfies inequality (
3
), so
(X,D)
is indeed the minimal log resolution of a del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}
of rank 1. Since
\mathcal{S}
does not appear in the classification of del Pezzo surfaces of rank 1 and height at most
2
in
[
PP24a
]
, we have
0pt(\overline{X})=3
, and since
\mathcal{S}
does not appear in Lemma
4.12
, either, we get
0pt(\overline{X})=2
, so
\overline{X}\in\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})
, as needed.

Thus it remains to prove
(2)
, i.e. to choose
p
such that one of the above cases
(a)
–
(c)
hold. We keep Notation
5.7
, and consider each case of Lemma
5.6
separately.

Case
5.6
(a)
, see Figure
13(a)
.
If
\psi=\tau
then
D
is as in
(1)
. Suppose
\psi\neq\tau
, and let
\eta
be a composition of
\tau
with a blowup at some
r\in\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}
. By Lemma
5.8
(b)
,
(c)
, we have
r\in D_{\tau}\cap A_{j}^{\tau}
for some
j\in\{0,\dots,3\}
. Recall from Notation
5.7
that
U_{\eta}
denotes the connected component of
D_{\eta}
containing
H_{1}^{\eta}
. Like all connected components of
D_{\eta}
, it is an admissible chain or an admissible fork, see Lemma
5.8
(a)
.

Suppose
r\in A_{0}
. We have
r\in H_{1}^{\tau}
, since otherwise
U_{\eta}
would be a non-admissible fork
\langle 2,[2],[3,2,2],[2,2,2]\rangle
. Now
U_{\eta}=[2,2,2,\boldsymbol{3},2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2]
, where, as usual, the bold numbers correspond to the horizontal components of
D_{\eta}
; and the underlined one corresponds to
H_{2}^{\eta}
. We compute
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\tfrac{11}{13}<1
, contrary to (
16
).

Suppose
r\in A_{2}
. Like before, the admissibility of
U_{\eta}
implies that
r\in L_{3}^{\tau}
. Now
U_{\eta}=[2,2,2,\boldsymbol{2},3,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\tfrac{25}{31}<1
, which again contradicts (
16
).

Thus
r\in A_{1}
or
r\in A_{3}
. It is sufficient to show that inequality (
16
) fails in the first case. Indeed, in case
r\in A_{3}
the weighted graph of
U_{\eta}
is obtained from the one in case
r\in A_{1}
either by attaching a vertex corresponding to a vertical curve, or by increasing a weight of such a vertex. In any case, by Lemma
2.4
, the log discrepancy of
H_{j}^{\eta}
in case
r\in A_{3}
does not exceed the corresponding one in case
r\in A_{1}
. Therefore, assume
r\in A_{1}
. Now
U_{\eta}=[2,2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2]
if
r\in H_{2}^{\tau}
and
U_{\eta}=\langle\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},[2],[2],[2,2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle
otherwise. The number
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})
equals
1
or
\tfrac{5}{7}<1
, so in both cases we get a contradiction with (
16
), as needed.

Case
5.6
(b)
with
q\in H_{2}
, see Figure
13(b)
.
For
j\in\{2,3\}
write
\{r_{j}\}=A_{j}^{\tau}\cap H_{2}^{\tau}
. If both
r_{2}
and
r_{3}
are base points of
\tau_{+}^{-1}
then
U
has two branching components, which is impossible. Hence interchanging
{l}_{2}
with
{l}_{3}
if necessary, we can assume
r_{3}\not\in\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}
.

Suppose
\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}\subseteq\{r_{2}\}\cup A_{1}
. If
r_{2}\not\in\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}
then
U
is a
(-2)
-chain, contrary to Lemma
5.10
. If
r_{2}\in\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}
then
U
is a fork, and the twig of
U
containing
H_{1}
has length
5
, so its remaining twigs are of type
[2]
. It follows that
\tau_{+}^{-1}
has no base points infinitely near to
r_{2}
, so
U
is a
(-2)
-fork, again contrary to Lemma
5.10
.

Thus
\tau_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
r\neq r_{2}
,
r\not\in A_{1}
. Let
\eta
be a composition of
\tau
with a blowup at
r
. Suppose
r\in A_{3}
. Then by assumption
r\neq r_{3}
, so
r\in U_{\tau}
and
D_{\eta}=[2,3,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]+\langle\uline{\boldsymbol{3}};[3],[2],[2]\rangle
, hence
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=1
; a contradiction with (
16
). If
r\in A_{2}
then by assumption
r\neq r_{2}
, so
r_{2}\not\in\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}
, and interchanging
{l}_{2}
with
{l}_{3}
we get back the case
r\in A_{3}
, excluded above. Thus
r\in A_{0}
. If
r\in L_{1}
then
\beta_{U_{\eta}}(H_{1})=3
, and
H_{1}
meets a twig
T_{j}\subseteq F_{j}
with
\#T_{j}=3
for
j\in\{2,3\}
, contrary to Lemma
2.2
. Therefore,
\{r\}=H_{1}\cap A_{0}
, so
D_{\eta}=[(2)_{3},\boldsymbol{3},(2)_{3}]+[2,3,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2]
, and again
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=1
; a contradiction with (
16
).

Case
5.6
(b)
with
q\in L_{3}
, see Figure
13(c)
.
If
\psi=\tau
then
D
is as in
(2)
. Suppose
\psi\neq\tau
, and let
\eta
be a composition of
\tau
with a blowup at some
r\in\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}
. Since
W_{\eta}
is an admissible fork, we have
r\not\in A_{1}
, and
r\in W_{\tau}
or
\{r\}=A_{0}\cap H_{1}
. In the latter case,
D_{\eta}=[2,2,2,\boldsymbol{3},3]+\langle\uline{\boldsymbol{2}},[2],[2,2],[2,3]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\frac{137}{299}<1
, a contradiction with (
16
). Thus
r\in W_{\tau}\setminus A_{1}
. Now
D_{\eta}
is
\langle\boldsymbol{2};[2],[2,2,2],[3]\rangle+\langle\uline{\boldsymbol{2}},[2],[4],[2,2]\rangle
if
r\in A_{0}
;
\langle 2;[2],[2],[3,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle+\langle\uline{\boldsymbol{3}};[2],[3],[2,2]\rangle
if
r\in A_{2}
, and
[2,2,2,\boldsymbol{2},3,2]+\langle\uline{\boldsymbol{2}};[2],[3],[2,3]\rangle
if
r\in A_{3}
. Thus
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\tfrac{17}{35}
,
\tfrac{13}{18}
or
\tfrac{241}{391}
, respectively, so in each case we get a contradiction with inequality (
16
).

Case
5.6
(c)
with
q\in H_{2}
, see Figure
13(d)
.
By Lemma
5.10

U
is not a sum of
(-2)
-curves, so
\tau_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
r\not\in A_{1}^{\tau}
. By Lemma
5.8
(b)
,
(c)

r\in A_{j}^{\tau}\cap D_{\tau}
for some
j\in\{0,2,3\}
. Let
\eta
be a composition of
\tau
with a blowup at some
r
; and for a base point
s
of
\eta_{+}^{-1}
let
\upsilon
be a composition of
\eta
with a blowup at
s
.

Case 1:

r\in A_{0}^{\tau}
.
Suppose
\{r\}=A_{0}\cap L_{1}
. Then
U_{\eta}
is a
(-2)
-fork
\mathrm{D}_{6}
. Since
U
is not a
(-2)
-fork by Lemma
5.10
, the map
\eta_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
s\not\in A_{1}^{\eta}
. After a blowup at
s
, the fork
U_{\upsilon}
must still be admissible, so
s\in A_{0}
or
\{s\}=A_{j}\cap U_{\eta}
for some
j\in\{2,3\}
. If
\{s\}=A_{0}\cap L_{1}
then
U_{\upsilon}
is still a
(-2)
-fork, now of type
\mathrm{E}_{7}
, and after one further blowup it becomes non-admissible, a contradiction. Thus
s\in U_{\eta}
. If
s\in A_{3}
then
D_{\upsilon}=\langle\boldsymbol{2};[2],[2],[2,3,2]\rangle+[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},3,2]+[2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=1
, contrary to (
16
). Hence
s\in A_{0}\cup A_{2}
; so
U_{\upsilon}=\langle\boldsymbol{2},[2],[3],[2,2,2]\rangle
,
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\upsilon})=\tfrac{1}{5}
and therefore
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})>\tfrac{2}{5}
by (
16
). But
W_{\upsilon}=\langle 3;[2],[2],[\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\rangle
if
s\in A_{0}
and
[2,3,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2,2]
if
s\in A_{2}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=\tfrac{2}{5}
or
\tfrac{8}{29}<\tfrac{2}{5}
; a contradiction.

Therefore,
\{r\}=A_{0}\cap H_{1}
. Suppose
\eta_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
s\not\in A_{0}^{\tau}
. Consider the case
s\in A_{1}
. Then
U_{\upsilon}=[2,\boldsymbol{3},2,2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\upsilon})=\frac{3}{7}
and therefore
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})>\frac{2}{7}
by (
16
). But
W_{\upsilon}=\langle 2,[2],[2,2],[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}]\rangle
if
s\in H_{2}
or
\langle 2;[2],[3],[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\rangle
otherwise, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=\frac{2}{7}
or
\frac{5}{23}<\frac{2}{7}
, a contradiction. If
s\in A_{2}
then
D_{\upsilon}
equals
[(2)_{3},\boldsymbol{3},(2)_{3}]+\langle 2;[2],[2],[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}]\rangle
if
s\in H_{2}
,

[3,\boldsymbol{3},2,2,2]+\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\rangle
if
s\in L_{2}
,
and
\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,2,2,\boldsymbol{3},2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\rangle+[3]
otherwise,
so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\upsilon})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=1
,

\tfrac{37}{46}
or

\tfrac{1}{4}
; a contradiction with (
16
). Eventually, in case
s\in A_{3}
we have

D_{\upsilon}=\langle 2,[2],[2],[2,\boldsymbol{3},2]\rangle+\langle 2;[2],[2],[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}]\rangle+[2]
if
s\in H_{2}
and

D_{\upsilon}=[2,\boldsymbol{3},2,3,2]+\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\rangle+[2]
otherwise,
so in either case
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\upsilon})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=1
; again a contradiction with (
16
).

We conclude that
\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{0}
. Let
\vartheta
be a composition of
\tau
with some
k-2\geqslant 1
blowups at
r
and its infinitely near points on the proper transforms of
H_{1}
, so that
A_{0}^{\vartheta}\cap H_{1}^{\vartheta}
is not a base point of
\vartheta_{+}^{-1}
, i.e.
\operatorname{Bs}\vartheta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{0}^{\vartheta}\cap W_{\vartheta}
. We have
U_{\vartheta}=[2,\boldsymbol{k},2,2,2]
and
W_{\vartheta}=\langle 2;[2],[(2)_{k-2}],[\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\rangle
. If
\psi=\vartheta
then inequality (
16
) gives
k\leqslant 4
, so
D
is as in
(14)
. Suppose
\psi\neq\vartheta
. Then by assumption
A_{0}^{\vartheta}\cap W_{\vartheta}
is a base point of
\vartheta_{+}^{-1}
. Let
\gamma
be a composition of
\vartheta
with a blowup at that point. Then
D_{\gamma}=\langle\boldsymbol{k};[2],[2],[2,2,2]\rangle+\langle 2,[2],[3,(2)_{k-3}],[\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\rangle+[2]
, a contradiction with
(
16
).

Case 2:

r\in A_{2}^{\tau}
and
A_{0}^{\tau}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
.
We have three cases:
r\in H_{2}
,
r\in L_{2}
and
r\in G_{2}
.

Subcase 2.1:

\{r\}=A_{2}^{\tau}\cap H_{2}
.
We have
U_{\eta}=[(2)_{3},\boldsymbol{2},(2)_{3}]
, so by Lemma
5.10
,
\eta_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
s\not\in A_{1}^{\eta}
. Assumption
A_{0}^{\tau}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
implies that
s\in A_{2}\cup A_{3}
. Interchanging
{l}_{2}
with
{l}_{3}
if necessary, we can assume
s\in A_{2}
. Suppose
\{s\}=A_{2}\cap H_{2}
. Then after a blowup at
s
,
U_{\upsilon}
is a
(-2)
-fork, so by Lemma
5.10
,
\upsilon_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
z\in A_{2}\cup A_{3}
. Let
\vartheta
be a composition of
\upsilon
with a blowup at
z
. Since
U_{\vartheta}
is an admissible fork, we have
\{z\}=A_{3}\cap U_{\upsilon}
. Now
D_{\vartheta}=\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,3,2,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle+[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\vartheta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})=\tfrac{53}{69}
, contrary to (
16
).

Thus
\{s\}=A_{2}\cap U_{\eta}
. If
\psi=\upsilon
then
D
is as in
(3)
. Assume that
\psi\neq\upsilon
, and let
\vartheta
be a composition of
\upsilon
with a blowup at
z\in\operatorname{Bs}\upsilon_{+}^{-1}
. Suppose
z\in A_{1}
. Then
U_{\vartheta}=[2,3,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\vartheta})=\tfrac{3}{5}
and thus
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})>\tfrac{1}{5}
by (
16
). But
W_{\vartheta}=[2,2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},2]
if
z\in H_{2}
and
\langle\uline{\boldsymbol{4}};[2],[2],[3,2]\rangle
otherwise, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})=\tfrac{1}{5}
or
\tfrac{1}{12}
; a contradiction.

Thus
z\in A_{2}\cup A_{3}
. If
z\in A_{2}
then
D_{\vartheta}=[2,4,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]+[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2]
if
z\in U_{\upsilon}
and

\langle 3;[2],[2],[2,2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle+[3,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2]
otherwise, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\vartheta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})=1
or
\tfrac{55}{63}
; contrary to (
16
). Hence
z\in A_{3}
. If
z\in H_{2}
then interchanging
{l}_{2}
with
{l}_{3}
we get back the case
\{s\}=A_{2}\cap H_{2}
, considered above, so we can assume
\{z\}=A_{3}\cap U_{\upsilon}
. Now
D_{\vartheta}=[2,3,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,3,2]+\langle\uline{\boldsymbol{4}};[2],[2],[2,2]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\vartheta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})=\tfrac{13}{21}
; a contradiction with (
16
).

Subcase 2.2:

\{r\}=A_{2}^{\tau}\cap L_{2}
.
Assume first that
\eta_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
s\not\in A_{1}^{\eta}
. Suppose
s\in A_{3}
. Then
D_{\upsilon}=\langle 2;[2],[2],[3,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle+[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2]
if
s\in H_{2}
and
[3,\boldsymbol{2},2,3,2]+\langle\uline{\boldsymbol{3}};[2],[2,2],[2,2]\rangle
otherwise, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\upsilon})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=1
or
\tfrac{127}{175}
; a contradiction with (
16
). Thus
s\in A_{2}
,
\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}\cap A_{3}^{\eta}=\emptyset
. If
s\in L_{2}
then
D_{\upsilon}=[4,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]+\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\upsilon})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=1
, contrary to (
16
). Hence
s\in W_{\eta}
, and
D_{\upsilon}=[2,3,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]+[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},3,2]
. If
\psi=\upsilon
then
D
is as in
(4)
. Assume
\psi\neq\upsilon
, and let
\vartheta
be a composition of
\upsilon
with a blowup at some
z\in\operatorname{Bs}\upsilon_{+}^{-1}
. If
z\in A_{1}
then the admissibility of
W_{\vartheta}
implies that
z\in H_{2}
, so
D_{\vartheta}=[2,3,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]+[2,2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},3,2]
and
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\vartheta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})=\tfrac{273}{323}
, contrary to (
16
). Thus
z\in A_{2}
, so
D_{\vartheta}=[3,3,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]+\langle 3;[2],[2],[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\rangle
if
z\in U_{\upsilon}
and

[2,2,3,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]+[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},4,2]
otherwise. We get
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\tfrac{61}{77}
or
\tfrac{933}{989}
, contrary to (
16
).

It remains to consider the case
\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
. Then
U=[3,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})=\frac{7}{11}
, and therefore
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\frac{2}{11}
by (
16
). Let
\vartheta
be a composition of
\eta
with some
k-3\geqslant 0
blowups at
A_{1}^{\eta}\cap H_{2}^{\eta}
and its infinitely near points on the proper transforms of
H_{2}^{\eta}
, so that
A_{1}^{\vartheta}\cap H_{2}^{\vartheta}\not\subseteq\operatorname{Bs}\vartheta_{+}^{-1}
. Then
W_{\vartheta}=[(2)_{k-1},\uline{\boldsymbol{k}},2,2]
, and the inequality
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\frac{2}{11}
gives
k\leqslant 4
. If
\psi=\vartheta
then
D
is as in
(5)
, otherwise after one further blowup we get
W=\langle\boldsymbol{k};[2],[2,2],[3,(2)_{k-2}]\rangle
, so
k=3
since
W
is admissible, and
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=\frac{1}{37}<\frac{2}{11}
; a contradiction.

Subcase 2.3:

\{r\}=A_{2}^{\tau}\cap G_{2}
.
We have
U_{\eta}=[2,2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2,2]
. If
\psi=\eta
then
D
is as in
(6)
. Suppose
\psi\neq\eta
. Since
A_{0}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
and
U_{\upsilon}
is admissible, we get

\{s\}=A_{1}\cap H_{2}
,

A_{2}\cap U_{\eta}
or

A_{3}\cap H_{2}
. Now
U_{\upsilon}=[2,2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2,2]
,

[2,2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,3,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2,2]
or

\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\upsilon})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=\tfrac{57}{67}
,
\tfrac{65}{73}
or
\tfrac{3}{7}<1
; a contradiction with inequality (
16
).

Case 3:

r\in A_{3}^{\tau}
and
(A_{0}^{\tau}\cup A_{2}^{\tau})\cap\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
.
Consider the case
r\in H_{2}
. Then
U_{\eta}
is a
(-2)
-fork. By Lemma
5.10
,
\eta_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
s\not\in A_{1}
, so
s\in A_{3}
by assumption.
If
s\in H_{2}
then
U_{\upsilon}
is still a
(-2)
-fork, so as before,
A_{3}^{\upsilon}
contains a base point of
\upsilon_{+}^{-1}
, and after blowing up at this point we get a non-admissible
U
, which is impossible. Thus
\{s\}=A_{3}\cap U_{\eta}
, so
U_{\upsilon}=\langle 2;[2],[3],[2,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle
. In particular,
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\upsilon})=\frac{3}{5}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\frac{1}{5}
by (
16
). Like before, the admissibility of
U
yields
\operatorname{Bs}\upsilon_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
. If
\psi=\upsilon
then
D
is as in
(15)
, otherwise after one further blowup
W
becomes
[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},2,2,2]
or
\langle\uline{\boldsymbol{4}};[2],[2],[3,2]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=\frac{1}{5}
or
\frac{1}{12}<\frac{1}{5}
, a contradiction.

Consider the case
r\in U_{\tau}
. Replace
\eta
with its composition with some
l-3\geqslant 0
blowups over
A_{1}\cap H_{2}
, on the proper transforms of
H_{2}
, so that
A_{1}^{\eta}\cap H_{2}^{\eta}\not\subseteq\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}
. Then
D_{\eta}=[2,3,2,\boldsymbol{2},2]+[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{l}},(2)_{l-1}]+[2]
. If
A_{1}
contains a base point of
\eta_{+}^{-1}
, say
s
, then
\{s\}=A_{1}\cap(D_{\tau}-H_{2})
, so
D_{\upsilon}=[2,3,2,\boldsymbol{2},2]+\langle\uline{\boldsymbol{l}};[2],[2],[3,(2)_{l-2}]\rangle+[2]
, contrary to (
16
). Thus
\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{3}
. Again, replace
\eta_{+}
with
k-2\geqslant 1
blowups over
r
, on the proper transforms of
U_{\tau}
, so
D_{\eta}=[2,k,2,\boldsymbol{2},2]+[(2)_{k-2},\uline{\boldsymbol{l}},(2)_{l-1}]+[2]
for some
k,l\geqslant 3
. If
\psi=\eta
then inequality (
16
) gives
l=3
or
l=4
,
k\in\{3,4\}
or
(k,l)=(3,5)
, so
D
is as in
(7)
–
(9)
. Assume
\psi\neq\eta
. Then the base point of
\eta_{+}^{-1}
is
\{s\}=A_{3}\cap W_{\eta}
, and after blowing up at
s
we get
D_{\upsilon}=\langle k;[2],[2],[2,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle+[3,(2)_{k-3},\uline{\boldsymbol{l}},(2)_{l-1}]+[2]
. Inequality (
16
) gives
l=3
. By assumption, we have
\operatorname{Bs}\upsilon_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{3}
, so the admissibility of
U
implies that
D
is as in
(16)
.

Case
5.6
(c)
with
q\in L_{3}
, see Figure
13(e)
.
Assume first that
\tau_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
r\in A_{2}
. The admissibility of
W
implies that
r\in H_{2}
. Now interchanging
{l}_{2}
with
{l}_{3}
we are back in case
5.6
(c)
with
q\in H_{2}
, considered above. Thus we can assume
A_{2}^{\tau}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
.

Assume
A_{1}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
. Using a similar argument as in the proof of Lemma
5.6
, we will now find another witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration which is again as in the previous case
q\in H_{2}
.

Recall that we assume
(A_{1}\cup A_{2})\cap\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
, so
A_{1}^{\tau},A_{2}^{\tau}\not\subseteq\tau_{+}(D)
. For
j\in\{1,2,3\}
let
G_{j}
,
G_{j}^{\prime}
be the first and second exceptional curve over
p_{j}
. Let
{l}_{12}
,
{l}_{1}^{\prime}
be the line joining
p_{1}
with
p_{2}
and the line tangent to
{c}
at
p_{1}
; and let
L_{12}=\tau^{-1}_{*}{l}_{12}
,
L_{1}^{\prime}=\tau^{-1}_{*}{l}_{1}^{\prime}
. The pencil of conics tangent to
{c}
at
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
\widetilde{p}\colon X_{\tau}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
such that
\tau_{+}(D)_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of a
2
-section
L_{3}
and
1
-section
L_{1}
, which are disjoint. Its degenerate fibers are supported on
\widetilde{F}_{1}\coloneqq A_{0}+H_{1}+L_{2}+L_{1}^{\prime}=[1^{\{1\}},2^{\{2\}},2,1^{\{2\}}]
,
\widetilde{F}_{2}\coloneqq G_{1}+L_{12}+G_{2}=[2^{\{1\}},1^{\{2\}},2]
and
\widetilde{F}_{3}\coloneqq G_{3}^{\prime}+G_{3}+H_{2}+A_{3}=\langle 2;[2],[2^{\{1\}}],[1^{\{2\}}]\rangle
, where the numbers decorated by
{1}
and
{2}
refer to the components meting the
1
-section
L_{1}
and the
2
-section
L_{3}
, respectively. Let
\widetilde{\tau}\colon X_{\tau}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the contraction of
(\widetilde{F}_{1}-H_{1})+(\widetilde{F}_{2}-G_{1})+(\widetilde{F}_{3}-H_{2})+L_{1}
. Replacing
p
and
\psi
with
\widetilde{p}\circ\tau_{+}
and
\widetilde{\tau}\circ\tau_{+}
we are back in case
q\in H_{2}
.

Thus we can assume that
\tau_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
r\in A_{1}
. As before, denote by
\eta
the composition of
\tau
with a blowup at
r
, and for a given base point
s
of
\eta_{+}^{-1}
, denote by
\upsilon
the composition of
\eta
with a blowup at
s
.

We have
\{r\}=A_{1}\cap H_{2}
, since otherwise
D_{\eta}=[3,\boldsymbol{2},2]+\langle\uline{\boldsymbol{2}};[2],[2,2],[3,2]\rangle+[2]
and
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=1
, contrary to (
16
). Suppose
\eta_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
s\in A_{0}
. Since
W_{\upsilon}
is admissible, we get
s\in L_{1}
and
D_{\upsilon}=\langle\boldsymbol{2};[2],[2],[3]\rangle+[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},3,2,2]
, so again
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\upsilon})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=1
, contrary to (
16
). Therefore,
\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}\cup A_{3}
.

Assume that
\eta_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
s\in A_{3}
. If
s\in L_{3}
then
D_{\upsilon}=[4,\boldsymbol{2},2]+\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\rangle+[2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\upsilon})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=1
, contrary to (
16
). Thus
\{s\}=A_{3}\cap W_{\eta}
. If
\psi=\upsilon
then
D
is as in
(10)
. Suppose
\psi\neq\upsilon
, and let
\vartheta
be a composition of
\upsilon
with a blowup at some
z\in\operatorname{Bs}\upsilon_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}\cup A_{3}
. If
z\in A_{1}
then
z\in H_{2}
since
W_{\vartheta}
is admissible, so
D_{\vartheta}=[2,3,\boldsymbol{2},2]+[2,3,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2,2,2]+[2]
and
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\vartheta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})=\tfrac{71}{77}
, contrary to (
16
). Thus
z\in A_{3}
. We get
D_{\vartheta}=[3,3,\boldsymbol{2},2]+\langle 3;[2],[2],[2,2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\rangle+[2]
if
z\in U_{\upsilon}
and
[2,2,3,\boldsymbol{2},2]+[2,4,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2,2,2]+[2]
if
z\in W_{\upsilon}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\vartheta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})=\tfrac{53}{63}
or
1
; contrary to (
16
).

It remains to consider the case
\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
. Replace
\eta
with
k-2\geqslant 1
blowups over
r
, on the proper transforms of
H_{2}
, so that
A_{1}\cap H_{2}\not\subseteq\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}
. Then
D_{\eta}=[3,\boldsymbol{2},2]+[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{k}},(2)_{k}]+[2]
. If
\psi\neq\eta
then by construction
\{s\}=A_{1}\cap(D_{\eta}-H_{2})
is a base point of
\eta_{+}^{-1}
, and the resulting fork
W_{\upsilon}
is not admissible, which is impossible. Thus
\psi=\eta
. Inequality (
16
) gives
k\leqslant 4
, so
D
is as in
(11)
.

Case
5.6
(c)
with
q\in G_{3}
, see Figure
13(f)
.
Since
U_{\tau}
is a
(-2)
-chain, by Lemma
5.10
we have
\psi\neq\tau
. Let
\eta
be a composition of
\tau
with a blowup at
r\in\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}
.

We can assume that
A_{2}^{\tau}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
. Indeed, if
r\in A_{2}^{\tau}
then since
D
has no circular subdivisor, we have
r\in H_{2}
or
r\in L_{2}
, so interchanging
{l}_{2}
with
{l}_{3}
we are back in the case
q\in H_{2}
or
q\in L_{3}
considered above.

Suppose
r\in A_{0}
. Then
U_{\eta}=\langle\boldsymbol{2};[2],[2],[2,3,\uline{\boldsymbol{2}},2,2]\rangle
if
r\in L_{1}
and
\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,\boldsymbol{3},2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{2}}]\rangle
if
r\in H_{1}
. In both cases, we have
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=1
; a contradiction with (
16
). Thus
\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}\cup A_{3}
.

Assume
r\in A_{1}
, so
r\in H_{2}
as
U
is admissible. If
\psi=\eta
then
D
is as in
(12)
. Suppose
\psi\neq\eta
, and let
\upsilon
be a composition of
\eta
with a blowup at
s\in\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}\cup A_{3}
. Since
U_{\upsilon}
is admissible, we have

\{s\}=A_{1}\cap H_{2}
or
A_{3}\cap U_{\eta}
, so

U_{\upsilon}=[(2)_{4},\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2]
or
[(2)_{3},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},3,2,\boldsymbol{2},2]
, hence
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\upsilon})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=1
, a contradiction with (
16
).

It remains to consider the case
\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{3}
. The admissibility of
U
implies that
\tau_{+}
is a composition of
k-2\geqslant 1
blowups on the proper transforms of
U_{\eta}
; hence
D
is as in
(13)
.

∎

5E.
The list of singularity types: case
\operatorname{char}{k}=2

We now turn to the case
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. Here we need to reconstruct
(X,D)
from log surfaces
(Y,D_{Y})
listed in Example
3.3
, see Lemma
5.6
(d)
,
(e)
. The result is summarized in Lemma
5.16
.
As in Lemmas
4.12
and
5.15
, we list all singularity types together with decorations which encode the structure of the chosen witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration, as explained in Section
2G
. Namely, the numbers decorated by
|j|
correspond to components of
D
meeting the vertical
(-1)
-curve
A_{j}
, the bold ones correspond to the horizontal components, and the underlined one to the
2
-section. Singularity types without decorations are listed in Tables
6
–
7
. Recall from Notation
2.14
that
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})
denotes the set of isomorphism classes of del Pezzo surfaces of rank one, height 3, and width 2.

We would like to reassure the reader that, although the resulting list turns out to be very long, the proof follows exactly the same lines as the proofs of Lemmas
5.15
and
4.12
above.

Lemma 5.16

(Classification in case
0pt=2
,
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
)
.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. Let
\mathcal{S}
be a singularity type of a log terminal surface. Let
\overline{X}
be a del Pezzo surfaces of rank 1, height 3, width 2 and type
\mathcal{S}
, i.e.
\overline{X}\in\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})
. Assume
\overline{X}
has no descendant with elliptic boundary. Then
\mathcal{S}
is listed below, and the following hold.

(
1
)

If
\mathcal{S}
is as in
(1)
–
(9)
, see Table
6
, then
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})
has moduli dimension
1
. It is represented by an almost universal family over
\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
, with symmetry group
G
listed in Table
6
; see Section
2E
for definitions.

(
2
)

If
\mathcal{S}
is as in
(10)
–
(106)
, see Table
7
, then
\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})=1
, i.e.
\overline{X}
is unique up to an isomorphism.

(
3
)

The minimal log resolution
(X,D)
of
\overline{X}
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p
such that
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to one of the surfaces
\overline{Y}
from Example
3.3
, and the combinatorial type of
(X,D,p)
is one of the following.

(a)

\overline{Y}
is of type
3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}+[2,3]
, see Example

(86)

\langle k;[2]^{|2|},[\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]^{|1,3|},[2^{|3|},\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2]\rangle+[3^{|0|},2^{|1|},2]+[3,(2)_{k-3},3]^{|2|}+[2]^{|3|}
,
k\geqslant 3
,

(87)

\langle 2;[2]^{|2|},[\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]^{|1,3|},[2^{|3|},\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2]\rangle+[3^{|0|},2^{|1|},2]+[4]^{|2|}+[2]^{|3|}
,

(88)

\langle\boldsymbol{2};[2]^{|0|},[2]^{|3|},[\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|1,3|},2^{|2|},2]\rangle+[4^{|0|},2^{|1|},2]+[3]^{|2|}+[2]^{|3|}
,

(89)

[2^{|3|},\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2,2^{|2|},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|3|},(2)_{k-2}]*(T^{*})^{|1|}+\langle k;[2],[3]^{|0|},\prescript{|1|}{}{T}\rangle+[3]^{|2|}+[2]^{|3|}
,
d(T)\leqslant 5
,

(90)

[2^{|3|},\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2,k^{|2|},\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}^{|1,3|}]+\langle 2;[2],[3]^{|0|},[2]^{|1|}\rangle+[3,(2)_{k-2}]^{|2|}+[2]^{|3|}
,
k\geqslant 3
,

(91)

[2^{|3|},\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2,k^{|2|},\uline{\boldsymbol{5}}^{|1,3|}]+\langle 2;[2],[3]^{|0|},[2^{|1|},2]\rangle+[3,(2)_{k-2}]^{|2|}+[2]^{|3|}
,
k\in\{3,4\}

(92)

\langle\boldsymbol{k};\prescript{|0|}{}{T}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},[3]^{|2|},[2]^{|3|}\rangle+[\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|1,3|},2^{|2|},2]+\prescript{|0|}{}{T}^{*}*[(2)_{k-2},3,2^{|1|},2]+[2]^{|3|}
,
d(T)\leqslant 5
,

(93)

\langle\boldsymbol{2},[3]^{|0|},[3]^{|2|},[2]^{|3|}\rangle+\prescript{|1|}{}{[}2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|3|},2^{|2|},2]+[2^{|0|},4,3^{|1|},2]+[2]^{|3|}
,

(94)

\langle\boldsymbol{2},[2]^{|0|},[3]^{|2|},[2]^{|3|}\rangle+\prescript{|1|}{}{[}(2)_{k-2},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|3|},2^{|2|},2]+[4^{|0|},k^{|1|},2]+[2]^{|3|}
,
k\geqslant 3
,

(95)

\langle\boldsymbol{k},[2]^{|0|},[3]^{|2|},[2]^{|3|}\rangle+[2^{|1|},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|3|},2^{|2|},2]+[3^{|0|},(2)_{k-3},3^{|1|},2]+[2]^{|3|}
,
k\in\{3,4\}
,

(96)

\langle\boldsymbol{2},[2^{|0|},2],[3]^{|2|},[2]^{|3|}\rangle+\prescript{|1|}{}{[}(2)_{k-2},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|3|},2^{|2|},2]+[5^{|0|},k^{|1|},2]+[2]^{|3|}
,
3\leqslant k\leqslant 6
,

(97)

\langle\boldsymbol{3},[2^{|0|},2],[3]^{|2|},[2]^{|3|}\rangle+[2^{|1|},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|3|},2^{|2|},2]+[4^{|0|},2,3^{|1|},2]+[2]^{|3|}
,

(98)

\langle\boldsymbol{2},[2^{|0|},2,2],[3]^{|2|},[2]^{|3|}\rangle+[2^{|1|},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|3|},2^{|2|},2]+[6^{|0|},3^{|1|},2]+[2]^{|3|}
,

(99)

[3^{|2|},\boldsymbol{k}^{|0|},2^{|3|}]+[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}^{|1,3|},2^{|2|},2]+\langle 2;\prescript{|0|}{}{[}(2)_{k-2},3],[2]^{|1|},[2]\rangle+[2]^{|3|}
,
k\in\{3,4\}
,

(100)

[3^{|2|},\boldsymbol{3}^{|0|},2^{|3|}]+[3^{|1|},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|3|},2^{|2|},2]+\langle 3;\prescript{|0|}{}{[}2,3],[2]^{|1|},[2]\rangle+[2]^{|3|}
,

(101)

\langle k;[2],[2]^{|2|},[2^{|3|},\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2]\rangle+\langle 2;[2],[3]^{|0|},[2]^{|1|}\rangle+[\uline{\boldsymbol{5}}^{|1,3|},(2)_{k-3},3^{|2|}]+[2]^{|3|}
,
k\geqslant 3
,

(102)

\langle 3;[2],[2]^{|2|},[2^{|3|},\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2]\rangle+\langle 2;[2],[3]^{|0|},\prescript{|1|}{}{[}(2)_{k-4}]\rangle+[\uline{\boldsymbol{k}}^{|1,3|},3^{|2|}]+[2]^{|3|}
,
k\in\{5,6\}
,

(103)

\langle k;[2],[3]^{|2|},[2^{|3|},\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2]\rangle+\langle 2;[2],[3]^{|0|},[2]^{|2|}\rangle+[\uline{\boldsymbol{5}}^{|1,3|},3,(2)_{k-3},3,2^{|2|}]+[2]^{|3|}
,
k\geqslant 3
,

(104)

\langle 2;[2],[3]^{|2|},[2^{|3|},\boldsymbol{2}^{|0|},2]\rangle+\langle 2;[2],[3]^{|0|},\prescript{|2|}{}{[}(2)_{k-4}]\rangle+[\uline{\boldsymbol{k}}^{|1,3|},4,2^{|2|}]+[2]^{|3|}
,
k\in\{5,6\}
,

(105)

\langle\boldsymbol{2};[3]^{|2|},[2]^{|0|},[2]^{|3|}\rangle+\prescript{|1|}{}{T}^{*}*[(2)_{k-2},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|3|},2^{|2|},2]+\langle k;[4]^{|0|},\prescript{|1|}{}{T}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},[2]\rangle+[2]^{|3|}
,
d(T)\leqslant 3
,

(106)

\langle\boldsymbol{2};[3]^{|2|},[2^{|0|},2],[2]^{|3|}\rangle+[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}^{|1,3|},2^{|2|},2]+\langle 2;[5]^{|0|},[2]^{|1|},[2]\rangle+[2]^{|3|}
.

As usual,
we first explain how to deduce the uniqueness results
(1)
,
(2)
from the list
(3)
.

Fix a singularity type
\mathcal{S}
as in the above list. No surface
\overline{X}\in\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=3}(\mathcal{S})
has a descendant with elliptic boundary: this follows from Lemma
2.13
in each case except
(8)
–
(9)
, where one directly compares
\mathcal{S}
with
[
PP24a
, Theorem E]
. Thus by
(3)
, the minimal log resolution
(X,D)
of
\overline{X}\in\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=3}(\mathcal{S})
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p
as in the above list. Each
\mathcal{S}
appears there exactly once, so it uniquely determines the combinatorial type of
(X,D,p)
, hence the combinatorial type
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
of
(X,\widecheck{D})
, see Notation
5.7
. Thus the set of isomorphism classes of minimal log resolutions of surfaces in
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})
, call it
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})
, is contained in the image of
\mathcal{P}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
, where
\mathcal{P}(*)
is the set of isomorphic classes of log surfaces of combinatorial type
*
, see
[
PP24a
, §2F]
.
In fact, these two sets are equal. Indeed, if
(X,\widecheck{D})\in\mathcal{P}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
and
(X,D)
is its image in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
, i.e.
D
equals
\widecheck{D}
minus all
(-1)
-curves, then
(X,D)
satisfies the inequality (
3
), so it is the minimal log resolution of a del Pezzo surface of rank one and type
\mathcal{S}
, which has height
3
because
\mathcal{S}
does not appear in
[
PP24a
]
, and has width
2
because
\mathcal{S}
does not appear in Lemma
4.12
, so
(X,D)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})
,
as claimed.

Let
d=\nu-3+\varepsilon
be the moduli dimension specified in Lemma
5.8
(d)
. In cases
(1)
–
(5)
we have
\nu=4
, so
\varepsilon=0
by Lemma
5.8
(c)(c.iii)
, and therefore
d=1
. In cases
(8)
–
(9)
we have
\nu=3
,
\varepsilon=1
, so again
d=1
. In the remaining cases we have
\nu=3
,
\varepsilon=0
, so
d=0
.

If
d=0
then Lemma
5.8
(d)
gives
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})=1
, so
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})=1
, as claimed in
(2)
. Assume
d=1
. Then by Lemma
5.8
(d)
the set
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
is represented by a universal
\operatorname{Aut}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
-faithful family over
B\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
. We check directly that any automorphism of the weighted graph of
D
preserves the vertices corresponding to the
1
- and
2
-section. Thus by
[
PP24a
, Lemmas 2.21, 2.20(c)]
our family, viewed as one representing
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})
, is almost faithful, so
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})
has moduli dimension
1
, as needed. Moreover, it follows that this family, say
f
, is
G
-equivariant, where
G\leqslant\operatorname{Aut}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
is trivial if
\nu=3
and permutes degenerate fibers of the same type if
\nu=4
; and two fibers of
f
are isomorphic if and only if they lie in the same
G
-orbit. We check directly that
G
is as in Table
6
. Eventually, we note that the homomorphism
G\longrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(B)\cong S_{3}
is injective, because
\operatorname{Aut}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
fixes the unique fiber with two
(-1)
-curves, so
G
is indeed the symmetry group of
f
, see Section
2E
.

Therefore, it remains to prove
(3)
, i.e. to verify the completeness of the above list.
We keep Notation
5.7
.

Case
\nu=4
, see Lemma
5.6
(d)
. and Figures
3(a)
,
3(b)

Put
\delta=1
if
p_{1}^{\prime}\not\in\operatorname{Bs}\psi^{-1}
, i.e. if
\overline{X}
swaps to a surface
3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}+[2,3]
, and
\delta=0
otherwise, see Figures
3(a)
and
3(b)
, respectively. Let
\gamma
be a composition of
\tau
with
k-2\geqslant 0
blowups over
A_{0}^{\tau}\cap H_{1}^{\tau}
, at the proper transforms of
H_{1}^{\tau}
, so that
A_{0}^{\gamma}\cap H_{1}^{\gamma}\not\subseteq\operatorname{Bs}\gamma_{+}^{-1}
. If
\psi=\gamma
then
k\geqslant 3
by Lemma
5.10
, hence
D
is as in
(1)
if
\delta=1
and as in
(3)
if
\delta=0
. Thus, we can assume that
\gamma_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
r
. If
r\in A_{0}
then by the definition of
\gamma
and by Lemma
5.8
(c)
we have
\{r\}=A_{0}^{\gamma}\cap H_{1}^{\gamma}
, so
\beta_{D}(H_{1})>3
, which is impossible. Thus
A_{0}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\gamma_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
. Let
\eta
be a composition of
\gamma
with a blowup at
r\in\operatorname{Bs}\gamma_{+}^{-1}
, and if
\psi\neq\eta
let
\upsilon
be a composition of
\eta
with a blowup at a given
s\in\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}
.

Assume first that
r\in A_{j}
for some
j\in\{2,3,4\}
, say
r\in A_{2}
. By Lemma
5.8
(c)
we have three cases:
r\in G_{2}
,
r\in L_{2}
or
r\in H_{2}
. Note that to get a contradiction with inequality (
16
), it is enough to show it for some minimal value of
k
, since we can reduce
k
by vertical swaps over
p_{0}
.

Consider the case
\{r\}=A_{2}\cap G_{2}
. For
k=2
we have
U_{\eta}=\langle\boldsymbol{2};[2],[2],[(2)_{\delta},\uline{\boldsymbol{4-\delta}},2,2]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=1
. Thus (
16
) fails for
k=2
, hence it fails for any
k\geqslant 2
, too, a contradiction.

Consider the case
\{r\}=A_{2}\cap H_{2}
. If
k=3
then
U_{\eta}=\langle\boldsymbol{3};[2],[2],[2,2,2]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})=\tfrac{1}{5}
, which by (
16
) gives
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})>\tfrac{2}{5}
. But
W_{\eta}=[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}]
or
[\uline{\boldsymbol{5}}]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\tfrac{2}{5}
, a contradiction. As before, we conclude that
k=2
. Lemma
5.10
implies that
\eta_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
s\in A_{j}^{\eta}
for some
j\in\{2,3,4\}
. Suppose
s\not\in A_{2}
. Then, say,
s\in A_{3}
, and since
U
is an admissible fork, we conclude that
\{s\}=A_{3}\cap L_{3}
. After a blowup at
s
we get
U_{\upsilon}=\langle\boldsymbol{2};[2,2,2],[3],[2]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})>\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\upsilon}))=\tfrac{2}{5}
, and
W_{\upsilon}=[(2)_{\delta},\uline{\boldsymbol{5-\delta}},2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H^{\upsilon}_{2})=\tfrac{5}{17}
or
\tfrac{4}{11}
; a contradiction. Therefore,
s\in A_{2}
. Since
H_{1}
is the only branching component of
U
, we have
s\in U_{\eta}
. Now
U_{\upsilon}=\langle\boldsymbol{2};[2,3,2],[2],[2]\rangle
,
W_{\upsilon}=[(2)_{\delta},\boldsymbol{5-\delta},2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\upsilon})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=1
; a contradiction with (
16
).

Eventually, consider the case
\{r\}=A_{2}\cap L_{2}
. We have
U_{\eta}=\langle\boldsymbol{k};[3],[2],[2]\rangle
and
W_{\eta}=[(2)_{(k-1)\delta},\uline{\boldsymbol{4-\delta}},2,2]
. If
k=3
then
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=1
, so from (
16
) we infer that
k=2
. If
\psi=\eta
then
D
is as in
(2)
if
\delta=1
and as in
(6)
if
\delta=0
. Suppose
\psi\neq\eta
, and as before fix
s\in\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}
. If
s\in A_{j}
for some
j\in\{3,4\}
then interchanging
{l}_{2}
with
{l}_{j}
if necessary we can assume
s\in L_{3}
. Now
U_{\upsilon}=\langle\boldsymbol{2};[3],[3],[2]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\upsilon})=\tfrac{1}{5}
and thus
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})>\frac{2}{5}
by (
16
), but
W_{\upsilon}=\langle\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},[2],[2,2],[2,2]\rangle
or
[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=\frac{1}{7}
or
\frac{1}{4}
, a contradiction. Assume
s\in A_{2}
. Then
U_{\upsilon}=\langle\boldsymbol{2};[4],[2],[2]\rangle
if
s\in L_{2}
and
U_{\upsilon}=\langle\boldsymbol{2};[2,3],[2],[2]\rangle
otherwise; hence
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\upsilon})=\tfrac{1}{3}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})>\tfrac{1}{3}
by (
16
). But
W_{\upsilon}=\langle 2;[2],[2],[(2)_{\delta},\uline{\boldsymbol{4-\delta}}]\rangle
or
[(2)_{\delta},\uline{\boldsymbol{4-\delta}},3,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=\tfrac{1}{3}
; a contradiction. We conclude that
\delta=0
and
\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
. Now
U_{\upsilon}=\langle\boldsymbol{2};[3],[2],[2]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\upsilon})=\tfrac{1}{2}
and
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})>\tfrac{1}{4}
by (
16
). Recall from Lemma
5.8
(c)
that
s\in D_{\eta}
. In fact,
s\in H_{2}
, since otherwise
W_{\upsilon}=[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2]
or
[3,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})\leqslant\tfrac{1}{4}
, which is impossible. If
\psi=\upsilon
then
D
is as in
(7)
. Otherwise, after one further blowup,
W
becomes
[\uline{\boldsymbol{6}},2,2]
or
[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=\tfrac{1}{4}
or
\tfrac{5}{23}
; a contradiction.

It remains to consider the case
\operatorname{Bs}\gamma_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
. Since
\psi\neq\gamma
, we have
\delta=0
, see Figure
3(b)
. We have
U=\langle\boldsymbol{k};[2],[2],[2]\rangle
, so
k\geqslant 3
by Lemma
5.10
, and
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}))=\tfrac{k-2}{2k-3}\geqslant\tfrac{1}{3}
. If
r\in G_{1}
then
W_{\eta}=[(2)_{k-2},3,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}]
, so for
k=3
we have
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\tfrac{1}{3}
; a contradiction. Hence
r\in H_{2}
or
L_{1}
, so
W_{\eta}=[\uline{\boldsymbol{5}}]
or
[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}]
and thus
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=\tfrac{2}{5}
. The inequality
\tfrac{k-2}{2k-3}<\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=\frac{2}{5}
gives
k=3
. If
\psi=\upsilon
then
D
is as in
(4)
or
(5)
. Suppose
\psi\neq\upsilon
. If
r\in H_{2}
then
W_{\upsilon}=[\uline{\boldsymbol{6}}]
or
[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{5}}]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=\tfrac{1}{3}
, which is false. Thus
r\in L_{1}
,
W_{\upsilon}=[2,3,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}]
or
\langle 2;[2],[2],[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}]\rangle
, and again
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=\tfrac{1}{3}
; a contradiction.

Case
\nu=3
, see Lemma
5.6
(e)
and Figure
3(c)
.

By Lemma
5.11
, the map
\varphi_{+}^{-1}
has base points
q_{1}\in A_{1}^{\varphi}
and
q_{2}\in A_{2}^{\varphi}
. Let
\tau
be a composition of
\varphi
with a blowup at
\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
.

As a warm-up, we consider the case with moduli, i.e. when for some decomposition
\psi=\gamma\circ\gamma_{+}
as in Notation
5.7
, the morphism
\gamma_{+}
has a base point
r\not\in D_{\gamma}
, see Lemma
5.8
(c)
. Assume first that
r\in A_{2}^{\gamma}
. Then the admissibility of
U
implies that
r=q_{2}
, i.e.
r
is the center of the first blowup over
p_{2}^{\prime}
. Again, from the admissibility of
U
we see that
\{q_{1}\}=A_{1}\cap H_{2}
and
A_{3}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\varphi_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
. Thus
U_{\tau}=\langle 2;[\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}],[2],[2,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle
. Applying once again the fact that
U
is an admissible fork, we infer that
\tau_{+}^{-1}
is a composition of some
k-2\geqslant 0
blowups over
A_{2}\cap U_{\tau}
, on the proper transforms of
U_{\tau}
. Thus case
(8)
holds.

Assume that
r\not\in A_{2}^{\gamma}
. Interchanging
{l}_{2}
with
{l}_{3}
, if needed, we can assume
r\not\in A_{3}^{\gamma}
, too, so
r\in A_{0}^{\gamma}
or
r\in A_{1}^{\gamma}
. In fact,
r\in A_{1}^{\gamma}
since
U
is admissible. In turn, the admissibility of
W
implies that
r
is the center of the first blowup over
p_{1}^{\prime}
, i.e.
r=q_{1}
; and
\{q_{2}\}=A_{2}^{\varphi}\cap L_{2}^{\varphi}
or
A_{2}^{\varphi}\cap H_{2}^{\varphi}
. In the first case, the admissibility of
U
implies that
\tau_{+}^{-1}
is a composition of blowups over
r
, so
D
is of the same (non-decorated) type as in case
(8)
found above. Applying the automorphism
(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2})\mapsto(A_{1},A_{2},A_{0})
from Remark
5.12
, we get back the case
(8)
.

Thus we can assume
\{q_{2}\}=A_{2}^{\varphi}\cap H_{2}^{\varphi}
. Then
U_{\tau}
is a
(-2)
-chain, so by Lemma
5.10
,
\tau_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
s
on
A_{0}^{\tau}
,
A_{2}^{\tau}
or
A_{3}^{\tau}
. Let
\eta
be a composition of
\tau
with a blowup at
s
. If
s\in A_{3}^{\tau}
then it is easy to see that the admissibility of
W
forces
U
to be a
(-2)
-chain or fork, contrary to Lemma
5.10
. Thus
A_{3}^{\tau}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
. Applying the automorphism
(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2})\mapsto(A_{2},A_{1},A_{0})
from Remark
5.12
, we can assume that
s\in A_{0}^{\tau}
. If
s\in L_{1}^{\tau}
then as before we get a contradiction with Lemma
5.10
, so
s\in H_{1}^{\tau}
. Since
W
is admissible, the morphism
\eta_{+}
is a composition of blowups over
r
, on the proper transform of
W_{\tau}
. Thus case
(9)
holds.

In the remaining part of the proof, we can and will assume that for any decomposition
\psi=\gamma\circ\gamma_{+}
as in Notation
5.7
, the base locus of
\gamma_{+}^{-1}
is contained in
D_{\gamma}
. Like in case
\nu=4
or in the proofs of Lemmas
4.12
and
5.15
, we will use this assumption without further comment. We now split the proof into cases according to the placement of the base points
q_{j}\in A_{j}^{\varphi}
from Lemma
5.11
.

Case 1:

q_{2}\in H_{2}
.

Subcase 1.1:

The map
\tau_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
q_{3}\in A_{3}
.
Let
\sigma
be a composition of
\tau
with a blowup at
q_{3}
.

Subcase 1.1.1:

q_{3}\in L_{3}
.
Recall that we have a base point
q_{1}\in A_{1}
, lying on
L_{1}
,
G_{1}
or
H_{2}
.

Subcase 1.1.1.1:

q_{1}\in L_{1}\cup G_{1}
.

Note that the weighted graphs of
U,W
obtained in case
q_{1}\in L_{1}
are weighted subgraphs of those for
q_{1}\in G_{1}
, so by Lemma
2.4
, if (
16
) fails for the former, it fails for the latter, too.

Assume first that
\psi\neq\sigma
, and let
\eta
be a composition of
\sigma
with a blowup at some
r\in\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}
.

Suppose
r\in A_{2}
. Then for
q_{1}\in L_{1}
we get
U_{\eta}+W_{\eta}=\langle 2;[2],[2],[3,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle+[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2]
if
r\in H_{2}
and
[3,\boldsymbol{2},2,3,2]+\langle\uline{\boldsymbol{3}};[2],[2,2],[2,2]\rangle
otherwise; hence
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=1
or
\tfrac{127}{175}
. Thus inequality (
16
) fails in this case, so by our observation it fails in case
q_{1}\in G_{1}
, too; a contradiction.

Similarly, if
\{r\}=A_{0}\cap L_{1}
or
A_{3}\cap L_{3}
then for
q_{1}\in L_{1}
we get
U_{\eta}+W_{\eta}=\langle\boldsymbol{2};[2],[3],[2,2,2]\rangle+[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2,2]
or
[4,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]+\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\rangle
, respectively, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=1
. As before, it follows that inequality (
16
) fails both for
q_{1}\in L_{1}
and
q_{1}\in G_{1}
; a contradiction.

Assume
\{r\}=A_{0}\cap H_{1}
. Then
U_{\eta}=[3,\boldsymbol{3},2,2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta}))=\tfrac{8}{23}
. If
q_{1}\in G_{1}
then
W_{\eta}=[2,3,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2,2]
, so
\frac{8}{23}<\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\tfrac{11}{41}
, which is impossible. Thus
q_{1}\in L_{1}
. If
\psi=\eta
then
D
is as in
(10)
with
k=2
. Suppose
\psi\neq\eta
, and let
\vartheta
be a composition of
\eta
with a blowup at
s\in\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}
. We know that
s\not\in A_{2}
and
\{s\}\neq A_{3}\cap L_{3}
. If
s\in W_{\eta}
then
W_{\vartheta}=[2,3,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2,2]
, so
\frac{8}{23}<\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})=\tfrac{8}{29}
; a contradiction. If
s\in A_{1}
then, since
s\not\in W_{\eta}
, we get
W_{\vartheta}=\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\rangle
, so
\frac{8}{23}<\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})=\tfrac{1}{4}
; a contradiction. Thus
s\in A_{0}
, hence
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})=\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\tfrac{2}{5}
and therefore
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\vartheta})>\tfrac{1}{5}
by (
16
). But
U_{\vartheta}=[3,\boldsymbol{4},2,2,2]
or
\langle\boldsymbol{3};[2],[3],[2,2,2]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\vartheta})=\tfrac{1}{5}
or
\frac{1}{17}
; a contradiction.

Assume
\{r\}=A_{3}\cap W_{\sigma}
. Then
U_{\eta}=[2,3,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
, hence
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta}))=\tfrac{4}{17}
. If
q_{1}\in G_{1}
then
W_{\eta}=[3,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},3,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})=\tfrac{1}{5}<\frac{4}{17}
, a contradiction. Thus
q_{1}\in L_{1}
. If
\psi=\eta
then
D
is as in
(10)
with
k=3
. Suppose
\psi\neq\eta
, and as before let
\vartheta
be a composition of
\eta
with a blowup at
s\in\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}
. We know that
s\in A_{1}\cup A_{3}
. If
s\in A_{1}
then
W_{\vartheta}=[2,3,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},3,2]
if
s\in W_{\eta}
and
\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,3,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\rangle
if
s\in L_{1}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})=\tfrac{2}{11}
or
\tfrac{1}{8}
; a contradiction. If
s\in A_{3}
then similarly
W_{\vartheta}=[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},4,2]
or
\langle 3;[2],[2],[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})=\tfrac{10}{43}
or
\tfrac{1}{11}
; a contradiction.

Therefore, we can assume
\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
. Now
U=[3,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}))=\tfrac{2}{11}
. Let
\vartheta
be a composition of
\sigma
with
k-2\geqslant 0
blowups at
A_{1}\cap W_{\sigma}
and its infinitely near points on the proper transforms of
W_{\sigma}
, so that
A_{1}\cap W_{\vartheta}\not\subseteq\operatorname{Bs}\vartheta_{+}^{-1}
. Now
W_{\vartheta}=[2+\varepsilon,k,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2,2]
, where
\varepsilon=0
if
q_{1}\in L_{1}
and
\varepsilon=1
if
q_{1}\in G_{1}
. We compute that
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\vartheta})>\tfrac{2}{11}
if and only if
k\leqslant 7-2\varepsilon
, so if
\psi=\vartheta
then
D
is as in
(11)
if
\varepsilon=0
and
(12)
if
\varepsilon=1
. Assume
\psi\neq\vartheta
. Since
\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
, definition of
\vartheta
shows that the unique base point of
\vartheta_{+}^{-1}
is
A_{1}\cap(D_{\vartheta}-W_{\vartheta})
. Since
W
is admissible,
\vartheta_{+}
is a single blowup, and
q_{1}\in L_{1}
. Now
W=\langle k;[2],[2],[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\rangle
, so the inequality
\frac{2}{11}<\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=\frac{1}{7k-10}
gives
k=2
, and
D
is as in
(27)
.

Subcase 1.1.1.2:

q_{1}\in H_{2}
.

We have
U_{\sigma}=[3,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
,
W_{\sigma}=[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2]
. Inequality (
16
) gives
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})>1-2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\sigma})=\frac{1}{5}
. For a given base point
r\in\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}
we denote by
\eta
a composition of
\sigma
with a blowup at
r
; and for a given
s\in\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}
we denote by
\vartheta
a composition of
\eta
with a blowup at
s
.

Consider the case
r\in A_{0}
. If
r\in L_{1}
then
U_{\eta}=\langle\boldsymbol{2},[2],[3],[2,2,2]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})=\frac{1}{5}
, which is false. Thus
\{r\}=A_{0}\cap H_{1}
. If
\psi=\eta
then
D
is as in
(13)
. Suppose
\psi\neq\eta
, and fix
s\in\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}
. If
s\in A_{0}
then
U_{\vartheta}=[3,\boldsymbol{4},2,2,2]
if
s\in H_{1}
and
\langle\boldsymbol{3},[2],[3],[2,2,2]\rangle
otherwise, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\vartheta})=\frac{1}{5}
or
\frac{1}{17}
, which is false. Thus
s\not\in A_{0}
. Recall that
U_{\eta}=[3,\boldsymbol{3},2,2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\frac{1}{2}(1-\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta}))=\frac{8}{23}
. If
s\in A_{1}\cup A_{2}
then
W_{\vartheta}=[\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},2,2]
or
[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\frac{4}{13}
or
\frac{5}{17}
, in any case
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})<\frac{8}{23}
, which is false. Thus
s\in A_{3}
, so
W_{\vartheta}=\langle 2;[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}],[2],[2]\rangle
if
s\in U_{\eta}
and
[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},3,2]
if
s\in W_{\eta}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})=\frac{1}{3}<\frac{8}{23}
; a contradiction.

Thus we can assume
\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}\cap A_{0}=\emptyset
. Consider the case
\{r\}=A_{2}\cap W_{\tau}
. If
\psi=\eta
then
D
is as in
(28)
. Suppose
\psi\neq\eta
, so
\eta_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
s\not\in A_{0}
. Because
U
is admissible,
s\not\in A_{2}
. If
s\in A_{3}
then
U_{\eta}+W_{\eta}=\langle 2;[2],[2],[4,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle+\langle 2;[2],[2],[\boldsymbol{5}]\rangle
if
s\in U_{\eta}
and
\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,3,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle+[\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},3,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\tfrac{5}{6}
or
\tfrac{59}{69}
, contrary to (
16
). Thus
s\in A_{1}
. We have
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})=\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})=\tfrac{1}{2}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})>\tfrac{1}{4}
by (
16
). But
W_{\eta}=[\uline{\boldsymbol{6}},2,2]
if
s\in W_{\eta}
and
[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},2,2]
otherwise, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\tfrac{1}{4}
or
\tfrac{5}{23}
; a contradiction.

Consider the case
\{r\}=A_{2}\cap U_{\tau}
. Then
U_{\eta}=[3,\boldsymbol{2},2,3,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\frac{1}{2}(1-\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta}))=\frac{7}{25}
. If
\psi=\eta
then
D
is as in
(14)
. Suppose
\eta_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
s
. Recall that
s\not\in A_{0}
. If
s\in A_{1}
then
W_{\eta}=[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},2,2]
if
s\in W_{\eta}
and
\langle\uline{\boldsymbol{4}};[2],[2],[2]\rangle
otherwise, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\tfrac{5}{23}<\frac{7}{25}
or
\tfrac{1}{5}<\frac{7}{25}
, which is impossible. If
s\in A_{3}
then
W_{\eta}=[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},3,2]
if
s\in W_{\eta}
and
\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}]\rangle
otherwise, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\frac{7}{31}<\frac{7}{25}
or
\frac{1}{5}
, which, again, is impossible. Thus
s\in A_{3}
, so
W_{\eta}=[3,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2]
if
s\in W_{\eta}
and
[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2]
otherwise, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\frac{2}{9}
or
\frac{1}{4}
; a contradiction since
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})>\frac{7}{25}
.

Therefore, we can assume
\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}\cup A_{3}
. Consider the case
\{r\}=A_{3}\cap U_{\tau}
. Then
U_{\eta}=[4,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\frac{1}{2}(1-\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta}))=\frac{1}{4}
. If
\psi=\eta
then
D
is as in
(29)
. Suppose
\psi\neq\eta
, so
\eta_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
s\in A_{1}\cup A_{3}
. If
s\in A_{1}
then
W_{\eta}=\langle 2;[2],[2],[\uline{\boldsymbol{5}}]\rangle
if
s\in H_{2}
and
\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}]\rangle
otherwise, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\tfrac{1}{4}
or
\tfrac{1}{5}<\frac{1}{4}
, which is impossible. Thus
s\in A_{3}
. Now
U_{\eta}+W_{\eta}=[5,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]+\langle 2;[2],[2,2],[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}]\rangle
if
s\in U_{\eta}
and
[2,4,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]+\langle 2;[2],[3],[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}]\rangle
otherwise, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=1
or
\tfrac{283}{297}
; a contradiction with (
16
).

Consider the case
\{r\}=A_{3}\cap W_{\tau}
. Let
\upsilon
be the composition of
\eta
with
k-2\geqslant 1
blowups over
r
, on proper transforms of
W_{\eta}
, so that
A_{3}\cap W_{\upsilon}\not\subseteq\operatorname{Bs}\upsilon_{+}^{-1}
. Then
