U_{\upsilon}+W_{\upsilon}=[(2)_{k-2},3,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]+[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},k,2]
. Inequality (
16
) gives
k\leqslant 5
. If
\psi=\upsilon
then
D
is as in
(15)
. Suppose
\psi\neq\upsilon
, and let
\gamma
be a composition of
\upsilon
with a blowup at
z\in\operatorname{Bs}\upsilon_{+}^{-1}
. If
z\in A_{3}
then by the definition of
\upsilon
we have
\{z\}=A_{3}\cap U_{\upsilon}
, so
U_{\gamma}+W_{\gamma}=[3,(2)_{k-3},3,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]+\langle k;[2],[2],[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}]\rangle
which yields a contradiction with (
16
). Thus
z\in A_{1}
. For
k=3
we have
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})=\frac{9}{17}
, so in any case
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\frac{4}{17}
.

If
z\not\in H_{2}
then
W_{\gamma}=[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},k,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\gamma})\leqslant\frac{7}{31}<\frac{4}{17}
, a contradiction. Thus
\{z\}=A_{1}\cap H_{2}
. Inequality (
16
) gives
k=3
. If
\psi=\gamma
then
D
is as in
(30)
. If
\psi\neq\gamma
then after one further blowup over
\operatorname{Bs}\gamma_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
we get
W=[\uline{\boldsymbol{6}},3,2]
or
[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},3,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=\frac{3}{14}
or
\frac{7}{41}
, a contradiction since
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\frac{4}{17}
.

Eventually, we can assume
\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
. Then
U=[3,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\frac{1}{2}(1-\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}))=\frac{2}{11}
. Now
D-U=T^{*}*[(2)_{k-2},\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2]+\langle k;T^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},[3],[2]\rangle
or
[(2)_{k-2},\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2]+[3,k,2]
for some
k\geqslant 2
and an admissible chain
T
; we put
T=0
in the latter case. The inequality
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\frac{2}{11}
implies that one of the following holds.

If
T=0
then
k\leqslant 9
, so
D
is as in
(16)
. Assume
T\neq 0
. If
k=2
then
T=[(2)_{l}]
,
l\in\{1,2,3\}
or
T=[3]
, so
D
is as in
(31)
or
(32)
, respectively. If
k=3
then
T=[2]
or
[2,2]
, so
D
is as in
(33)
. Otherwise,
k=4
,
T=[2]
, and
D
is as in
(34)
.

Subcase 1.1.2:

q_{3}\in G_{3}
.

If
q_{1}\in G_{1}
then
U_{\sigma}=[3,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\sigma})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\sigma})=1
, contrary to (
16
). Assume
q_{1}\in L_{1}
. If
\psi=\sigma
then
D
is as in
(17)
. Suppose
\psi\neq\sigma
, and let
\eta
be a composition of
\sigma
with a blowup at
r\in\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}
. Since
U
is admissible, we have
\{r\}=A_{2}\cap H_{2}
,
A_{3}\cap U_{\sigma}
,
A_{1}\cap L_{1}
,
A_{1}\cap U_{\sigma}
or
A_{0}\cap H_{1}
. Then
U_{\eta}=\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle
,

[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},3,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
,

\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\rangle
,

[2,3,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
, or

[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2,2,\boldsymbol{3},2,2,2]
; so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\tfrac{3}{7}
,

\tfrac{65}{73}
,

\tfrac{3}{4}
,

\tfrac{57}{61}
or

\tfrac{9}{13}
; a contradiction with (
16
).

Thus we can assume
q_{1}\in H_{2}
. If
\psi=\sigma
then
D
is as in
(18)
with
k=2
. Assume
\psi\neq\sigma
, and as before let
\eta
be a composition of
\sigma
with a blowup at
r\in\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}
. If
r\in A_{0}
then
r\in H_{1}
since
U
is admissible, so
U_{\eta}=[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2,\boldsymbol{3},2,2,2]
and
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\tfrac{27}{31}
, contrary to (
16
). If
r\in A_{2}
then
U_{\eta}=\langle 2;[2],[2],[\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle
if
r\in H_{2}
and
[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,3,2]
otherwise, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\tfrac{3}{4}
or
\tfrac{9}{13}
, again contrary to (
16
).

Thus
\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}\cup A_{3}
. Assume
r\in A_{3}
. Since
U
is admissible we have
r\in U_{\sigma}
. If
\psi=\eta
then
D
is as in
(18)
with
k=3
. In turn, if
\psi\neq\eta
then after one blowup at some
s\in\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}\cup A_{3}
,
U
becomes
[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},4,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
if
s\in A_{3}
, and
[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},3,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
or
[\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},3,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
if
s\in A_{1}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=1
,
\tfrac{65}{79}
or
\tfrac{55}{59}
, contrary to (
16
). Thus we can assume
\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
. If
\psi=\eta
then
D
is as in
(35)
if
r\in H_{2}
or
(19)
otherwise. After one further blowup,
U
becomes
[\uline{\boldsymbol{6}},2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
or
[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
in the first case, and
[3,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
or
[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
in the second case, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=1
,
\tfrac{15}{17}
,
\tfrac{51}{59}
or
\tfrac{45}{52}
, contrary to (
16
).

Subcase 1.1.3:

q_{3}\in H_{2}
.

Suppose that for both
j\in\{2,3\}
, the curve
A_{j}
contains a base point of
\sigma_{+}^{-1}
, say
s_{j}
. Let
\upsilon
be a composition of
\varphi
with a blowup at
q_{2},s_{2},q_{3},s_{3}
(so we do not blow up at
q_{1}
yet). Since
U_{\upsilon}
has at most one branching component, we have, say,
s_{2}\in U_{\sigma}
. Now
U_{\upsilon}+W_{\upsilon}=[2,3,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,3,2]+[\uline{\boldsymbol{6}}]
if
s_{3}\in U_{\sigma}
and
\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,3,2,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle+[\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},2]
if
s_{3}\in H_{2}
, so in either case
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\upsilon})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=1
, a contradiction with (
16
). Thus interchanging
{l}_{2}
with
{l}_{3}
if necessary, we can assume
A_{3}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
. Since by Lemma
5.10

U
is not a
(-2)
-chain, the map
\sigma_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
r\not\in A_{1}
, so
r\in A_{0}\cup A_{2}
.

Subcase 1.1.3.1:

r\in A_{2}
.
Suppose
r\in H_{2}
. By Lemma
5.10

U
is not a
(-2)
-fork, so
\sigma_{+}^{-1}
has another base point off
A_{1}
, and since
U
is admissible, this base point is
A_{0}\cap H_{1}
. Like before, letting
\upsilon
be a composition of
\varphi
with a blowup at
q_{2}
,
q_{3}
,
s
and that point, we get
U_{\upsilon}+W_{\upsilon}=\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,2,2,\boldsymbol{3},2]\rangle+[\uline{\boldsymbol{5}}]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\upsilon})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\upsilon})=1
, a contradiction with (
16
).

Thus
\{r\}=A_{2}\cap U_{\sigma}
. Let
\eta
be a composition of
\sigma
with a blowup at
r
, and for a given
s\in\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}
, let
\vartheta
be a composition of
\eta
with a blowup at
s
. Suppose
s\in A_{0}
. Since
U
is admissible, we have
s\in H_{1}
. Now
U_{\vartheta}=[2,3,2,\boldsymbol{3},2,2,2]
, so inequality (
16
) gives
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\frac{1}{2}(1-\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\vartheta}))=\frac{5}{13}
. But
W_{\vartheta}=[\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},2]
if
q_{1}\in H_{2}
;
[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2]
if
q_{1}\in L_{1}
and
[2,3,2,\boldsymbol{4},2]
if
q_{1}\in G_{1}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})=\frac{1}{3}
,
\frac{5}{17}
or
\frac{5}{23}
; a contradiction. Thus
\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}\cup A_{2}
.

Assume that
\eta_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
s\in A_{2}
. If
s\in H_{2}
then
U_{\vartheta}=\langle 3,[2],[2],[2,2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle
, so (
16
) gives
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\frac{1}{2}(1-\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta}))=\frac{2}{7}
, but we have
W_{\vartheta}=[\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},3]
,
[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},3]
or
[3,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},3]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})=\frac{2}{7}
,
\frac{6}{29}
or
\frac{8}{49}
, a contradiction. Thus
\{s\}=A_{2}\cap U_{\eta}
. We have
U_{\vartheta}=[2,4,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\frac{1}{2}(1-\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\vartheta}))=\frac{1}{4}
. If
q_{1}\in L_{1}
or
G_{1}
then
W_{\vartheta}
is a chain
[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2]
or has this chain as a weighted subgraph, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})\leqslant\frac{1}{4}
, which is false. Thus
q_{1}\in H_{2}
. If
\vartheta_{+}^{-1}
has a base point on
A_{1}
then after blowing up at this point we get
W=[\uline{\boldsymbol{6}},2,2]
or
[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=\frac{1}{4}
or
\frac{5}{23}
, which is impossible as
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\frac{1}{4}
. Thus
\operatorname{Bs}\vartheta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{2}
. Let
\upsilon
be a composition of
\vartheta
with
k-4\geqslant 0
further blowups over
s
, on the proper transforms of
U_{\eta}
, so that
A_{2}\cap U_{\upsilon}\not\subseteq\operatorname{Bs}\upsilon_{+}^{-1}
. Now
U_{\upsilon}=[2,k,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
and
W_{\upsilon}=[\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},(2)_{k-2}]
, so inequality (
16
) gives
k\leqslant 5
. If
\psi=\upsilon
then
D
is as in
(20)
. Otherwise, by the definition of
\upsilon
the unique base point of
\upsilon_{+}^{-1}
is
A_{2}\cap W_{\upsilon}
, and after blowing up at that point we get
U+W=\langle k;[2],[2],[2,2,2,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle+[\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},(2)_{k-3},3]
, which leads to a contradiction with inequality (
16
).

It remains to consider the case
\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
. Now
U=[2,3,2,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}))=\tfrac{1}{5}
by (
16
). Consider the case
q_{1}\in G_{1}
. Then the admissibility of
W
gives
\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq W_{\eta}
. If
\psi=\eta
then
D
is as in
(21)
, otherwise after a blowup at the (unique) base point of
\eta_{+}^{-1}
we get
W=[3,3,\boldsymbol{4},2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=\frac{1}{5}
, which is false.

Consider the case
q_{1}\in L_{1}
. Let
\vartheta
be a composition of
\eta
with
k-2\geqslant 0
blowups over
q_{1}
, on the proper transforms of
W_{\eta}
, so that
W_{\vartheta}\cap A_{1}^{\vartheta}\not\subseteq\operatorname{Bs}\vartheta_{+}^{-1}
. We have
W_{\vartheta}=[2,k,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2]
, so the inequality
\frac{1}{5}<\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})=\frac{2k+1}{14k+11}
gives
k\leqslant 3
. If
\psi=\vartheta
then
D
is as in
(22)
, otherwise after a blowup at the unique base point of
\vartheta_{+}^{-1}
we get
W=\langle k;[2],[2],[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=\frac{k-1}{7k-9}\leqslant\frac{1}{5}
, a contradiction.

Eventually, consider the case
q_{1}\in H_{2}
. Let
\vartheta
be a composition of
\eta
with
k-2\geqslant 0
blowups over
q_{1}
, on the proper transforms of
D_{\eta}-W_{\eta}
, so that
\operatorname{Bs}\vartheta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq W_{\vartheta}\cap A_{1}^{\vartheta}
. Then
W_{\vartheta}=[(2)_{k-2},\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},2]
, so the inequality
\frac{1}{5}<\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})=\frac{k+1}{7k-5}
gives
k\leqslant 4
. If
\psi=\vartheta
then
D
is as in
(23)
. Assume
\psi\neq\vartheta
, and let
\upsilon
be the composition of
\vartheta
with
l-5\geqslant 1
blowups over
A_{1}\cap W_{\vartheta}
, on the proper transforms of
W_{\vartheta}
. Then
W_{\upsilon}=[\uline{\boldsymbol{l}},2]
if
k=2
, and
[l-2,(2)_{k-3},\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},2]
otherwise. The inequality
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\frac{1}{5}
implies that the first case holds and
l\leqslant 7
. If
\psi=\upsilon
then
D
is as in
(36)
; otherwise after one further blowup we get
W=[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{l}},2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=\frac{1}{l-1}\leqslant\frac{1}{5}
, which is false.

Subcase 1.1.3.2:

r\in A_{0}
,
\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}\cap A_{2}=\emptyset
.

Let
\eta
be a composition of
\sigma
with
k-2
blowups over
A_{0}\cap H_{1}
, on the proper transforms of
H_{1}
, so that
A_{0}\cap H_{1}\not\subseteq\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}
. Since
U
is admissible, we get
A_{0}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
. Our assumptions imply that
r\in H_{1}^{\tau}
, so
k-2\geqslant 1
; and
\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
.

We have
U=[(2)_{3},\boldsymbol{k},(2)_{3}]
, so (
16
) gives
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\frac{1}{2}(1-\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta}))=\frac{k-2}{2k-3}\geqslant\frac{1}{3}
. If
q_{1}\in G_{1}
then for
k=3
we have
W_{\eta}=[2,3,2,\boldsymbol{4}]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=\frac{1}{3}
, which in fact yields a contradiction for all
k
(apply (
16
) for
\gamma
being a composition of
\tau
with one blowup at
r
). Assume
q_{1}\in L_{1}
. Then
W_{\eta}=[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}]
, so the inequality
\frac{k-2}{2k-3}<\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=\tfrac{2}{5}
gives
k=3
. If
\psi=\eta
then
D
is as in
(24)
. If
\psi\neq\eta
then after one blowup over
q_{1}
we get
W=\langle 2;[2],[2],[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}]\rangle
or
[2,3,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=\tfrac{1}{3}
; a contradiction.
If
q_{1}\in H_{1}
then
W_{\sigma}=[\uline{\boldsymbol{5}}]
, so as before we get
k=3
; if
\psi=\eta
then
D
is as in
(25)
, otherwise after one blowup
W=[\uline{\boldsymbol{6}}]
or
[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{5}}]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=\tfrac{1}{3}
; a contradiction.

Subcase 1.2:

\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}\cap A_{3}=\emptyset
.
Let
\gamma
be a composition of
\varphi
with a blowup at
q_{2}
.
Then
(X_{\gamma},D_{\gamma})
admits an involution
(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2})\mapsto(A_{2},A_{1},A_{0})
from Remark
5.12
; call it
\iota
. We have
\iota(G_{1}^{\gamma})=G_{1}^{\gamma}
and
\iota(L_{1}^{\gamma})=H_{2}^{\gamma}
. Therefore, replacing
\gamma_{+}
with
\iota\circ\gamma_{+}
, if needed, we can assume that
q_{1}\in G_{1}
or
q_{1}\in H_{2}
.

Subcase 1.2.1:

U
is a chain.

Then
U=[2,\boldsymbol{k},2,l,2]
for some
k,l\geqslant 2
, and
(k,l)\neq 2
by Lemma
5.10
. Here the
(-k)
-curve meets
A_{0}
, and the
(-l)
-curve meets
A_{2}
.

Subcase 1.2.1.1:

q_{1}\in G_{1}
.
The involution
\iota\in\operatorname{Aut}(X_{\gamma},D_{\gamma})
maps the divisors
A_{1}^{\gamma}
,
G_{1}^{\gamma}
and
W_{\gamma}
to themselves, and interchanges
A_{0}^{\gamma}
with
A_{2}^{\gamma}
. Thus replacing
\gamma_{+}
with
\iota\circ\gamma_{+}
we remain in Case
1.2.1.1
, but with the roles of
k
and
l
interchanged. Thus we can assume
k\geqslant l
. In particular, we have
k\geqslant 3
, i.e.
A_{0}^{\tau}\cap H_{1}\subseteq\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}
.

Consider the case when
W
is a chain. Then
W=[(2)_{k-2},3,m,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},(2)_{l-2}]
for some
m\geqslant 2
. Inequality (
16
) gives
l=2
, and either
k=3
; or
k=4
,
m\leqslant 5
; or
k\leqslant 7
,
m=2
, hence
D
is as in
(37)
–
(39)
. If
W
is a fork then since
W
is admissible and
k\geqslant 3
we have
W=\langle m;[2],[\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}],[2,3]\rangle
, so
(k,l)=(3,2)
and
D
is as in
(62)
.

Subcase 1.2.1.2:

q_{1}\in H_{2}
.
Denote by
L
the connected component of
D
containing
L_{1}
. Assume first that
L
is a chain. Then
W=[(2)_{m-2},\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},(2)_{l-2}]
for some
m\geqslant 2
. Since
(k,l)\neq(2,2)
, inequality (
16
) shows that either
l=2
and
D
is as in
(40)
–
(42)
, or
k=2
and
D
is as in
(43)
–
(47)
, or
k,l\geqslant 3
,
m=2
and
D
is as in
(48)
–
(49)
.

Assume now that
L
is a fork, so
L=\langle m;[2],[(2)_{k-2},3],T^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}\rangle
for some
m\geqslant 2
and admissible
T
; and
W=T^{*}*[(2)_{m-2},\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},(2)_{l-2}]
. Since
\rho(\overline{X})=1
, the surface
\overline{X}
is del Pezzo if and only if
0>K_{\overline{X}}\cdot\pi(A_{0})=1-\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})-\operatorname{ld}(C)
, where
C
is the tip of
L
meeting
A_{0}
. This inequality is equivalent to

(17)

\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})+\operatorname{ld}(C)>1,

which will be now more convenient than inequality (
16
), equivalent to
K_{\overline{X}}\cdot\pi(F)<0
for a fiber
F
.

Consider the case
k\geqslant 3
. Suppose
l\geqslant 3
. Then by Lemma
2.4
, log discrepancies of
H_{1}
and
C
do not exceed those in case
(k,l,m)=(3,3,2)
;
T=[2]
, when
U=[2,\boldsymbol{3},2,3,2]
and
L=\langle 2;[\mathit{2},3],[2],[2]\rangle
, where
C
is the italicized tip. In this case,
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})+\operatorname{ld}(C)=1
; contrary to (
17
). Thus
l=2
. If
k\geqslant 4
then
T=[2]
by the admissibility of
L
, so
U=[2,\boldsymbol{k},2,2,2]
,
L=\langle m;[\mathit{2},(2)_{k-3},3],[2],[2]\rangle
, and inequality (
17
) gives
(k,m)=(4,2)
, so
D
is as in
(63)
. If
k=3
then
d(T)\leqslant 3
by the admissibility of
L
, so
D
is as in
(64)
.

Consider now the case
k=2
, so
l\geqslant 3
by assumption and
d(T)\leqslant 5
by the admissibility of
L
. Let
B
be the branching component of
L
. Inequality (
17
) is equivalent to

(18)

\operatorname{ld}(B)>\frac{2l-7}{4l-5}.

If
l=3
then (
18
) holds and
D
is as in
(65)
. Assume
l\geqslant 4
, so
\operatorname{ld}(B)>\frac{1}{11}
, which implies that
T\in\{[2],[2,2]\}
or
T\in\{[3],[2,2,2]\}
and
m=2
. If
T=[2]
then

\operatorname{ld}(B)=\frac{1}{3m-4}
, so
m\leqslant 4
and (
18
) shows that
D
is as in
(66)
if
m=2
(so
\operatorname{ld}(B)=\frac{1}{2}
) or
(67)
if
m>2
. If
T=[2,2]
then
\operatorname{ld}(B)=\frac{1}{6m-9}
, so by (
18
) either
(m,l)=(3,4)
and
D
is as in
(68)
, or
m=2
,
l\leqslant 7
, and
D
is as in
(69)
. In the remaining cases we have
m=2
,
\operatorname{ld}(B)=\frac{1}{5}
, so
l=4
by (
18
), and
D
is as in
(70)
if
T=[3]
and as in
(71)
if
T=[2,2,2]
.

Subcase 1.2.2:

U
is a fork.
Since
U
is admissible and has a twig of length three, one of the following holds.

(a)

U=\langle\boldsymbol{k};[2],[2],[2,l,2]\rangle
or
\langle k;[2],[2],[2,\boldsymbol{l},2]\rangle
for some
k,l\geqslant 2
,
(k,l)\neq(2,2)
by Lemma
5.10
.

(b)

U=\langle\boldsymbol{k};[2],[3],[2,2,2]\rangle
or
\langle k;[2],[3],[2,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle
for some
k\geqslant 2
. Put
l=2
in this case.

Subcase 1.2.2.1:

q_{1}\in G_{1}
.
Like in Case
1.2.1.1
, applying the automorphism
(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2})\mapsto(A_{2},A_{1},A_{0})
from Remark
5.12
we can assume that the long twig of
U
meets
A_{2}
, i.e.
\beta_{D}(H_{1})=3
.

Put
\varepsilon=1
in case
(a)
and
\varepsilon=2
in case
(b)
. The proper transform of
A_{1}^{\varphi}
, which is a component of
W
, meets twigs of
W
of types
[(2)_{\varepsilon}]*[(2)_{k-2},3]
and
[(2)_{l-2},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]
. If
W
is a fork then the admissibility of
W
implies that
k=2
and
\varepsilon=1
, which is impossible. Thus
W=[(2)_{\varepsilon}]*[(2)_{k-2},3,m,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},(2)_{l-2}]
for some
m\geqslant 2
. In case
(b)
, inequality (
16
) gives
k=m=2
, so
D
is as in
(72)
. In case
(a)
we have
W=[3,(2)_{k-3},3,m,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},(2)_{l-2}]
if
k\geqslant 3
and
W=[4,m,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},(2)_{l-2}]
,
l\geqslant 3
if
k=2
. By (
16
), the first case holds and
(k,l,m)=(3,2,2)
, so
D
is as in
(73)
.

Subcase 1.2.2.2:

q_{1}\in H_{2}
.
As before, denote by
L
the connected component of
D
containing
L_{1}
.

Assume
\beta_{D}(H_{1})=3
. Suppose
L
is a fork. Let
T_{U},T_{L}
be the components of
U
and
L
meeting
A_{0}
. Since
\overline{X}
is del Pezzo, we have
0>\pi(A_{0})\cdot K_{\overline{X}}=1-\operatorname{ld}(T_{U})-\operatorname{ld}(T_{L})
by adjunction, so
\operatorname{ld}(T_{U})+\operatorname{ld}(T_{L})>1
. Passing to a weighted subgraph as in Lemma
2.4
, we can assume that one of the following holds: in case
(a)
,
k\geqslant 3
:

U=\langle 3,[\mathit{2}],[2],[2,2,2]\rangle
,
L=\langle 2;[\mathit{3},3],[2],[2]\rangle
;
in case
(a)
,
l\geqslant 3
:

U=\langle 2;[\mathit{2}],[2],[2,3,2]\rangle
,
L=\langle 2;[\mathit{4}],[2],[2]\rangle
;
and in case
(b)
:

U=\langle 2;[\mathit{3}],[2],[2,2,2]\rangle
,
L=\langle 2;[\mathit{2},4],[2],[2]\rangle
;
where the italicized number refers to
T_{U}
or
T_{L}
. In each case, we get
\operatorname{ld}(T_{U})+\operatorname{ld}(T_{L})=1
; a contradiction.

Thus
L
is a chain, so
W=[(2)_{m-2},\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},(2)_{l-2}]
for some
m\geqslant 2
. Consider the case
l\geqslant 3
, so
U
is as in
(a)
. Then inequality (
16
) gives
(k,l,m)=(2,3,2)
, and
D
is as in
(74)
. Thus we can assume
l=2
. In case
(a)
we have
k\geqslant 3
by assumption, so inequality (
16
) gives
m=2
or
(k,m)=(3,3)
, and
D
is as in
(75)
or
(76)
. In case
(b)
inequality (
16
) gives
m=2
or
(k,m)=(2,3)
, so
D
is as in
(77)
or
(78)
.

Assume
\beta_{D}(H_{1})=2
. Consider case
(a)
,
k=2
. Then
l\geqslant 3
. Write
\psi=\sigma\circ\sigma_{+}
so that
U_{\sigma}=\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,\boldsymbol{3},2]\rangle
,
W_{\sigma}=[\uline{\boldsymbol{5}}]
. If
\psi=\sigma
then
D
is as in
(79)
. Suppose
\psi\neq\sigma
, and let
\eta
be a composition of
\sigma
with a blowup at
r\in\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}
. If
r\in A_{1}
then
W_{\eta}=[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{5}}]
or
[\uline{\boldsymbol{6}}]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=\frac{1}{3}=\frac{1}{2}(1-\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\sigma}))
, contrary to (
16
). Thus
\{r\}=A_{0}\cap H_{1}
, so
U_{\eta}=\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,\boldsymbol{4},2]\rangle
and
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})=\frac{1}{5}=1-2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\sigma})
, again a contradiction with (
16
).

For the remaining cases, recall that
\overline{X}
is del Pezzo if and only
K_{\overline{X}}\cdot\pi(A_{0})<0
. It is equivalent to inequality (
17
), where
C
is the tip of
L
meeting
A_{0}
. When writing the type of
L
, we refer to
C
by a number in italics.

Suppose
l\geqslant 3
. Then case
(a)
holds and the weighted graph of
U+L
has a weighted subgraph
\langle 3;[2],[2],[2,\boldsymbol{3},2]\rangle+[\mathit{2},3,2,2]
. Using Lemma
2.4
, we get
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})+\operatorname{ld}(C)\leqslant\frac{3}{11}+\frac{8}{11}=1
, a contradiction with (
17
).

Thus
l=2
, so
U=\langle k;[2],[2],[2,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle
for some
k\geqslant 3
or
\langle k,[3],[2],[2,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle
for some
k\geqslant 2
. We compute
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})=\frac{2k-3}{4k-7}
or
\frac{6k-9}{12k-19}
, respectively. In any case
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})\leqslant\frac{3}{5}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(C)>\frac{2}{5}
by (
17
).

Assume that
L
is a chain. Then
L=[\mathit{3},m,2]
for some
m\geqslant 2
. We have
\operatorname{ld}(C)=\frac{2m}{6m-5}
, so inequality (
18
) implies that
D
is as in
(80)
–
(82)
in case
(a)
and as in
(83)
–
(84)
in case
(b)
.

Assume that
L
is a fork. If
m\geqslant 3
then the weighted graph of
L
has a weighted subgraph
\langle 3;[\mathit{3}],[2],[2]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(C)\leqslant\frac{2}{5}
, which is false. Thus
m=2
, so
L=\langle 2;[\mathit{3}],[2],T\rangle
for some admissible
T
,
d(T)\leqslant 5
. If
T=[(2)_{s}]
for some
s\geqslant 1
then
\operatorname{ld}(C)=\frac{4}{7+s}
, so inequality (
17
) implies that
D
is as in
(101)
–
(102)
in case
(a)
or as in
(103)
–
(104)
in case
(b)
. In the remaining cases, the weighted graph of
L
has a weighted subgraph
\langle 2;[\mathit{3}],[2],[3]\rangle
or
\langle 2;[\mathit{3}],[2],[3,2]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(C)\leqslant\frac{2}{5}
or
\frac{6}{17}
; a contradiction because
\operatorname{ld}(C)>\frac{2}{5}
.

Case 2:

q_{2}\in G_{2}
.

Suppose
A_{3}
contains a base point of
\varphi_{+}^{-1}
, say
q_{3}
. Since
D
has no circular subdivisor, we have
q_{3}\in L_{3}\cup H_{2}
. If
q_{3}\in H_{2}
then interchanging
{l}_{2}
with
{l}_{3}
we get back in Case
1
, so we can assume
q_{3}\in L_{3}
. Since
U
is admissible, we have
q_{1}\in H_{2}
. Now after a blowup at
q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}
we get
U=[3,\boldsymbol{2},2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=1
, a contradiction with inequality (
16
). Thus
A_{3}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\varphi_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
.

Subcase 2.1:

q_{1}\in G_{1}
.
Suppose
\tau_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
r\in A_{0}
, and let
\sigma
be a composition of
\tau
with a blowup at
r
. Then
U_{\sigma}=[2,\boldsymbol{3},2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{2}},2,3,2]
if
r\in H_{1}
and
\langle\boldsymbol{2};[2],[2],[4,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{2}},2,2]\rangle
if
r\in L_{1}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\sigma})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\sigma})=1
; a contradiction with (
16
). Thus
\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}\cup A_{2}
. Since
U
is admissible,
\tau_{+}
is a composition of
k_{j}-2\geqslant 0
blowups at
A_{j}\cap U_{\tau}
, each time on the proper transforms of
U_{\tau}
. Hence
U=[3,k_{1},\uline{\boldsymbol{2}},k_{2},2,\boldsymbol{2},2]
. If
k_{2}=2
then
D
is as in
(50)
. Otherwise,
k_{1}=2
,
k_{2}\leqslant 4
by (
16
), and
D
is as in
(51)
.

Let
V
be the proper transform of the line joining
p_{2}
with
p_{3}
. Its image
V^{\gamma}\subseteq X_{\gamma}
is a
(-1)
-curve satisfying
V^{\gamma}\cdot D_{\gamma}=3
and meeting
D_{\gamma}
on
L_{1}^{\gamma},G_{2}^{\gamma}
and
G_{3}^{\gamma}
.
Let
\pi_{\gamma}\colon X_{\gamma}\longrightarrow\overline{X}_{\gamma}
be the contraction of
D_{\gamma}
. By Lemma
5.8
(a)

\overline{X}_{\gamma}
is a del Pezzo surface. Thus
0>\pi_{\gamma}(V^{\gamma})\cdot K_{\overline{X}_{\gamma}}=2-\operatorname{ld}(L_{1}^{\gamma})-\operatorname{ld}(G_{2}^{\gamma})-\operatorname{ld}(G_{3}^{\gamma})
by adjunction. The assumption
A_{3}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\varphi_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
implies that
G_{3}^{\gamma}=[2]
is a connected component of
D_{\gamma}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(G_{3}^{\gamma})=1
. We get

(19)

\operatorname{ld}(L_{1}^{\gamma})+\operatorname{ld}(G_{2}^{\gamma})>1.

Note that both (
19
) and (
16
) are equivalent to ampleness of
-K_{\overline{X}_{\gamma}}
: in the remaining part of Case
2
, it will be more convenient use (
19
). To this end, we introduce the following notation.

Let
L_{\gamma}
,
G_{\gamma}
be the connected components of
D_{\gamma}
containing
L_{1}
and
G_{2}
, respectively. We write their types italicizing the number corresponding to
L_{1}
and
L_{3}
. For example, if
\eta
is a composition of
\varphi
with a blowup at
q_{2}
then
L_{\eta}=[\mathit{3}]
and
G_{\eta}=[\mathit{3}]
. In particular,
\operatorname{ld}(G_{2}^{\eta})=\frac{2}{3}
, so Lemma
2.4
implies that
\operatorname{ld}(G_{2}^{\gamma})\leqslant\frac{2}{3}
whenever
\gamma=\eta\circ\widetilde{\gamma}
for a morphism
\widetilde{\gamma}
. Inequality (
19
) gives
\operatorname{ld}(L_{1}^{\gamma})>\tfrac{1}{3}
.

Subcase 2.2:

q_{1}\in L_{1}
.

If both
A_{0}^{\tau}
and
A_{1}^{\tau}
contain base points of
\tau_{+}^{-1}
then after blowing up at these points,
L
becomes
[\mathit{6}]
,
[\mathit{5},2]
or
[2,\mathit{4},2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(L_{1})=\tfrac{1}{3}
, which is impossible. Thus
\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{j}\cup A_{2}
for some
j\in\{0,1\}
. Replacing, if needed,
\tau_{+}
with
\iota\circ\tau_{+}
, where
\iota\in\operatorname{Aut}(X_{\tau},D_{\tau})
is the involution
(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2})\mapsto(A_{1},A_{0},A_{2})
from Remark
5.12
, we can assume that
\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{0}\cup A_{2}
.

Let
\sigma
be a composition of
\tau
with
k_{j}-2\geqslant 0
blowups at
A_{j}\cap U_{\sigma}
,
j\in\{0,2\}
, each time on the proper transform of
U_{\tau}
, so that
A_{j}^{\sigma}\cap U_{\sigma}\not\subseteq\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}
. Since
U
is admissible, we have either
\psi=\sigma
, or
\sigma_{+}
is a single blowup at
A_{0}\cap L
. Thus
G=[\mathit{3},(2)_{k_{2}-2}]
. If
\psi=\sigma
then
L=[\mathit{4},(2)_{k_{0}-2}]
, otherwise
L=[\mathit{4},(2)_{k_{0}-3},3]
if
k_{0}\geqslant 3
or
[\mathit{5}]
if
k_{0}=2
. If
k_{2}=2
then Lemma
5.10
implies that
k_{0}\geqslant 3
, so
D
is as in
(52)
or
(85)
. If
k_{2}\geqslant 3
then inequality (
19
) implies that
\psi=\sigma
and either
k_{0}=2
or
(k_{0},k_{2})=(3,3)
, so
D
is as in
(53)
or
(54)
.

Subcase 2.3:

q_{1}\in H_{2}
.
Let
\sigma
be a composition of
\tau
with
k_{j}-2\geqslant 0
blowups over
A_{j}\cap U_{\tau}
,
j\in\{0,2\}
, each time on the proper transform of
U_{\tau}
, so that
A_{j}^{\sigma}\cap U_{\sigma}\not\subseteq\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}
. If
\psi\neq\sigma
, we denote by
\eta
a composition of
\sigma
with a blowup at a given
r\in\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}
. Note that
\{r\}=A_{0}\cap L
,
\{r\}=A_{2}\cap G
or
r\in A_{1}
.

If
r\in A_{2}
then
U_{\eta}=\langle k_{2};[2],[\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}],[2,\boldsymbol{k_{0}},2]\rangle
, so since
U
is admissible,
k_{0}=2
,
\psi=\eta
, and
D
is as in
(86)
or
(87)
. Thus we can assume
\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{0}\cup A_{1}
; hence
G=[\mathit{3},(2)_{k_{2}-2}]
and by (
19
),
\operatorname{ld}(L_{1})>1-\operatorname{ld}(G_{2})=\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{1}{4k_{2}-2}\geqslant\tfrac{1}{3}
. Assume
r\in A_{0}
. If
k_{0}\geqslant 3
then
L_{\eta}=[3,(2)_{k_{0}-3},\mathit{3},2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(L_{1})=\tfrac{k_{0}}{4k_{0}-3}\leqslant\tfrac{1}{3}
, which is false. Hence
k_{0}=2
. We have
L_{\eta}=[\mathit{4},2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(L_{1})=\tfrac{2}{5}
and
k_{2}=2
by (
19
). If
\psi=\eta
then
D
is as in
(88)
, otherwise
\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
as
U
is admissible, and after one further blowup we get
L=[\mathit{4},3,2]
or
\langle 2;[2],[2],[\mathit{4}]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(L_{1})=\tfrac{1}{3}
, a contradiction.

Therefore, we can assume
\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
. Recall that
L_{\sigma}=[(2)_{k_{0}-2},\mathit{3},2,2]
and
G_{\sigma}=[(2)_{k_{2}-2},\mathit{3}]
, so inequality (
19
) gives
k_{0}=2
;
k_{0}=3
,
k_{2}\leqslant 5
; or
k_{0}\in\{4,5,6\}
,
k_{2}\leqslant 3
. If
\psi=\sigma
then
D
is as in
(55)
,
(56)
or
(57)
.

Assume
\psi\neq\sigma
. We have
L_{\eta}=[(2)_{k_{0}-2},\mathit{3},3,2]
if
r\in L_{\sigma}
and
\langle 2;[2],[2],[(2)_{k_{0}-2},\mathit{3}]\rangle
if
r\in H_{2}
. If
k_{0}=3
then
\operatorname{ld}(L_{1}^{\eta})=\frac{1}{3}
, which is impossible; and any
k_{0}\geqslant 3
can be reduced to
k_{0}=3
by a vertical swap. Thus
k_{0}=2
. If
k_{2}=2
then
D
is as in
(89)
(note that inequality (
19
) is satisfied for any possible twig
T
, since
G=[\mathit{3}]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(G_{2})=\frac{2}{3}
; and
L_{1}
is a maximal twig of type
[3]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(L_{1})>\tfrac{1}{3}
). Assume
k_{2}\geqslant 3
, so
\operatorname{ld}(G_{2})=\frac{k_{2}}{2k_{2}-1}\leqslant\frac{3}{5}
and thus
\operatorname{ld}(L_{1})>\frac{2}{5}
by (
19
). If
r\in L_{\sigma}
then
L_{\eta}=[3,\mathit{3},2]
, so
\frac{2}{5}<\operatorname{ld}(L_{1}^{\eta})=\tfrac{5}{13}
, which is impossible. Hence
r\in H_{2}
. If
\psi=\eta
then
D
is as in
(90)
. Assume
\psi\neq\eta
, and let
\vartheta
be a composition of
\eta
with blowup at
s\in\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
. If
s\in L_{\eta}
then
L_{\vartheta}=\langle 2;[2],[3],[\mathit{3}]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(L_{1}^{\vartheta})=\tfrac{2}{5}
which is impossible. Hence
s\in H_{2}
,
L_{\vartheta}=\langle 2;[2],[2,2],[\mathit{3}]\rangle
, and
\frac{4}{9}=\operatorname{ld}(L_{1}^{\vartheta})>\frac{k_{2}-1}{2k_{2}-1}
, so
k_{2}\leqslant 4
. If
\psi=\vartheta
then
D
is as in
(91)
, otherwise after one further blowup we get
\operatorname{ld}(L_{1})=\tfrac{2}{5}
or
\tfrac{6}{17}
; a contradiction because
\operatorname{ld}(L_{1})>\frac{2}{5}
.

Case 3:

q_{2}\in L_{2}
.

Subcase 3.1:

The map
\tau_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
q_{3}\in A_{3}^{\tau}
.

If
q_{3}
lies on
H_{2}
or
G_{2}
then after interchanging
{l}_{2}
with
{l}_{3}
we get back to Case
1
or
2
, so we can assume
q_{3}\in L_{3}
. Let
\sigma
be a composition of
\tau
with a blowup at
q_{3}
.

The admissibility of
W
implies that
q_{1}\in H_{2}
. Hence
U_{\sigma}=[3,\boldsymbol{2},3]
,
W_{\sigma}=[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2,2]
. Suppose
\sigma_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
r\not\in A_{0}
, and let
\eta
be a composition of
\sigma
with a blowup at
r
.

If
r\in A_{1}
then
U_{\eta}
is still a chain
[3,\boldsymbol{2},3]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})=\tfrac{1}{2}
and therefore
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})>\tfrac{1}{4}
by (
16
). But
W_{\eta}=[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2]
if
r\in H_{2}
and
\langle\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},[2],[2,2],[2,2]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\tfrac{1}{4}
or
\tfrac{1}{7}
; a contradiction. If
r\in A_{j}
for some
j\in\{2,3\}
then
U_{\eta}+W_{\eta}=[3,\boldsymbol{2},4]+\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\rangle
if
r\in U_{\sigma}
and
[3,\boldsymbol{2},3,2]+[2,2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},3,2]
if
r\in W_{\sigma}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\eta})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\eta})=\tfrac{31}{34}
or
\tfrac{536}{551}
, contrary to (
16
). We conclude that
\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{0}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\sigma})=\tfrac{2}{5}
and therefore
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})>\tfrac{1}{5}
by (
16
). Let
\vartheta
be a composition of
\sigma
with
k-2\geqslant 0
blowups over
A_{0}\cap H_{1}
, on the proper transforms of
H_{1}
. Now
U_{\vartheta}=[3,\boldsymbol{k},3]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\vartheta})=\tfrac{2}{3k-2}
, and the inequality
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})>\tfrac{1}{5}
gives
k\in\{2,3\}
. If
\psi\neq\vartheta
then after one blowup at
\operatorname{Bs}\vartheta_{+}^{-1}=A_{0}\cap(D_{\vartheta}-H_{1})
we get
U=\langle\boldsymbol{k};[2],[3],[3]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})=\tfrac{1}{6k-7}\leqslant\tfrac{1}{5}
; a contradiction. Thus
\psi=\vartheta
, and
D
is as in
(26)
.

Subcase 3.2:

A_{3}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
.

If
q_{1}\in L_{1}
or
q_{1}\in G_{1}
then replacing
\tau_{+}
with
\iota\circ\tau_{+}
, where
\iota
is the automorphism
(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2})\mapsto(A_{1},A_{2},A_{0})
from Remark
5.12
, we get back to Case
1
. Thus we can assume
q_{1}\in H_{2}
. Suppose each of the
(-1)
-curves
A_{0},A_{1},A_{2}
contains a base point of
\tau_{+}^{-1}
, and let
\sigma
be a composition of
\tau
with a blowup at those three points. Then
U_{\sigma}=[4,\boldsymbol{3},2]
,
[2,3,\boldsymbol{3},2]
,
\langle\boldsymbol{2};[4],[2],[2]\rangle
, or
\langle\boldsymbol{2},[2,3],[2],[2]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\sigma})=\frac{1}{3}
; and
W_{\sigma}=[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},3,2]
,
[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},3,2]
,
\langle 2;[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}],[2],[2]\rangle
or
\langle 2;[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}],[2],[2]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\sigma})=\frac{1}{3}
; a contradiction with (
16
). Thus
A_{j}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
for some
j\in\{0,1,2\}
; and using the automorphism
(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2})\mapsto(A_{2},A_{0},A_{1})
from Remark
5.12
we can assume
j=2
, i.e.
\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{0}\cup A_{1}
.

If
\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{j}
for some
j\in\{0,1\}
then applying the above automorphism
(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2})\mapsto(A_{2},A_{0},A_{1})
we can assume
j=0
; so
D
is as in
(58)
or
(92)
. Note that in this case
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=\frac{4}{7}>\frac{1}{2}
, so inequality (
16
) is automatically satisfied. Thus we can assume that
\tau_{+}^{-1}
has a base point on each
A_{j}
,
j\in\{0,1\}
.

As before, denote by
L_{\gamma}
the connected component of
D_{\gamma}
containing
L_{1}
. Let
\sigma
be a composition of
\tau
with
k_{j}-2\geqslant 0
blowups over
A_{j}\cap(D_{\tau}-L_{\tau})
,
j\in\{0,1\}
, each time on the proper transforms of
D_{\tau}-L_{\tau}
, so that
A_{j}^{\sigma}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}\subseteq L_{\sigma}
. If
\psi=\sigma
then by assumption
k_{0},k_{1}\geqslant 3
, and inequality (
16
) shows that
D
is as in
(59)
–
(61)
.

Assume
\psi\neq\sigma
. Consider first the case
\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{0}
, so by assumption
k_{1}\geqslant 3
; and
U+W=\langle\boldsymbol{k_{0}};T,[3],[2]\rangle+[(2)_{k_{1}-2},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2,2]
for some admissible
T
,
d(T)\leqslant 5
. If
T=[4]
,
[(2)_{4}]
,
[3,2]
or
[2,3]
then for
(k_{0},k_{1})=(2,3)
we get
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})+2\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=1
,
1
,
\frac{181}{187}
or
\frac{241}{253}
, respectively, which contradicts (
16
). Using Lemma
2.4
, we conclude that
T=[3]
or
T=[(2)_{s}]
for some
s\in\{1,2,3\}
. If
T=[3]
then inequality (
16
) gives
(k_{0},k_{1})=(2,3)
, so
D
is as in
(93)
. If
T=[(2)_{s}]
then inequality (
16
) implies that
D
is as in
(94)
–
(98)
.

Consider now the case
\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
. Then by assumption
k_{0}\geqslant 3
and
U+W=[3,\boldsymbol{k_{0}},2]+T^{*}*[(2)_{k_{1}-2},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2,2]
for some admissible
T
, such that
L=\langle k_{1};[(2)_{k_{0}-2},3],T^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},[2]\rangle
. The admissibility of
L
implies that either
T=[2]
, or
d(T)=3
and
k_{0}=3
. In the latter case, (
16
) gives
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\frac{1}{2}(1-\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}))=\frac{4}{13}
, but we compute
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=\frac{3k_{1}-2}{12k_{1}-11}\leqslant\frac{4}{13}
if
T=[2,2]
and
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=\frac{3k-1}{12k-7}\leqslant\frac{5}{17}<\frac{4}{13}
if
T=[3]
; a contradiction. Thus the first case holds, and (
16
) shows that
D
is as in
(99)
or
(100)
.

It remains to consider the case when
A_{j}\cap L_{\sigma}
is a base point of
\sigma_{+}^{-1}
for both
j\in\{0,1\}
. Let
\eta
be a composition of
\sigma
with a blowup at those two points; so
D_{\eta}=\langle\boldsymbol{k_{0}};[3],[2],[2]\rangle
+
[3,(2)_{k_{1}-3},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}},2,2]+\langle k_{1};[4],[2],[2]\rangle+[2]
; where
[3,(2)_{-1},\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}]\coloneqq[\uline{\boldsymbol{4}}]
. We have
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\sigma})=\frac{1}{3k_{0}-4}
and
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\sigma})=\frac{k_{1}}{4k_{1}-3}\leqslant\frac{2}{5}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})>\frac{1}{5}
by (
16
); in particular
k_{0}=2
. If
\operatorname{Bs}\eta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
then
D
is as in
(105)
: note that any such
\overline{X}
is del Pezzo since
\pi(A_{0})\cdot K_{\overline{X}}=1-\operatorname{ld}(C)-\operatorname{ld}(L_{1})<1-\frac{3}{4}-\frac{1}{4}=0
, where
C
is the new
(-2)
-twig of
U
.

Assume that
\eta_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
r\in A_{0}
, and let
\vartheta
be a composition of
\eta
with a blowup at
r
. If
r\in L_{1}
then
U_{\vartheta}=\langle\boldsymbol{2},[3],[3],[2]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\vartheta})=\frac{1}{5}
, which is impossible. Thus
r\in U_{\eta}
. Now
U_{\vartheta}=\langle\boldsymbol{2},[2,2],[3],[2]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\vartheta})=\frac{1}{3}
, and the inequality
\frac{k_{1}}{4k_{1}-3}=\operatorname{ld}(H_{2}^{\vartheta})>1-2\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}^{\vartheta})=\frac{1}{3}
yields
k_{1}=2
. If
\psi=\vartheta
then
D
is as in
(106)
. Suppose
\psi\neq\vartheta
. If
\vartheta_{+}^{-1}
has a base point on
A_{0}
then after one blowup at this base point we get
U=\langle\boldsymbol{2},[2,2,2],[3],[2]\rangle
or
\langle\boldsymbol{2},[3,2],[3],[2]\rangle
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{1})=\frac{1}{5}
or
\frac{1}{17}<\frac{1}{5}
, which is impossible. Thus
\operatorname{Bs}\vartheta_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
and
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})>\frac{1}{2}(1-\operatorname{ld}(H_{1}))=\frac{1}{3}
. After one blowup at a base point of
\operatorname{Bs}\vartheta_{+}^{-1}
we get
W=[\uline{\boldsymbol{5}},2,2]
or
[2,\uline{\boldsymbol{4}},2,2]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})=\frac{4}{13}
or
\frac{5}{17}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H_{2})<\frac{1}{3}
, a contradiction.
∎

6.
Case
0pt(\overline{X})=1

In the previous sections we have classified del Pezzo surfaces of rank
1
, height
3
and width at least
2
. Thus it remains to settle the case
0pt=1
. We keep the following notation.

(20)

\overline{X}
is a log terminal del Pezzo surface of rank one such that
0pt(\overline{X})=3
and
0pt(\overline{X})=1
,
(X,D)
is its minimal log resolution and
p\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration such that
H\coloneqq D_{\mathrm{hor}}
is a
3
-section.

Recall that the condition
0pt(\overline{X})=3
means that for any
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
we have
F\cdot D\geqslant 3
for a fiber
F
; and the condition
0pt(\overline{X})=1
means that whenever the equality holds, the horizontal part
H
of
D
is irreducible. Like in Section
5
, the analysis splits into two cases, according to the separability of the morphism
p|_{H}\colon H\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
of degree
3
. Of course,
p|_{H}
can be inseparable only if
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
.

The assumption (
20
) is very restrictive. In fact, if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3
then we will see in Lemma
6.6
that a surface
\overline{X}
satisfying (
20
) and having no descendant with elliptic boundary is unique up to an isomorphism. If
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
there is no such surface; and if
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
there are only a few of them, listed in Lemma
6.7
.

This section is organized in the same way as the previous ones. First, in Lemma
6.1
we roughly describe degenerate fibers of
p
and we fix the notation. Next, in Lemma
6.2
we infer the remaining cases of Propositions
B
and
C
.
To prove Theorem
A
, we treat cases
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
and
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
separately in Sections
6C
and
6D
.

6A.
Proof of Propositions
B
and
C

Lemma 6.1

(Degenerate fibers)
.

Let
(X,D)
be as in (
20
).
Let
F_{1},\dots,F_{\nu}
be all degenerate fibers of
p
. Then each
F_{j}
has a unique
(-1)
-curve, say
L_{j}
. Let
\tau_{+}\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X_{\tau},\widecheck{D}_{\tau})
be the contraction of
\sum_{j}L_{j}
and of all non-branching vertical
(-1)
-curves in the subsequent images of
D
. Put
\widehat{F}_{j}=\tau_{+}(F_{j})_{\mathrm{red}}
,
\widehat{H}=\tau_{+}(H)
. Then for each
j\in\{1,\dots,\nu\}
one of the following holds.

(a)

\widehat{F}_{j}=[3,1,2,2]
,
\widehat{H}\cdot\widehat{F}=1
and
\widehat{H}
meets
\widehat{F}_{j}
in the
(-1)
-curve. In this case put
\mu_{j}=3
.

(b)

\widehat{F}=[2,1,2]
,
\widehat{H}\cdot\widehat{F}=2
and
\widehat{H}
meets
\widehat{F}_{j}
in a tip and in the
(-1)
-curve. In this case put
\mu_{j}=2
if
\widehat{H}
meets
\widehat{F}_{j}
normally; and
\mu_{j}=3
otherwise (i.e. when
\widehat{H}
meets
\widehat{F}_{j}
in a node).

By Lemma
2.5
(b)
each
F_{j}
has only one
(-1)
-curve, say
L_{j}
, and by Lemma
2.5
(a)
,
(F_{j})_{\mathrm{red}}-L_{j}\subseteq D
.

Suppose
\widehat{F}_{j}=[0]
. Then
[
PP24a
, Lemma 2.8(c)]
implies that
\widehat{F}_{j}\subseteq\widecheck{D}_{\tau}
, so since
\widehat{D}_{\tau}
is snc,
\widehat{H}
meets
\widehat{F}_{j}
in three points. As
\tau_{+}^{-1}
has only one base point on
\widehat{F}_{j}
, namely the image of
L_{j}
, the proper transform of
\widehat{F}_{j}
is a component of
D
meeting
H
at least twice; a contradiction.

Suppose
\widehat{F}_{j}=[1,1]
. Since
F_{j}
has only one
(-1)
-curve, the unique base point of
\tau_{+}^{-1}
on
\widehat{F}_{j}
is its node. In particular,
\widehat{F}_{j}\subseteq\widecheck{D}_{\tau}
. Since
\widecheck{D}_{\tau}
is snc,
\widehat{H}
meets
\widehat{F}_{j}
normally, so it meets one of the tips of
\widehat{F}_{j}
at least twice. The proper transform of that tip is a component of
D
meeting
H
at least twice; a contradiction as before.

Therefore,
\widehat{F}_{j}
has a unique
(-1)
-curve, say
\widehat{L}_{j}
, so
\widehat{L}_{j}
has multiplicity
\mu_{j}\geqslant 2
in
\widehat{F}_{j}
. By the definition of
\tau_{+}
, we have
3\leqslant\beta_{\widecheck{D}_{\tau}}(\widehat{L}_{j})=\widehat{L}_{j}\cdot\widehat{H}+\beta_{(\widehat{F}_{j})_{\mathrm{red}}}(\widehat{L}_{j})\leqslant\tfrac{1}{\mu_{j}}F_{j}\cdot H+2<4
, so
\beta_{(\widehat{F}_{j})_{\mathrm{red}}}(\widehat{L}_{j})=2
and
\widehat{L}_{j}\cdot\widehat{H}=1
. It follows that
\mu_{j}\leqslant 3
and
\widehat{F}_{j}

is as in
(a)
if
\mu_{j}=3
and as in
(b)
if
\mu_{j}=2
.

∎

Lemma 6.2

(Propositions
B
and
C
, case
0pt=1
)
.

We keep the notation and assumptions of Lemma
6.1
. Then the following hold.

(a)

We have
\nu=3
, i.e.
p
has exactly three degenerate fibers. One can order them so that
F_{1}
is as in
6.1
(a)
.

(b)

There is a birational morphism
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
such that
\psi_{*}D={q}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{3}
, where
{q}\coloneqq\psi_{*}H
is a cubic with a cusp
p_{1}
, and for each
j\in\{1,2,3\}
,
{l}_{j}\coloneqq\psi_{*}F_{j}
is a line passing through a point
p_{0}\not\in{q}
, tangent to
{q}
with multiplicity
\mu_{j}
at some point
p_{j}
.

(c)

One of the following holds.

(i)

\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3
and
\mu_{j}=2
for
j\in\{2,3\}
,

(ii)

\operatorname{char}{k}=3
and
\mu_{j}=3
for
j\in\{2,3\}
.

(d)

The minimalization
\psi
from
(b)
factors as
\psi=\varphi\circ\varphi_{+}
, where
\varphi_{+}\colon(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(Y,D_{Y})
is a vertical swap onto the minimal log resolution of one of the surfaces from Example
3.4
.

Let
\nu_{3}
,
\nu_{2}
be the number of degenerate fibers
F_{j}
of
p
such that
\widehat{F}_{j}
is as in Lemma
6.1
(a)
and
(b)
, respectively. Clearly,
\nu=\nu_{2}+\nu_{3}
.
Let
\overline{\varphi}_{+}
be the composition of
\tau_{+}
with the contraction of all vertical
(-1)
-curves in the subsequent images of
D
which meet the image of
H
once, normally. Put
\overline{F}_{j}=(\overline{\varphi}_{*}F_{j})_{\mathrm{red}}
,
\overline{H}=\overline{\varphi}(H)
. Note that
\overline{F}_{j}=[0]
is the image of a tip of
\widehat{F}_{j}
, so the proper transform
V_{j}\coloneqq\overline{\varphi}_{*}^{-1}\overline{F}_{j}
is a component of
D
. Put
\widehat{V}_{j}=\tau_{+}(V_{j})
. By the definition of
\mu_{j}
, we have
(\overline{F}_{j}\cdot\overline{H})_{q_{j}}=\mu_{j}
for some point
q_{j}\in\overline{F}_{j}
.

The curve
\overline{H}
is a smooth, rational
3
-section on a Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{m}
for some
m\geqslant 0
. Write
\overline{H}\sim af+3C
, where
f
is, fiber,
C
is a section of self-intersection
m
, and
a=\overline{H}\cdot\Xi_{m}\geqslant 0
, where
\Xi_{m}
is the negative section. By adjunction
-2=\overline{H}\cdot(\overline{H}+K_{\mathbb{F}_{m}})=4a-6m-6
, so
m=\tfrac{2}{3}(1-a)\leqslant\tfrac{2}{3}
. Thus
(m,a)=(0,1)
, so
\overline{H}^{2}=6
and

(21)

\widehat{H}^{2}=6-2\nu_{2}-3\nu_{3}.

(a)
We need to show that
\nu=3
and
\nu_{3}\geqslant 1
. The pullback of the other projection of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
such that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of
\nu+1

1
-sections, namely
H
and
V_{1},\dots,V_{\nu}
. Since
0pt(\overline{X})=1
, we get
\nu\geqslant 3
.

Suppose
\nu\geqslant 4
. Formula (
21
) gives
\widehat{H}^{2}=6-2\nu-\nu_{3}\leqslant-2
. Thus
\tau_{+}
defines a vertical swap
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X_{\tau},D_{\tau})
, where
D_{\tau}
equals
\widecheck{D}_{\tau}
minus all vertical
(-1)
-curves. By Lemma
2.7
(b)

(X_{\tau},D_{\tau})
is the minimal log resolution of some del Pezzo surface of rank one. Applying (
21
) again, we get
-\widehat{H}^{2}=3\nu-6-\nu_{2}
. By Lemma
6.1
(b)
,
\widehat{H}
meets a
(-2)
-tip of each of the
\nu_{2}
fibers of type
[2,1,2]
. Thus
\nu_{2}=\beta_{D_{\tau}}(\widehat{H})\leqslant 3
, and for
\nu_{2}=0,1,2
or
3
the connected component of
D_{\tau}
containing
\widehat{H}
is of type
[\boldsymbol{3\nu-6}]
,
[2,\boldsymbol{3\nu-7}]
,
[2,\boldsymbol{3\nu-8},2]
or
\langle\boldsymbol{3\nu-9},[2],[2],[2]\rangle
, respectively. As usual, the bold number refers to
\widehat{H}
. Lemma
2.4
implies that in any case
\operatorname{ld}(\widehat{H})\leqslant\frac{2}{3\nu-6}\leqslant\frac{1}{3}
, a contradiction with Lemma
2.6
.

Thus
\nu=3
. Suppose
\nu_{3}=0
, so
\nu_{2}=3
. By Lemma
6.1
(b)
,
\widehat{H}
meets each
\widehat{F}_{j}
once in a
(-2)
-tip
\widehat{V}_{j}
and once in a
(-1)
-curve, call it
A_{j}
. In particular,
H
meets
V_{j}
for all
j\in\{1,2,3\}
. It follows that the connected component of
D
containing
H
, call it
U
, is an admissible fork, with a branching component
H
and maximal twigs, say,
T_{j}
, meeting
H
in
V_{j}
. Moreover, by the definition of
\tau_{+}
either
T_{j}=V_{j}
and
\tau_{+}
is an isomorphism near
F_{j}
, or
T_{j}=(\tau_{+}^{-1})_{*}\widehat{F}_{j}=[2,2,2]
and
(F_{j})_{\mathrm{red}}\wedge\operatorname{Exc}\tau_{+}
is a
(-1)
-tip of
(F_{j})_{\mathrm{red}}
. Indeed, otherwise the proper transform of
A_{j}
would be a branching component of
T_{j}
, which is impossible. Since
U
is an admissible fork, we have
T_{j}=[2,2,2]
for at most one
j
. Thus
\#\operatorname{Exc}\tau_{+}\leqslant 1
, so
H^{2}\geqslant\widehat{H}^{2}-1=-1
by (
21
); a contradiction.

(b)
Part
(a)
implies that
(\overline{F}_{1}\cdot\overline{H})_{q_{1}}=3
. Let
\eta\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a composition of a blowup at
q_{1}
and the contraction of the proper transforms of the horizontal and vertical lines through
q_{1}
. Clearly,
\psi\coloneqq\eta\circ\overline{\varphi}
is a morphism. Now
{q}\coloneqq\psi_{*}H
is a cubic with a cusp at the image of
V_{1}
, call it
p_{1}
; and
{l}_{j}\coloneqq\psi_{*}F_{j}
are lines meeting at
p_{0}\not\in{q}
. We have
({l}_{1}\cdot{q})_{p_{1}}=3
and
({l}_{j}\cdot{q})_{p_{j}}=\mu_{j}
for
j\in\{2,3\}
, where
p_{j}\coloneqq\eta(q_{j})
.

(c)
The morphism
p|_{H}\colon H\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
of degree three is ramified at
H\cap F_{j}
with multiplicity
\mu_{j}
. If
p|_{H}
is not separable, then
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
and
(c)(c.ii)
holds. Assume
p|_{H}
is separable. By the Hurwitz formula, see
[
Har77
, Corollary IV.2.4]
, we have
\sum_{j=1}^{3}\mu_{j}\leqslant 7
. Since
\mu_{1}=3
, we get
\mu_{2}=\mu_{3}=2
. By
[
PP24b
, Lemma 5.5]
a configuration
{q}+{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{3}
as in
(b)
with
({l}_{j}\cdot{q})_{p_{j}}=\mu_{j}
exists only if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3
, so
(c)(c.i)
holds.

(d)
By
(a)
,
\nu_{2}\leqslant 2
. If
\nu_{2}\leqslant 1
then
\mu_{j}=3
for some
j\in\{2,3\}
, so case
(c)(c.ii)
holds, and
\tau_{+}
defines a vertical swap onto a log surface as in Example
3.4
(b)
if
\nu_{2}=1
and
3.4
(c)
if
\nu_{2}=0
.

Assume
\nu_{2}=2
. Formula (
21
) gives
\widehat{H}^{2}=-1
. If
\operatorname{Bs}\tau_{+}^{-1}\subseteq\widehat{F}_{1}
then
\widehat{V}_{2}+\widehat{H}+\widehat{V}_{3}=[2,1,2]
supports a fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration whose pullback to
X
has heoght
2
with respect to
D
, which is impossible. Hence the
(-1)
-curve in, say,
\widehat{F}_{2}
contains a base point of
\tau_{+}^{-1}
, call it
r
. It follows from definition of
\tau_{+}
that
\widehat{H}
meets
\widehat{F}_{2}
in two points, i.e.
\mu_{2}=2
, so case
(c)(c.i)
holds, see Figure
4(a)
. It remains to prove that
r\in\widehat{H}
. Suppose the contrary. Since
D
has no circular subdivisor, we have
r\in\widehat{V}_{2}
. Since
H^{2}<-1=\widehat{H}^{2}
, the curve
\widehat{H}
contains another base point of
\tau_{+}^{-1}
, say
s
; and interchanging
{l}_{2}
with
{l}_{3}
if necessary, we can assume
\{s\}=\widehat{H}\cap\widehat{F}_{1}
. Write
\tau_{+}=\sigma\circ\sigma_{+}
, where
\sigma
is a blowup at
r
and
s
. Then
H^{\sigma}\coloneqq\sigma_{+}(H)
is a
(-2)
-curve, so
\sigma_{+}
defines a vertical swap
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X_{\sigma},D_{\sigma})
. By Lemma
2.7
(b)
,
(X_{\sigma},D_{\sigma})
is a minimal resolution of some del Pezzo surface of rank one. The connected component of
D_{\sigma}
containing
H^{\sigma}
is of type
\langle\boldsymbol{2},[2],[3],[2,2]\rangle
with branching component
H^{\sigma}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H^{\sigma})=\tfrac{1}{3}
; a contradiction with Lemma
2.6
.

∎

Given the above results, the proofs of Propositions
B
and
C
can now be completed as follows.

Let
\overline{X}
be a del Pezzo surface of rank one and height
3
, and let
(X,D)
be its minimal log resolution. f
0pt(\overline{X})=3
then
\overline{X}
satisfies the assumption (
5
), so by Lemma
4.6
the minimalization
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
from Lemma
4.2
is as in Proposition
B
(a)
. If
0pt(\overline{X})=2
then
\overline{X}
satisfies (
14
), so Lemma
5.4
provides a minimalization
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
as in Proposition
B
(b)
. Eventually, if
0pt(\overline{X})=1
then the condition (
20
) holds, and by Lemma
6.2
(c)
the minimalization
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
from
6.2
(b)
is as in Proposition
B
(c)
.

∎

Like before, let
\overline{X}
be a del Pezzo surface of rank one and height
3
, let
(X,D)
be its minimal log resolution. If
0pt(\overline{X})=3
then by Lemma
4.7
(c)
the surface
\overline{X}
vertically swaps to one of the canonical surfaces from Example
3.1
, so Proposition
C
(a)(a.i)
holds. If
0pt(\overline{X})=2
then by Lemma
5.6

\overline{X}
vertically swaps to a surface from Example
3.2
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
or
3.3
if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, so
C
(a)(a.ii)
,
(b)(b.i)
or
C
(b)(b.iv)
holds, respectively. Eventually, if
0pt(\overline{X})=1
then by Lemma
6.2
(c)
we have
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
; and by Lemma
6.2
(d)

\overline{X}
vertically swaps to a surface from Example
3.4
(a)
. By Proposition
3.6
(a)
the surfaces from Example
3.4
(a)
,
(b)
have descendants with elliptic boundary. We conclude that
C
(b)(b.ii)
,
(b)(b.iii)
or
C
(c)
holds, as claimed.

∎

6B.
Further restrictions and notation

We now reconstruct the vertical swap
\varphi_{+}
from Lemma
6.2
(d)
. To do this, we first introduce Notation
6.3
which is analogous to
4.8
and
5.7
. Next, we prove Lemma
6.4
, which, just like Lemma
4.11
or
5.8
, asserts that recovering
\varphi_{+}
is a combinatorial task: simply blow up at one of the common points of a vertical
(-1)
-curve and a boundary, as long as the inequality (
22
) is satisfied. In particular, we will see that a surface
\overline{X}
as in (
20
) is determined uniquely, up to an isomorphism, by its singularity type.

Notation 6.3

(Recovering the minimalization
\psi
, cf. Notations
4.8
,
5.7
)
.

Let
(X,D)
be as in (
20
). Let
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the minimalization from Lemma
6.2
(b)
, and let
\psi=\varphi\circ\varphi_{+}
be its factorization given by Lemma
6.2
(d)
. We study factorizations
\psi=\gamma\circ\gamma_{+}
, where
\gamma
is a composition of
\varphi
with some morphism and
\gamma_{+}\colon(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X_{\gamma},D_{\gamma})
is a vertical swap. For any such factorization we put
H^{\gamma}=(D_{\gamma})_{\mathrm{hor}}
and denote by
U_{\gamma}
the connected component of
D_{\gamma}
containing
H^{\gamma}
. For
j=1,2,3
we denote by
A_{j}^{\gamma}
the unique
(-1)
-curve in
\gamma^{-1}(p_{j})
, so
\widecheck{D}_{\gamma}\coloneqq\gamma_{+}(D)=D_{\gamma}+\sum_{j}A_{j}^{\gamma}
. We put
L_{j}=\varphi^{-1}_{*}{l}_{j}
. As usual, we skip the superscript
\gamma
if it is clear from the context.

Lemma 6.4

(Recovering
\psi
step by step, cf. Lemmas
4.11
and
5.8
)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a del Pezzo surface as in (
20
); i.e. of height
3
and width
1
. For every factorization
\psi=\gamma\circ\gamma_{+}
as in Notation
6.3
, the following hold.

(a)

The log surface
(X_{\gamma},D_{\gamma})
is a minimal resolution of an lt del Pezzo surface of rank one. In particular,

(22)

\operatorname{ld}(H^{\gamma})>\frac{1}{3}.

(b)

Every base point of
\gamma_{+}^{-1}
is a common point of
D_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{reg}}
and of a vertical
(-1)
-curve
A_{j}^{\gamma}
for some
j\in\{1,2,3\}
.

(c)

The isomorphism class of the log surface
(X,\widecheck{D})
is uniquely determined by the weighted graph of
\widecheck{D}
.

(d)

Put
h^{i}=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
. Then
h^{0}=h^{1}=0
and
h^{2}=0,1,2
if
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to a surface from Example
3.4
(a)
,
(b)
,
(c)
, respectively; see Lemma
6.2
(d)
.

The proof is analogous to the proof of Lemma
4.11
, so we give only a sketch. Part
(a)
follows from Lemma
2.7
(b)
and Lemma
2.6
. Part
(b)
is a consequence of the admissibility of
U
. Indeed, if
(b)
fails then some base point of
\gamma_{+}^{-1}
lies on
A_{j}^{\gamma}\setminus D_{\gamma}
for some
j\in\{1,2,3\}
and looking at Figure
4
we see that the proper transform of
A_{j}^{\gamma}
is either a component of a circular subdivisor of
U
, or a branching component of
U
in case
U
is not an admissible fork. Parts
(c)
and
(d)
follow from
(b)
and
[
PP24a
, Lemma 2.18]
in the same way as Lemma
4.11
(f)
,
(g)
follows from
4.11
(b)
. Indeed, by
(b)
the morphism
(X,\widecheck{D})\longrightarrow(Y,\widecheck{D}_{Y})
is inner, where
(Y,D_{Y})
is as in Example
3.4
and
\widecheck{D}_{Y}
is the sum of
D_{Y}
and
(-1)
-curves shown there. Since by Proposition
3.6
(d)
the latter is unique, so is
(X,\widecheck{D})
. The number
h^{i}
equals
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))
, which is listed in Proposition
3.6
(f)
.

∎

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\overline{X}
be as in (
20
), and assume that
\overline{X}
has no descendant with elliptic boundary. Then the connected component
U
of
D
containing
H
is neither a
(-2)
-chain nor a
(-2)
-fork.

Suppose the contrary. If
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
then
\varphi_{+}=\mathrm{id}
,
i.e.
\overline{X}
is the surface from Example
3.4
(a)
, which by Proposition
3.6
(a)
has a descendant with elliptic boundary; a contradiction. Thus
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
. Now
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to the surface from Example
3.4
(b)
, and, after interchanging
F_{1}
with
F_{2}
if necessary (cf. Lemmas
3.7
and
3.10
) we have
\operatorname{Bs}\varphi_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{2}^{\varphi}\cap L_{2}^{\varphi}
, see Figure
4(b)
. In particular,
\varphi_{+}
is an isomorphism near the proper transform of the line joining
p_{1}
with
p_{2}
, which therefore remains an elliptic tie;
a contradiction.

∎

6C.
The list of singularity types: case
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3

Lemma 6.6

(Classification in case
0pt=1
,
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
, see Table
4
)
.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
. Assume that
\overline{X}
is a del Pezzo surface of rank one, height
3
, and width
1
, and
\overline{X}
has no descendant with elliptic boundary. Then
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
and the following hold.

(
1
)

The surface
\overline{X}
is unique up to an isomorphism.

(
2
)

The minimal log resolution
(X,D)
of
\overline{X}
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p
such that
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to the surface from Example
3.4
(a)
. The combinatorial type of
(X,D,p)
is

[2,2,2^{|1|},3]+[2,2^{|2|},2,\boldsymbol{3}^{|1,2,3|},2^{|3|}]+[2]^{|3|},

where the numbers decorated by
|j|
correspond to components meeting the
j
-th vertical
(-1)
-curve, and the bold number corresponds to the horizontal component of
D
, see Section
2G
.

We use Notation
6.3
. Lemma
6.5
implies that
\psi\neq\varphi
. Let
\sigma
be a composition of
\varphi
with a blowup at
r\in\operatorname{Bs}\varphi_{+}^{-1}
. Recall from Lemma
6.4
(b)
that
r\in D_{\varphi}\cap A_{j}^{\varphi}
for some
j\in\{1,2,3\}
.

Suppose
r\in A_{3}
. We have
r\in L_{1}\cup H
, because
D
has no circular subdivisor. If
r\in L_{1}
then
U_{\sigma}
is a non-admissible fork
\langle\boldsymbol{2};[3],[2,2],[2,2,2]\rangle
, which is impossible. Thus
\{r\}=A_{3}\cap H
. Now
U_{\sigma}=[(2)_{3},\boldsymbol{3},(2)_{3}]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H^{\sigma})=\tfrac{1}{3}
; a contradiction with inequality (
22
).
Suppose
r\in A_{2}
. Then
U_{\sigma}=\langle 2;[2],[2],[2,\boldsymbol{3},2]\rangle
if
r\in H
and
\langle\boldsymbol{2};[2],[2],[2,3,2]\rangle
otherwise, so
\operatorname{ld}(H^{\sigma})=\tfrac{1}{3}
; again a contradiction with (
22
).

Therefore,
\operatorname{Bs}\varphi_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
. The admissibility of
U
implies that
r\in H
. If
\psi=\sigma
then
(2)
holds. Suppose
\psi\neq\sigma
, and let
\eta
be a composition of
\sigma
with a blowup at some
s\in\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
. Now
U_{\eta}=[2,\boldsymbol{4},2,2,2]
if
s\in H
and
\langle\boldsymbol{3},[2],[2],[2,2,2]\rangle
otherwise, hence
\operatorname{ld}(H^{\eta})=\tfrac{3}{11}<\frac{1}{3}
or
\tfrac{1}{5}<\frac{1}{3}
; a contradiction with (
22
).

We conclude that the surface
\overline{X}
is obtained by an elementary vertical swap from the surface constructed in Example
3.4
(a)
. Such a surface has no descendant with elliptic boundary by Lemma
2.13
. It has height
3
and width
1
, because its singularity type does not appear in the classification
[
PP24a
]
, or in Lemmas
4.12
,
5.15
. It is unique up to an isomorphism by Lemma
6.4
(c)
.

∎

6D.
The list of singularity types: case
\operatorname{char}{k}=3

Lemma 6.7

(Classification in case
0pt=1
,
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
, see Table
5
)
.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
. Let
\mathcal{S}
be a singularity type of a log terminal surface. Let
\overline{X}
be a del Pezzo surface of rank
1
, height
3
, width
1
and type
\mathcal{S}
, i.e.
\overline{X}\in\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})
, see Notation
2.14
. Assume
\overline{X}
has no descendant with elliptic boundary. Then
\mathcal{S}
is one of the types listed below, and the following hold.

(
1
)

We have
\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})=1
, i.e.
\overline{X}
is unique up to an isomorphism.

(
2
)

The minimal log resolution
(X,D)
of
\overline{X}
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p
such that
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to a surface
\overline{Y}
from Example
3.4
(b)
or
3.4
(c)
, and the combinatorial type of
(X,D,p)
is one of the following.

(a)

\overline{Y}
is of type
4\cdot[3]+3\mathrm{A}_{2}
, see Example
3.4
(c)
, and
(X,D,p)
is one of the following:

(1)

[\boldsymbol{3}]^{|1,2,3|}+[2,2]^{|1|}+\prescript{|1|}{}{[}3]+[2,2]^{|2|}+\prescript{|2|}{}{[}3]+[2,2]^{|3|}+\prescript{|3|}{}{[}3]
,

(2)

\prescript{|2,3|}{}{[}\boldsymbol{4},(2)_{k-2}]^{|1|}+[2,2,k^{|1|},3]+[2,2]^{|2|}+\prescript{|2|}{}{[}3]+[2,2]^{|3|}+\prescript{|3|}{}{[}3]
,
k\geqslant 2
,

(3)

[\boldsymbol{5}]^{|1,2,3|}+\langle 2;[2]^{|1|},[3],[2,2]\rangle+[2,2]^{|2|}+\prescript{|2|}{}{[}3]+[2,2]^{|3|}+\prescript{|3|}{}{[}3]
,

(4)

\prescript{|2,3|}{}{[}\boldsymbol{4},(2)_{k-3},3]^{|1|}+\langle k;[2]^{|1|},[3],[2,2]\rangle+[2,2]^{|2|}+\prescript{|2|}{}{[}3]+[2,2]^{|3|}+\prescript{|3|}{}{[}3]
,
k\geqslant 3
,

(5)

[\boldsymbol{5}]^{|1,2,3|}+[2,2,2^{|1|},3]+[2,2,2^{|2|},3]+[2,2]^{|3|}+\prescript{|3|}{}{[}3]
,

(6)

[2,2,2,\boldsymbol{4}]^{|1,2,3|}+[2,2,2^{|1|},3]+[4]^{|2|}+[2,2]^{|3|}+\prescript{|3|}{}{[}3]
,

(7)

[3,2,\boldsymbol{4}]^{|1,2,3|}+[2,2,2^{|1|},3]+[2,3]^{|2|}+[2,2]^{|3|}+\prescript{|3|}{}{[}3]
,

(8)

[3,k^{|1|},\boldsymbol{3}]^{|2,3|}+[2,3,(2)_{k-2}]^{|1|}+[2,2]^{|2|}+[3]^{|2|}+[2,2]^{|3|}+\prescript{|3|}{}{[}3]
,
k\geqslant 2
,

(9)

\langle 2;[2]^{|1|},[3],[\boldsymbol{3}]^{|2,3|}\rangle+[2,4]^{|1|}+[2,2]^{|2|}+[3]^{|2|}+[2,2]^{|3|}+\prescript{|3|}{}{[}3]
,

(10)

\langle k;[2]^{|1|},[3],[\boldsymbol{3}]^{|2,3|}\rangle+[2,3,(2)_{k-3},3]^{|1|}+[2,2]^{|2|}+[3]^{|2|}+[2,2]^{|3|}+\prescript{|3|}{}{[}3]
,
k\geqslant 3
,

(b)

\overline{Y}
is of type
2\cdot[3]+\mathrm{A}_{1}+3\mathrm{A}_{2}
, see Example
3.4
(b)
, and
(X,D,p)
is one of the following:

(11)

[2^{|3|},\boldsymbol{3}^{|2,3|},(2)_{k-2}]^{|1|}+[2,2,k^{|1|},3]+[2,2]^{|2|}+\prescript{|2|}{}{[}3]+[2]^{|3|}
,
k\geqslant 2
,

(12)

[2^{|3|},\boldsymbol{4}]^{|1,2,3|}+\langle 2;[2]^{|1|},[3],[2,2]\rangle+[2,2]^{|2|}+\prescript{|2|}{}{[}3]+[2]^{|3|}
,

(13)

[2^{|3|},\boldsymbol{3}^{|2,3|},(2)_{k-3},3]^{|1|}+\langle k;[2]^{|1|},[3],[2,2]\rangle+[2,2]^{|2|}+\prescript{|2|}{}{[}3]+[2]^{|3|}
,
k\geqslant 3
,

(14)

[\boldsymbol{4}^{|1,2,3|},2^{|3|}]+[2,2,2^{|1|},3]+[2,2,2^{|2|},3]+[2]^{|3|}
,

(15)

[2,2,2,\boldsymbol{3}^{|1,2,3|},2^{|3|}]+[2,2,2^{|1|},3]+[4]^{|2|}+[2]^{|3|}
,

(16)

[3,2,\boldsymbol{3}^{|1,2,3|},2^{|3|}]+[2,2,2^{|1|},3]+[2,3]^{|2|}+[2]^{|3|}
,

(17)

[2,2,k^{|1|},\boldsymbol{2}^{|2,3|},2^{|3|}]+[4,(2)_{k-2}]^{|1|}+[2,2]^{|2|}+[3]^{|2|}+[2]^{|3|}
,
k\geqslant 3
,

(18)

\langle k;[2]^{|1|},[2,2],[2^{|3|},\boldsymbol{2}]^{|2,3|}\rangle+[4,(2)_{k-3},3]^{|1|}+[2,2]^{|2|}+[3]^{|2|}+[2]^{|3|}
,
k\geqslant 3
.

As in the proof of Lemmas
4.12
,
5.15
or
5.16
, we first note that the classification
(2)
implies the uniqueness result
(1)
. Fix a singularity type
\mathcal{S}
as in the above list. Lemma
2.13
and a direct application of
[
PP24a
, Theorem E]
in case
(1)
, shows that no surface
\overline{X}\in\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})
has a descendant with elliptic boundary. Hence by
(2)
, the minimal log resolution
(X,D)
of
\overline{X}
has a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p
as above. Since
\mathcal{S}
appears exactly once on the above list, it uniquely determines the combinatorial type of
(X,D,p)
. The latter uniquely determines the isomorphism class of
\overline{X}
by Lemma
6.4
(c)
, so
\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})\leqslant 1
. We claim that the equality holds. Clearly, a triple
(X,D,p)
of each combinatorial type above exists. We check that it satisfies the inequality (
3
), so
(X,D)
is the minimal log resolution of a del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}
of rank 1. We have
0pt(\overline{X})=3
because
\mathcal{S}
does not appear in
[
PP24a
]
, and
0pt(\overline{X})=1
since
\mathcal{S}
does not appear in Lemmas
4.12
or
5.15
. Thus
\overline{X}\in\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})
, as needed.

Therefore, it is enough to prove
(2)
, i.e. to verify the completeness of the above list.

We use Notation
6.3
. By Lemma
6.2
(d)
we have a factorization
\psi=\varphi\circ\varphi_{+}
, where
\varphi_{+}\colon(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X_{\varphi},D_{\varphi})
is a vertical swap onto the minimal log resolution of a surface
\overline{Y}
from Example
3.4
(b)
or
(c)
. Put
\varepsilon=1
in the first case and
\varepsilon=0
in the second case. By the definition of
\varphi_{+}
we have
\operatorname{Bs}\varphi_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}^{\varphi}\cup A_{2}^{\varphi}\cup A_{3}^{\varphi}
if
\varepsilon=0
and
\operatorname{Bs}\varphi_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}^{\varphi}\cup A_{2}^{\varphi}
if
\varepsilon=1
. In any case,
\operatorname{Bs}\varphi_{+}^{-1}\subseteq D_{\varphi}
by Lemma
6.4
(b)
. For any factorization
\psi=\gamma\circ\gamma_{+}
as in Notation
6.3
, we denote by
T_{j}^{\gamma}
,
G_{j}^{\gamma}
the proper transforms of the
(-3)
- and
(-2)
-curve meeting
A_{j}^{\varphi}
.

If
\psi=\varphi
then, since
U
is not a
(-2)
-chain by Lemma
6.5
, we have
\varepsilon=0
and
D
is as in
(1)
. Assume
\psi\neq\varphi
. Reordering the degenerate fibers, if needed, we can assume that
\varphi_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
q\in A_{1}
. Let
\upsilon
be a composition of
\varphi
with some
k-1\geqslant 1
blowups over
q
, on the proper transforms of
A_{1}^{\varphi}
. By Lemma
5.8
(b)
we can choose
\upsilon
so that
\operatorname{Bs}\upsilon_{+}^{-1}\cap A_{1}^{\upsilon}\subseteq U_{\upsilon}
. If
\psi\neq\upsilon
then for a given
r\in\operatorname{Bs}\upsilon_{+}^{-1}
we denote by
\sigma
a composition of
\upsilon
with a blowup at
r
; and similarly, if
\psi\neq\sigma
we let
\eta
be a composition of
\sigma
with a blowup at
s\in\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}
.

Case 1:

q\in H^{\tau}
.
If
\psi=\upsilon
then
D
is as in
(2)
if
\varepsilon=0
and as in
(11)
if
\varepsilon=1
. Assume
\psi\neq\upsilon
and fix
r\in\operatorname{Bs}\upsilon_{+}^{-1}
.

Subcase 1.1:

r\in A_{1}
.
By the definition of
\upsilon
we have
\{r\}=A_{1}\cap U_{\upsilon}
. Assume
\psi=\sigma
. If
k=2
then
D
is as in
(3)
or as in
(12)
, and if
k\geqslant 3
then
D
is as in
(4)
or as in
(13)
. Assume
\psi\neq\sigma
. Since all forks in
D
are admissible, we have
s\not\in A_{1}
. After possibly interchanging
F_{2}
with
F_{3}
in case
\varepsilon=0
we get
s\in A_{2}
. If
s\in U_{\sigma}
then
U_{\eta}=[2]*[(2)_{k-2},\boldsymbol{5-\varepsilon},(2)_{\varepsilon}]
; so
\operatorname{ld}(H^{\eta})=\frac{k-1}{4k-5}\geqslant\frac{1}{3}
if
\varepsilon=0
and
\frac{2k-1}{10k-11}\geqslant\frac{1}{3}
if
\varepsilon=1
; contrary to (
22
). If
s\in T_{2}
then
U_{\eta}=[2]*[(2)_{k-2},\boldsymbol{4},2,2,2]
if
\varepsilon=0
;
[2,\boldsymbol{4},2,2,2]
if
(\varepsilon,k)=(1,2)
and
\langle\boldsymbol{3};[2],[3],[2,2,2]\rangle
otherwise, so
\operatorname{ld}(H^{\eta})=\frac{2k+1}{18k-19},\frac{3}{11}
, or
\frac{1}{17}
; contrary to (
22
). Thus
s\in G_{2}
so
U_{\eta}=[2]*[(2)_{k-2},\boldsymbol{4},2,3]
if
\varepsilon=0
,
[2,\boldsymbol{4},2,3]
if
(\varepsilon,k)=(1,2)
and
\langle\boldsymbol{3},[2],[3],[3,2]\rangle
otherwise, so
\operatorname{ld}(H^{\eta})=\frac{k+1}{12k-13},\frac{7}{29}
or
\frac{1}{47}
; a contradiction with (
22
).

Subcase 1.2:

A_{1}\cap\operatorname{Bs}\upsilon_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
.
After possibly interchanging
F_{2}
with
F_{3}
in case
\varepsilon=0
, we can assume
r\in A_{2}
.

Suppose
k=3
. Then
U_{\sigma}=[(2)_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{5-\varepsilon},2]
if
r\in H
;

U_{\sigma}=[2,\boldsymbol{4},2,2,2]
or
\langle\boldsymbol{3};[2],[2],[2,2,2]\rangle
if
r\in T_{2}
; and
U_{\sigma}=[2,\boldsymbol{4},2,3]
or
\langle\boldsymbol{3};[2],[2],[3,2]\rangle
if
r\in G_{2}
. For such
U_{\sigma}
, the number
\operatorname{ld}(H^{\sigma})
equals
\tfrac{1}{3}
;
\tfrac{3}{11}
,
\tfrac{1}{5}
and
\tfrac{7}{29}
,
\tfrac{1}{7}
, so (
22
) fails. If
k\geqslant 4
, we get a contradiction with (
22
) after a vertical swap to the previous case
k\geqslant 3
. Thus
k=2
. If
\psi=\sigma
then
D
is as in
(5)
or
(14)
if
r\in H
, as in
(6)
or
(15)
if
r\in T_{2}
, and as in
(7)
,
(16)
if
r\in G_{2}
.

Assume
\psi\neq\sigma
. If
r\in H
then reordering the fibers, if needed we get back to the Case
1.1
, with
\{s\}=A_{2}\cap H_{1}
.
Thus we can assume
H\cap\operatorname{Bs}\sigma_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
.

Subcase 1.2.1:

r\in T_{2}
.
If
\varepsilon=1
then
s\in A_{2}
, so
U_{\eta}=[2,2,3,\boldsymbol{3},2]
if
s\in U_{\sigma}
and
U_{\eta}=\langle 2;[2],[2,2],[2,\boldsymbol{3}]\rangle
otherwise, so
\operatorname{ld}(H^{\eta})=\tfrac{9}{29}
or
\tfrac{3}{13}
, contrary to (
22
). Thus
\varepsilon=0
. If
s\in A_{2}
then
U_{\eta}=[2,2,3,\boldsymbol{4}]
if
s\in U_{\sigma}
and
U_{\eta}=\langle 2;[2],[2,2],[\boldsymbol{4}]\rangle
if
s\in T_{2}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H^{\eta})=\tfrac{8}{25}
or
\tfrac{2}{7}
, contrary to (
22
). Hence
s\in A_{3}
. By assumption
s\not\in H
, so
s\in T_{3}
or
s\in G_{3}
. We get
U_{\eta}=[(2)_{3},\boldsymbol{4},(2)_{3}]
or
U_{\eta}=[(2)_{3},\boldsymbol{4},2,3]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H^{\eta})=\tfrac{1}{5}
or
\tfrac{9}{53}
, contrary to (
22
).

Subcase 1.2.2:

r\in G_{2}
.
Suppose
s\in A_{2}
. If
\varepsilon=1
then
s\in U_{\sigma}
since
U_{\eta}
is admissible; hence
U_{\eta}=[3,3,\boldsymbol{3},2]
and
\operatorname{ld}(H^{\eta})=\tfrac{5}{17}<\frac{1}{3}
; contrary to (
22
). Thus
\varepsilon=0
, so
U_{\eta}=[3,3,\boldsymbol{4}]
if
s\in U_{\sigma}
and
\langle 2;[2],[3],[\boldsymbol{4}]\rangle
otherwise, and
\operatorname{ld}(H^{\eta})=\tfrac{9}{29}
or
\tfrac{3}{11}
; a contradiction with (
22
). Thus
s\in A_{3}
; in particular,
\varepsilon=0
. We have
s\not\in H
by assumption, and if
s\in T_{3}
then interchanging
F_{2}
with
F_{3}
we get back to Case
1.2.1
. Thus we can assume
s\in G_{3}
, so
U_{\eta}=[3,2,\boldsymbol{4},2,3]
and
\operatorname{ld}(H^{\eta})=\tfrac{1}{7}
; a contradiction with (
22
).

Case 2:

q\in T_{1}
,
H\cap\operatorname{Bs}\varphi_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
.
Suppose
\upsilon_{+}^{-1}
has a base point
r\not\in A_{1}
, so, say,
r\in A_{2}
. By assumption
r\not\in H
, so
r\in T_{2}\cup G_{2}
. If
\varepsilon=0
then
U_{\sigma}
is a non-admissible fork, which is impossible, so
\varepsilon=0
. Now for
k=2
we get

U_{\eta}=[(2)_{3},\boldsymbol{3},(2)_{3}]
if
r\in T_{2}
and
U_{\eta}=[(2)_{3},\boldsymbol{3},2,3]
if
r\in G_{2}
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H^{\eta})=\frac{1}{3}
or
\frac{3}{11}
, contrary to (
22
). Thus for any
k\geqslant 2
, we get a contradiction with (
22
) after a vertical swap. Thus
\operatorname{Bs}\upsilon_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
.

Assume
\varepsilon=0
. Conjugating the automorphism (
4
) by a permutation
(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})\mapsto(F_{3},F_{2},F_{1})
we get an involution
\iota\in\operatorname{Aut}(X_{\varphi},D_{\varphi})
mapping
(T_{1},A_{1})
to
(H,A_{1})
. Under the isomorphism
\operatorname{Aut}(X_{\varphi},D_{\varphi})\cong S_{4}
explained in Remark
3.11
, this involution corresponds to a
180^{\circ}
rotation about the horizontal axis in Figure
6
. Now replacing
\varphi_{+}
with
\iota\circ\varphi_{+}
we get back to Case
1
.

Therefore, we can assume
\varepsilon=1
. If
\psi=\upsilon
then
k\geqslant 3
by Lemma
6.5
, and
D
is as in
(17)
. Assume
\psi\neq\upsilon
. By our assumptions, the unique base point of
\upsilon_{+}^{-1}
is
\{r\}=A_{1}\cap T_{1}
. The admissibility of
U
implies that
\upsilon_{+}
is a single blowup at
r
. As before, Lemma
6.5
gives
k\geqslant 3
, so
D
is as in
(18)
.

Case 3:

q\in G_{1}^{\tau}
,
(H\cup\bigcup_{i}T_{i})\cap\operatorname{Bs}\varphi_{+}^{-1}=\emptyset
.
Suppose
r\in A_{2}
. Then by assumption
\{r\}=A_{2}\cap G_{2}
, so the admissibility of
U
implies that
\varepsilon=0
. Now for
k=2
we have
U_{\sigma}=[3,2,\boldsymbol{3},2,3]
, so
\operatorname{ld}(H^{\sigma})=\tfrac{2}{9}<\tfrac{1}{3}
, contrary to (
22
); and as before the case
k\geqslant 3
can be reduced to
k=2
by a vertical swap. Thus
\operatorname{Bs}\varphi_{+}^{-1}\subseteq A_{1}
.

Assume
\varepsilon=1
. Let
\sigma_{\textnormal{\ref{item:Aut_symmetry}}}\in\operatorname{Aut}(X_{\varphi},D_{\varphi})
be as in Lemma
3.7
(b)
: under the isomorphism
\operatorname{Aut}(X_{\varphi},D_{\varphi})\cong D_{6}
explained in Remark
3.8
,
\sigma_{\textnormal{\ref{item:Aut_symmetry}}}
corresponds to the symmetry through the vertical axis in Figure
5
. Now, replacing
\varphi_{+}
with
\sigma_{\textnormal{\ref{item:Aut_symmetry}}}\circ\varphi_{+}
we get back to Case
1
.

Thus we can assume
\varepsilon=0
. If
\psi=\upsilon
then
D
is as in
(8)
. Assume
\psi\neq\upsilon
. Then the
\operatorname{Bs}\upsilon_{+}^{-1}=A_{1}\cap G_{1}
. The admissibility of
U
implies that
\upsilon_{+}
is a single blowup at
A_{1}\cap G_{1}
, so
D
is as in
(9)

if
k=2
and
(10)

if
k\geqslant 3
.∎

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a singularity type of a log terminal surface.
As in Notation
2.14
, let
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=w}(\mathcal{S})
be the set of isomorphism classes of del Pezzo surfaces of rank
1
, height
3
, width
w
and singularity type
\mathcal{S}
.

Assume
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=3}(\mathcal{S})\neq\emptyset
. Then by Lemma
4.12

\mathcal{S}
is as in Table
4
(top part), and either
\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=3}(\mathcal{S})=1
or
\mathcal{S}
is as in Theorem
A
(b)(b.i)
and
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=3}(\mathcal{S})
consists of the two surfaces from Example
4.9
.

Assume
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})\neq\emptyset
. If
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
then Lemma
5.15
shows that
\mathcal{S}
is as in Table
4
(part
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
), and
\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})=1
. If
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, Lemma
5.16
shows that either
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})
has moduli dimension
1
(with a representing family over
\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
) and
\mathcal{S}
is as in Table
6
, or
\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})=1
and
\mathcal{S}
is as in Table
7
.

Eventually, assume
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})\neq\emptyset
. If
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
then by Lemma
6.6
we have
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
,
\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})=1
and
\mathcal{S}
is as in the last row of Table
4
. If
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
then by Lemma
6.7

\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})=1
and
\mathcal{S}
is as in Table
5
.

We check directly that each
\mathcal{S}
appears in at most one of the above lemmas, so for each
\mathcal{S}
only one of the sets
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=w}(\mathcal{S})
is nonempty. Moreover, the classification in
[
PP24a
]
shows that none of the above singularity types can be realized by a del Pezzo surface of rank one and height at most
2
, see Tables 7–10 loc. cit. Thus the set
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=3}(\mathcal{S})\cup\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})\cup\mathcal{P}_{0pt=3}^{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})
described above equals the set
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 3}(\mathcal{S})
from Theorem
A
, as needed.

∎

In cases
0pt(\overline{X})=3,2
and
1
the numbers
h^{i}
are computed in Lemmas
4.11
(g)
,
5.8
(d)(d.ii)
and
6.4
(d)
, respectively.

∎

7.
Comparison with Lacini’s list

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3
. In this case, all (non-canonical) log terminal del Pezzo surfaces of rank one are arranged in 24 broad series described in
[
Lac24
, §6.1]
. We note that the proof of Theorem
A
is independent from
[
Lac24
]
, which follows a different method, based on
[
KM99
]
. In this section, we check which surfaces in
[
Lac24
, §6.1]
have height at least
3
, and identify those of height
3
with the ones in Theorem
A
. This comparison is summarized in Table
3
below, for the comparison in the other direction see Table
4
.

We recall that the description in
[
Lac24
]
does not address the question of uniqueness of del Pezzo surfaces constructed there. In fact, as we will see below, the 24 series in loc. cit. are not disjoint.
We will also see from the comparison that surfaces from Lemmas
4.12
(16)
and
5.15
(2)
seem to be missing in
[

.

Table 3.
Del Pezzo surfaces from

.

For completeness, we explain how to deduce the above comparison. Let
\overline{X}
be a surface from
[
Lac24
]
, and let
\mathcal{S}
be its singularity type. In each case, we first check if
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
or
\overline{X}
has a descendant with elliptic boundary (this is usually apparent from the constructions in loc. cit.). Assume that neither of those conditions hold. If
\mathcal{S}
is not listed in Table
4
, then by Theorem
A
we get
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 4
. We will discuss such surfaces in our forthcoming article, where we will show that in fact
0pt(\overline{X})=4
. Assume now that
\mathcal{S}
is listed in Table
4
, and let
\overline{X}^{\prime}
be a corresponding surface from Theorem
A
. The latter result shows that
\overline{X}^{\prime}
is uniquely determined by
\mathcal{S}
, unless
\mathcal{S}
is the exceptional type (

⋆

‣
4C
) from Theorem
A
(b)(b.i)
, in which case we get two surfaces
\overline{X}_{1}^{\prime}
,
\overline{X}_{2}^{\prime}
. Our goal is to show that, in case
\mathcal{S}\neq\mbox{\eqref{eq:25}}
, there is an isomorphism
\overline{X}\cong\overline{X}^{\prime}
, and in the exceptional case
\mathcal{S}=\mbox{\eqref{eq:25}}
, there is an isomorphism
\overline{X}\cong\overline{X}_{i}^{\prime}
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
. (In fact, we will see that type (

⋆

‣
4C
) is realized by two surfaces
\overline{X}_{1},\overline{X}_{2}
in
[
Lac24
]
, and we will find isomorphisms
\overline{X}_{i}\cong\overline{X}_{i}^{\prime}
for
i=1,2
).

Let
(X,D)
and
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be the minimal log resolutions of
\overline{X}
and
\overline{X}^{\prime}
, respectively. Lemmas
4.12
,
5.15
and
6.6
cited in Theorem
A
show that on
X^{\prime}
there are
(-1)
-curves
A_{j}^{\prime}
meeting
D^{\prime}
in components corresponding to numbers decorated by
|j|
, see Section
2G
for the notation. Using the description of each series in
[
Lac24
]
, we find
(-1)
-curves
A_{j}
on
X
which meet the exceptional divisor
D
in the same way as the curves
A_{j}^{\prime}
meet
D^{\prime}
, i.e. so that the weighted graphs of
D+\sum_{j}A_{j}
and
D^{\prime}+\sum_{j}A_{j}^{\prime}
are isomorphic. Contracting
\sum_{j}A_{j}^{\prime}
and some new
(-1)
-curves in the subsequent images of
D^{\prime}
, we get a minimalization
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\longrightarrow(Z^{\prime},B^{\prime})
as in Proposition
B
. Contracting the corresponding subdivisor of
D+\sum_{j}A_{j}
we get a minimalization
(X,D)\longrightarrow(Z,B)
such that
\rho(Z)=\rho(Z^{\prime})
and the weighted graphs of
B
and
B^{\prime}
are isomorphic. The list of minimal log surfaces
(Z^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in Proposition
B
shows that there is an isomorphism
(Z,B)\cong(Z^{\prime},B^{\prime})
. By the universal property of blowing up, cf.
[
PP24a
, Lemma 2.18]
, this isomorphism lifts to an isomorphism
(X,D)\cong(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
, which descends to the required isomorphism
\overline{X}\cong\overline{X}^{\prime}
.

Now, we follow this strategy for each series LDP1–LDP24 from
[
Lac24
]
. We use the notation from loc. cit.

,

and

LDP 2–4

(1)

,

LDP 6

This surface is of type

LDP 7

These are the surfaces from Lemma
5.15
(14)
.
Let
r
be the cusp of
C
and let
s
be the inflection point of
C
, i.e.
L\cap C=\{s\}
. The
(-1)
-curve over
r
corresponds to
|0|
. The proper transforms of the lines joining
r
with
p
and
s
correspond to
|2|
and
|3|
, respectively. It remains to find a
(-1)
-curve corresponding to
|1|
, i.e. meeting
D
exactly in the first and the second exceptional curve over
r
.

Let
\sigma\colon\mathbb{P}^{2}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the standard quadratic transformation centered at
p,r,s
, and let
{l}_{p},{l}_{r},{l}_{s}
be lines contracted by
\sigma^{-1}
to
p,r,s
, respectively. Then
{c}\coloneqq\sigma_{*}C
is a conic tangent to
{l}_{r}
and passing through
{l}_{p}\cap{l}_{s}
; and
{l}\coloneqq\sigma_{*}L
is a line passing through
{l}_{r}\cap{l}_{p}
and tangent to
{c}
at
{c}\cap{l}_{s}
. Let
{c}^{\prime}
be the conic passing through
{l}_{r}\cap{c}
and tangent to
{l}
,
{l}_{p}
at their common points with
{l}_{s}
. We have
{c}^{\prime}\neq{c}
since
{c}
is not tangent to
{l}_{p}
. Now, the proper transform of
{c}^{\prime}
corresponds to
|1|
, as needed.

and

LDP 20

This surface has height at most
2
. Indeed, the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
